# Sūrah Qāf (Chapter 50) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Chapter Named After a Single Letter**

Sūrah Qāf takes its name from the first sound that meets the ear when it is recited: *Qāf*, one letter of the Arabic alphabet, standing alone before the oath that follows — "By the Glorious Qur'an." The compilers of the muṣḥaf knew other names for it too: it is reported in the classical sciences under the title *al-Bāsiqāt*, "the Tall Ones," after the towering date-palms of verse 10 with their packed, tiered flower-spikes, an image so striking in the desert landscape that it came to designate the whole chapter. The naming is itself instructive. A sūrah that will spend forty-four verses arguing that the God who made the heavens without fissure can reassemble a dissolved human body begins with a letter — the smallest unit of the very medium in which the argument is carried: speech. The challenge is built into the heading. The Arabs who declared resurrection "a far-fetched return" (v. 3) were, among other things, a people who worshipped eloquence; the sūrah opens by placing one of their own letters on the table like a coin whose mint no one can identify.

The chapter is unanimously counted among the Makkan sūrahs — revelations addressed to the Quraysh in the middle years of the Prophet's ﷺ struggle in Makkah, before the Community had a state, a treasury, or law courts. One report preserves a dissent about verse 38 (see its commentary below), but the overwhelming judgement, transmitted from ʿAbdullāh ibn al-Zubayr, Qatādah, Ibn Zumayk, Abū al-ʿĀliyah and every major compiler of *asbāb al-nuzūl*, is that Qāf is Makkan through and through, and its content confirms the verdict: its subject-matter is not property or punishment statutes but God, revelation, resurrection, death, and the reckoning — the four pillars of Makkan preaching. The one topic the chapter does legislate is prayer (vv. 39-40), and even that appears as medicine for a persecuted preacher told to "bear patiently what they say" (v. 39), not as the constitutional ordinance of a settled community.

**"The Sūrah of the Threat"**

Another title the commentators recorded for Qāf reflects a piece of statistical observation: the verbal root *w-ʿ-d* in its sense of threat and warning — *waʿīd* — occurs only six times in the entire Qur'an, and four of those six fall inside this single sūrah (vv. 14, 20, 28, 45). That density earned Qāf the description "Sūrat al-Waʿīd," the Chapter of the Threat, and it explains something a modern reader may find disorienting: the chapter moves between exquisite descriptions of gardens, water and date-palms, and a hell that is asked "Have you been filled?" and answers "Is there more?" (v. 30). The two movements are not a contradiction but one argument with two poles. A threat, in Qur'anic usage, is not an anger-management outburst; it is information. It tells the hearer that the road is real and that cliffs exist, in exactly the way a sign at a mountain pass does. This is why the chapter can, in the space of a page, speak of a sky "with no rifts" (v. 6) and of "the severe punishment" (v. 26): both are treated as things to be *seen*, and the sūrah's repeated verb is *abṣar*, to see or to look — the "insight" (*baṣīrah*) of v. 8, the "piercing sight" of v. 22, the "he who gives ear" of v. 37. Qāf is a chapter about vision: about what people look at, and what they refuse to look at.

The theme of the warning also explains the sūrah's sharp focus on the word *dhikr* — reminder, remembrance, memorial — in its many forms: *dhakkār* (v. 45), *dhikrā* (v. 8), *dhakara* and *tadhakkur* throughout. Revelation, on this chapter's account, adds nothing to humanity that was not already latent in it: it reminds. It uncovers, removes a cover (*ghaṭāʾ*, v. 22), and makes people look at what they were already facing. The final verse — "So remind, by means of the Qur'an, whoever fears My threat" (v. 45) — closes the circle: the sūrah that began with an oath by the Glorious Qur'an ends by telling the Prophet ﷺ precisely what that Qur'an is *for*, and to whom its address effectively runs.

**Qāf and the Beginning of the Mufaṣṣal**

In the standard ordering of the Qur'an, Qāf marks the opening of the *mufaṣṣal*, the "detailed" or "distinct" group of sūrahs that runs from chapter 50 to chapter 114 — so called because their brevity means the basmalah, separating sūrah from sūrah, recurs with great frequency, making the divisions (*faṣl*) between them conspicuous. In the reports transmitted on the divisions of the Book, the Prophet ﷺ spoke of the *miʾīn*, the *mathānī* and the *mufaṣṣal* as portions of revelation given him in place of what went before; and the Companions organised their Qur'anic learning around these groupings, so that a pupil who had memorised the mufaṣṣal was held to have taken the greater part of the Book.

That Qāf stands at the head of this great sequence, and that it is known in the sciences of the Qur'an as *Fātiḥat al-Mufaṣṣal*, "the Opening of the Distinct Group", fits the chapter's function: the shorter sūrahs that follow press the two great subjects this one introduces — resurrection and tawḥīd — with ever shorter, sharper means. Qāf is thus a threshold, and it behaves like one: it is comparatively short, its rhyme is driven by a single heavy consonant (*-q*, *-ūq*, *-ūb*, *-ād*) which produces an unusual acoustic effect of hammering finality, and its verses are of that middle length that allows a preacher to hold them in memory and repeat them publicly.

**The Prophet's Own Use of the Sūrah**

The Prophet ﷺ gave this chapter a liturgical prominence unmatched by almost any other single sūrah of its length. Muslim transmits from Umm Hishām bint al-Ḥārith, a woman of the Ansar:

> "I learned *Qāf, wal-Qur'ān al-Majīd* from the tongue of the Messenger of God ﷺ, recited on the minbar every Friday." (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, Book of Prayer on Friday)

A further report, transmitted through the Tābiʿī al-Shaʿbī and quoted by the compilers of the sūrah's virtues and occasions, adds two details about the Prophet's ﷺ use of the chapter in the sermon: that he recited *Qāf* until he reached the story of Noah, and that when he did so his sermon became shorter. Its chain is weaker than the report from Umm Hishām above, so it is cited here for its content rather than for its strength.

That last detail — the sermon shortened when Qāf was recited — is precisely what an observer notices in the text itself: this is a sermon-shaped sūrah, meant to be delivered to a sitting congregation in a single breath, with an argument that runs from the rejectors' incredulity (vv. 1-5) through the cosmos (vv. 6-11) to the graveyard (vv. 19-26) and then to the gardens (vv. 31-35), before ending with a consolation and a commission (vv. 36-45). The source's introduction adds, on the authority of al-ʿAskarī and al-Qurṭubī, that he read *Qāf* together with Sūrah 54, *al-Qamar* — the sūrah of the Hour drawing near and the sky splitting open — during the two ʿĪd prayers, and that he often recited *Qāf* in the dawn prayer. The frequency and the variety of these reports are what led the compilers to treat the chapter as a fixture of the Prophet's ﷺ public recitation in Makkah and Madinah alike. The early jurists drew a practical lesson from this: that a khaṭīb may substitute the recitation of a sūrah for part of the sermon, and that it is sunnah to keep the khutbah proportionate, since the people have their own needs and their own limits of attention — a rule of preaching this sūrah's own manner demonstrates.

Some scholars of an earlier generation, struck by this prophetic frequency, held Qāf to be among the greatest of the sūrahs, and a report circulating among the Tābiʿūn — transmitted with a chain the jurists do not rank as sound but which they quote for its edifying content — has the Prophet ﷺ say that on the Day of Resurrection the *kursī* will be set at the right hand of the Merciful and that no sūrah will come to intercede for its people more swiftly than Qāf. Where a report is of this standing, the sober scholarly practice is the one al-Dhahabī and Ibn Ḥajar describe: to benefit from its wisdom without building law or creed upon it.

**A Coherent Argument, Not a Miscellany**

Western readers trained to find the Qur'an episodic have repeatedly been surprised by the architecture of Qāf. Modern structural analysis of the sūrah has shown a symmetry that was not lost on the classical commentators either: the chapter's fifteen opening verses set up the two denials of the Quraysh — that a human being can be a warner (v. 2) and that the dead can rise (v. 3) — and the thirty remaining verses answer exactly those two denials in turn, in mirror order, so that the whole sūrah forms a chiasm. A working map of the movement:

- **vv. 1-5** — The oath by the Glorious Qur'an, and the two astonishments of the rejectors, answered by God's all-encompassing knowledge.
- **vv. 6-11** — Six acts of cosmic observation: sky, earth, mountains, plants, blessed water, date-palms, a dead land revived — each a proof of the same power, each ending with "so shall be the coming forth."
- **vv. 12-15** — Eight communities that said no before Makkah said no; and the rhetorical question that ends the argument: *Did We tire at the first creation?*
- **vv. 16-30** — The individual's journey: nearer than the jugular vein, the two recording angels, the intoxication of death, the trumpet, the driver and witness, the lifting of the cover, the companion's testimony, the sentence, and the dialogue with Hell.
- **vv. 31-35** — The mirror-journey of the penitent servant: the Garden drawn near, the entry greeting of peace, and "with Us there is more."
- **vv. 36-37** — The destroyed mighty, and the one kind of person who can still learn from them: the one who has a heart.
- **vv. 38-44** — Creation in six days without fatigue; the Prophet's own medicine of patience and prayer; the Caller, the Day of Coming Forth, and a gathering "easy for Us."
- **v. 45** — God knows what they say; you are not a taskmaster over them; remind by the Qur'an.

The movement is a single descent from cosmology to the deathbed and back out to the pulpit. What holds it together is one claim, variously proved: the same *qudra* (power) displayed in making the world is displayed in remaking a person, and the same *ʿadl* (justice) that makes the first claim meaningless unless the second is true. That is why the sūrah's six warnings of threat are all attached, in the end, to an invitation rather than a coercion; the last word of the chapter is *dhakkir* — remind (v. 45).

**How to Read What Follows**

The commentary below treats each of the forty-five verses of Sūrah Qāf as an independent writing task, in strict order, exactly as the governing method requires. Where the source commentary groups verses (6-11, 12-14, 17-18, 24-26, 29-30, 41-42), those verses are here separated and each given its own full treatment, so that no verse is truncated to spare the chapter's length. Every section carries the verse's translation as a blockquote, then expanded commentary drawn from Arabic language and morphology, the classical exegetes (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, Ibn ʿAbbās and others), authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections identified, *asbāb al-nuzūl* and ninth-century Arabian context where the sources supply it, theological reasoning, and honest application to the life a modern reader actually lives. All direct quotations from the Qur'an, ḥadīth and the scholars appear in blockquotes; material that the tradition itself flags as weak or as *Isrāʾīliyyāt* is labelled as such rather than presented as established fact. The chapter runs from *Qāf* to "whoever fears My threat." Nothing has been compressed.

## Sūrah Qāf 50:1

> **Qāf. By the Glorious Qur'an.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Letter Standing at the Door of an Oath**

The sūrah opens with one consonant, pronounced by its name — *qāf* — and then swears: *wal-Qur'ān al-Majīd*, "By the Glorious Qur'an." Twenty-nine sūrahs of the Qur'an begin with what the sciences call *al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt*, "the disconnected letters," letters set apart from any word, recited as letters. Three sūrahs open with a single isolated letter, and all three are sūrahs preoccupied with prophethood and its critics: *Ṣād* (38), *Qāf* (50), and *Nūn* (68). In each, the letter is immediately followed by an oath by the revelation: "By the Qur'an full of the Reminder" (38:1), "Qāf. By the Glorious Qur'an" (50:1), "Nūn. By the Pen and what they inscribe" (68:1). The pattern is not accidental. Where the single letter and the oath coincide, the chapter is being introduced precisely as *speech* whose source is contested — Ṣād answers those who said, "We have not heard of this in the former religion" (38:4, 38:7); Nūn answers those called "against whom is the curse of God and of the cursers" (68:9-16); and Qāf answers a tribe astonished that a warner should come from among them (50:2). The letters announce the issue: this is revelation, and you are being asked to decide about it before you decide about anything in it.

Most of the commentators hold that the ultimate meaning of these letters is known only to God, and the majority position — the one reported from Abū Bakr, ʿAlī, Ibn ʿAbbās, and stated as the soundest course by al-Ṭabarī — is to affirm them as real and purposeful while suspending final judgement. Al-Ṭabarī writes that the correct view among those he considers is that "God knows best what He intends by it," with this rider: the letters are also an oath by which God honours the Qur'an, and the honouring is intelligible even where the specification is not. This is a rare and instructive form of scholarly restraint: the exegete reports every defensible reading, does not discard any of them as useless, and refuses to convert a possibility into a claim.

**What the Ear Does With *Qāf***

Before any interpretation, there is sound. The letter *qāf* is articulated from the back of the tongue against the soft palate (*min aqṣā al-lisān ʿalā al-ḥak*), with what the phoneticians of Arabic call *ijtāl* — heaviness — because its mouthful is full and its voice resonant. So the sūrah begins with a stop, a low, compact sound made deep in the throat, and the reciter's breath is arrested before it is released into the flowing vow that follows. Any listener who has sat with a Qur'an teacher knows the effect: the single letter gathers the room.

That the recited form of the letter mattered to the first audience is shown by a report transmitted in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb:

> "I heard Hishām ibn Ḥakīm ibn Uqba reciting Sūrat Qāf during the lifetime of the Messenger of God ﷺ. I listened to his recitation, and behold, he was varying many letters in it, and I almost pounced on him while he was still in prayer; then I waited until he pronounced the salaam and I said: Who taught you this Qur'an I heard you reciting? He said: The Messenger of God ﷺ taught it me. I said: You are lying! The Messenger of God ﷺ taught it me, and I recite it! So I seized him by his garment and brought him to the Messenger of God ﷺ, and he said: Release him, ʿUmar. It was revealed to me that the Qur'an should be recited on seven modes (aḥruf); so recite whichever of them is easy for you." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, Book of the Virtues of the Qur'an)

This is the only sūrah in the whole of Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī that is named inside the chain of a report about the variety of its recitation. For our purposes one fact is decisive: Sūrah Qāf was recited publicly, again and again, in the Prophet's ﷺ lifetime, by men who would have differed about it had it not been given in those modes — the chapter was, in the most literal sense, a text the community knew by ear.

**"Majīd": A Word That Resists Paraphrase**

*Al-Majīd* is the active participle of *majd*, and the lexicographers distinguish it sharply from *madḥ* (praise) and from *karam* (generosity): *majd* is highness of rank and excellence that does not depend on being acknowledged. Al-Jawharī, Ibn Fāris and al-Rāghib all note that *majīd* describes one whose nobility is intrinsic and continuous — the form *faʿīl* here carries the sense of permanent possession, not of occasional action. It is used sparingly. The same epithet is applied to God in a verse the commentators set beside ours — "the Lord of the Throne, the Glorious" (85:15) — and in the angels' greeting to Abraham's household: "God's mercy and blessings be upon you, people of the House; He is Praiseworthy, Glorious" (11:73). The Qur'an calls the revelation itself *majīd* in only two places: here, and "Nay, it is a Glorious Qur'an (*majīd*) in a Preserved Tablet" (85:21-22). The pairing is the sūrah's first piece of theology: the Book is glorious because its Source is glorious, and its glory is of the same type — not conferred, not negotiated, not dependent on readers.

There is a discipline in the word-choice. The Qur'an calls the same revelation *karīm*, "a generous Book, hidden in honour, which none touch save the purified" (56:77-79); and *ʿazīz*, "those who disbelieve in the Reminder when it comes to them — and it is a Mighty Book" (41:41); and it names its own subject-matter "the Tremendous News" (*nabaʾin ʿaẓīm*, 38:67), about which the rejectors "are in disagreement" (38:67). *Majīd* belongs to that family and adds to it the notion of unforced splendour: a *karīm* Book gives; an *ʿazīz* Book cannot be overcome; a *majīd* Book is what it is whether or not you attend. The same root is used, pointedly, of the opposite of prophecy — "and do not obey every obstinate swearer, despiser, slanderer, carrier of tale-bearings, and extortioner" (68:12-13), where one of the epithets is *majīd*, "arrogant," because arrogance is the borrowed imitation of the quality: a swelling of the self that has nothing behind it. Between the two uses of *majīd* sits the sūrah's whole quarrel: one is glory that is, the other only glory that is claimed.

**Where the Oath Lands**

Arabic oaths require a response (*jawāb al-qasam*), and the grammar of this opening has occupied the commentators precisely because the sentence that follows begins with *bal*, "rather" — a particle of transition, not of sworn assertion. So: to what is "By the Glorious Qur'an" the oath? Al-Farrāʾ and al-Zajjāj proposed the answer is supplied later in the sūrah; candidates advanced include "We know best what they say" (v. 45), the closing command "So remind by the Qur'an whoever fears My threat" (v. 45) — which yields a beautifully circular structure, the oath and its answer meeting at the chapter's two ends — and "We did indeed create man and We know what his soul whispers to him" (v. 16). Others, following a reading transmitted from al-Farrāʾ, take the response as elliptical and supplied by sense: *la-innā la-qaādirūn ʿalā al-baʿth*, "We are indeed powerful to return the dead," which the sūrah then argues for openly. A third group, reported by al-Qurṭubī, makes the whole of the sūrah the response — an oath followed by nothing but an unbroken sermon, an arrangement they compare to an orator who swears an audience to attention and then never releases the tension.

One should notice what this scholarly disagreement is *about*. Nothing doctrinal turns on the syntactic gap: whichever answer is supplied, the sūrah's content is unchanged. The disagreement is therefore itself a lesson in the manners of the discipline — the commentators are content to enumerate possibilities rather than to force a text to yield certainty it does not offer. The rhetorical effect, however, is ascertainable: an oath by the Book at the head of a chapter addressed to doubters puts the burden of proof where the Qur'an habitually puts it, not on the miracle but on the reader's seriousness. An oath is spoken when truth is being doubted.

**The Emerald Mountain, and the Rules for Handling Wonder-Stories**

Many of the commentators — among them Ibn Jurayj, Qatādah in one of the two reports from him, and much of the popular literature — record that *Qāf* is the name of a mountain, of a green emerald whose light tints the sky, that encompasses the earth and against which the heavens are raised. This imagery, which flourished in Islamic cosmography and reappears in the *ʿAjāʾib* genre, comes largely from the transmissions of Kaʿb al-Aḥbār and others of the *Isrāʾīliyyāt* — materials derived from Jewish lore which Muslims transmitted for reflection but did not treat as revealed. Ibn Kathīr is characteristically blunt in his *Tafsīr* about reports of this class attached to the verse: no sound, established report from the Prophet ﷺ reaches us in this matter, and the transmitted accounts of the mountain "are from the reports of the People of the Book and of what cannot be verified." Al-Qurṭubī likewise notes that some said *Qāf* is a mountain in the Fire, an interpretation which the majority rejected.

What the Study Quran's note preserves, however, is more interesting than the literal claim: the "cosmic mountain" reading "is not a literal physical mountain, but a cosmic mountain that represents the created order as such." That is how such images function in a revealed text read devotionally: they give the imagination a shape for something that has no shape. A boundary-mountain encircling the world says, in the language of desert people, that the world is bounded, held, and therefore not self-explanatory. The reader may take the point without accepting geology.

**Four Other Readings, and What They Share**

The commentaries list alternatives to the mountain: that *Qāf* is one of the names of God; that it denotes the divine names beginning with that letter — *al-Qadīr*, *al-Qahhār*, *al-Quddūs*, *al-Qābīḍ*, *al-Muqīt*; that it is one of the names of the Qur'an; that it names a class of created things, one of the mountains of light around the Throne, or one of the letters in which God's attributes are encoded. The report from Ibn ʿAbbās that the disconnected letters are names of God is transmitted by al-Ṭabarī and al-Ḥākim, but its chain contains links the hadith critics did not certify, and the cautious ruling is to quote it as a *report*, not a *proof*. What all these readings share is a refusal to treat the letter as mere typography: each one insists that something real is happening when a revealed text utters the alphabet — either God is naming Himself, or naming His speech, or naming a reality at the edge of the visible world.

Against that shared instinct stands the plain linguistic fact, which is the firmest ground: *qāf* is a letter, and letters of this kind occur in the Qur'an as the openings of sūrahs whose meaning is left with God. The sūrah's first word is thus also its first argument: language itself is not self-explanatory, and a Book made of it is greater than the sum of its syllables.

**For the Reader: Learning to Be Addressed**

Set at the head of a sermon-shaped chapter, "Qāf. By the Glorious Qur'an" performs an act anyone who reads seriously will recognise: it demands that the text be approached on its own terms before its claims are weighed. There is an obvious modern analogue. A document arrives bearing an attestation, a seal, a notary's line; the seal does not prove each sentence inside it, but it changes the reader's obligations — you do not skim a sworn statement the way you skim rumour. The sūrah places its own seal first and, in the next verse, immediately reports a people who treated the sworn statement as a novelty. Between those two moments, forty-three verses will argue the case. The reader who wants the argument should stand still at verse 1 long enough to be addressed by it: an oath means someone is being called to attention, and the One calling is not the least qualified witness in the room.

## Sūrah Qāf 50:2

> **Nay, but they marvel that a warner has come unto them from among their own; so the disbelievers say, "This is an astounding thing!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Bal": The Particle That Changes the Subject**

The word *bal* at the head of the verse is not a conjunction carrying the sentence forward; it is a correction that turns the hearer around. Arabic grammarians call it *iḍrāb*: it interrupts what was being said and replaces it with something more important. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both observe that after the oath by the Glorious Book, one expects the response of the oath — a claim about the Qur'an's truth. Instead the verse reports a reaction. The expectation is not abandoned; it is *inverted*: instead of "this Book is true," we hear "they were astonished that a warner came to them." The Book's truth and the people's astonishment are set side by side like a scale, and the reader is left to notice which of the two is unstable. The effect of *bal*, then, is diagnostic. The problem in Makkah was not a shortage of evidence. It was a reflex: amazement misdirected.

**The Anatomy of Amazement**

*ʿAjabū* — "they marvelled" — comes from *ʿajab*, the startled contraction of the soul before something it cannot place. Its vocabulary is dense in this sūrah and in its near-twin, Sūrah Ṣād: "They marvelled that a warner had come to them from among themselves, and the disbelievers said: This is a marvellous thing!" (38:4). The Qur'an uses the same root at once to expose and to mock: "Do you marvel at God's affair?" (11:73, said to Sarah's guests), and "If you wonder, then wondrous is the saying of the disbelievers: What! When we are scattered dust, shall we be in a new creation?" (13:5, 11:5). Two marvels are being compared, and the sūrah quietly rules that only one of them is warranted. It is not astonishing that an unseen God should send speech to His creatures — that is mercy, and mercy is the ordinary manner of the Creator in this Book. What would genuinely deserve the name *ʿajab* is that He should have left them unaddressed: "We did not create the heavens and the earth and what is between them in play" (21:16), "Did you suppose that We created you aimlessly?" (23:115). The rejectors, in other words, have marvelled at the wrong item in the inventory.

**"From Among Themselves": The Scandal of Proximity**

The offence is specified by two words: *mundhir* and *minhum*, "a warner" and "from them." Their own. A warner is one who gives public notice of danger — *nadhar*, to see a coming harm and to warn others of it; the *nadheer* is a man who has seen the fire and come back shouting. The Quraysh did not object that the message was incoherent; they objected to the sender's file. And the objection is recorded elsewhere in their own words, which this verse cites without quotation marks, in the Qur'an's characteristic manner of letting the same sentence recur:

> "Why was this Qur'an not sent down upon a great man from one of the two cities?" (43:31)
> "What is this Messenger who eats food and walks in the marketplaces? Why was not an angel sent down upon him, to be a warner with him?" (25:7)
> "Do people marvel that We revealed to a man from among them, saying: Warn mankind, and give glad tidings to those who believe that for them is a firm standing with their Lord?" (10:2)

and, of the demand that the messenger be an angel or a sign, "And no messenger comes to them but they say of him: A sorcerer, a madman" (51:52; cf. 54:24, "a single madman, rebuffed").

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the same demand, variously worded, is reported in 6:8-9, 7:63, 11:12, 14:10, 17:93-94, 23:24, 33:27, 36:15, 41:14, 54:24, and 64:6 — which means that the complaint of verse 2 is not a local quarrel of one clan but the standing human objection to prophecy as such, pressed by nation after nation against every messenger. The Qur'an's inclusion of the objection therefore also functions as evidence: a Book composed by a man in his forties in Makkah would have gained nothing by recording, in nine different words, the same sneer spoken in nine different centuries.

**Why a Human Warner Had to Be Human**

The verse's emphasis on *min anfusihim*, "from among their own selves," is not defensive; it states a condition of the possibility of guidance. A messenger from another species could be admired but not imitated, addressed but not followed. The Qur'an's answer to the demand for angels is exactly this: "If he had been an angel, We would have made him a man, and We would have confused them in what they confuse" (6:9). The logic deserves to be spelled out. Warning requires that the warner stand inside the situation being warned about — that he know fatigue, temptation, hunger, fear of loss, the pressure of what people think — so that the prohibition he delivers is demonstrably livable. This is why the sūrah will end with a command to patience and prayer addressed to the preacher himself (v. 39), and why the Prophet's ﷺ own biography is treated in the Qur'an as part of the proof, not as decoration: "Say: I am only a human being like you, to whom it is revealed that your God is one God" (18:110).

Ibn Kathīr, commenting on 25:7-8, reports the answer given to those who wanted a treasure or a palace: God does not send revelation to furnish a life of ease for one man, but to lift a people; had the messenger's biography been one of unbroken advantage it would have refuted his claim, not confirmed it. A physician who has never been ill is suspected; one who has survived the disease and teaches its treatment is credible.

**The Offence of Being Warned**

The disbelievers' sentence — *hādhā shay'un ʿajīb*, "this is an astounding thing" — is not an argument, and the sūrah knows it; the verse does not say "they objected" but "they marvelled." Al-Jubbāʾī, cited by al-Zamakhsharī and followed by the source's own note (JJ), offers a subtler reading: their amazement is at the very suggestion that they are in a condition requiring a message — that they, honourable elders of a sanctuary-tribe, should need *admonition*. This is the most psychologically penetrating comment on the verse in the classical corpus. The insult is not located in the content of the warning but in the implication of the posture: to be warned is to be found at risk. Reactance — the reflex to defend one's freedom by rejecting the source of the correction — is well documented in modern psychology: a person told he is driving badly will often drive more defiantly rather than more carefully, because the correction has threatened his standing and he answers the threat, not the fact.

The verse therefore isolates an ailment of the hearer that no amount of new evidence could cure, since the resistance is to being addressed, not to what is said. This is precisely why God does not, at this point, pile on proof. He turns to the sky (v. 6). The proof of the cosmos is not resisted for the same reason: nobody's social standing is at stake in the stars.

**Amazement as a Category Error**

There is a second, philosophical strand of objection folded into the first: how could an eternal God speak to a particular man, at a particular time, in a particular dialect? The Quraysh treated prophecy as a category error — the Infinite should not contract to a single human biography. But the sūrah has just described the Qur'an as *majīd*, glorious in the manner of its Owner: a glory that is intrinsic does not diminish by being given, and a mercy that is truly mercy must reach the person where he actually is, in his own tongue. "We sent no messenger but with the language of his people, to make clear to them" (14:4). The demand that the warner be a "great man from one of the two cities" (43:31) also reveals that the amazement is not theological at bottom but sociological: they are not asking how speech descends from heaven; they are asking who is allowed to speak in their town.

**The Preacher's Patience, Prescribed in Advance**

Finally, notice what the sūrah does *not* do here. It does not grant the Quraysh a sign, and it does not command the Prophet ﷺ to withdraw. Verse 39 will gather up the whole opening scene in a single instruction: "So bear patiently what they say, and glorify the praise of your Lord before the rising of the sun and before the setting." The astonishment recorded in verse 2 is thus framed twice — at the beginning as diagnosis, at the end as the occasion for worship — which is the sūrah's judgement on every hostile reaction to sincere speech: it is not to be answered with outrage, and not to be obeyed. The believer responds to amazement with prayer, and continues the warning. The verse also establishes, for all who inherit the preaching task, that a message rejected as "astounding" has not been refuted. It has only been met by a hearer whose first instinct was to protect his position rather than examine his danger — and for such a hearer the sūrah will reserve, until the very last verse, a door rather than a sentence: "So remind, by the Qur'an, whoever fears My threat" (v. 45).

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:3

> **What! When we are dead and have become dust? That is a far-fetched return!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question With No Verb of Its Own**

Arabic allows a conditional clause to stand without its consequence when the consequence is too obvious or too contemptible to state, and that is exactly the device here. *ʾIdhā mitnā wa-kunnā turāban* — "when we die and become dust" — has no response: the hearers' sentence trails off, because the sentence they cannot finish is the one they refuse to complete ("…shall we really be sent back?"). Al-Zamakhsharī notes the same unfinished structure in "What! When we are scattered dust — are we then in a new creation?" (13:5), and the classical grammarians treat these ellipses as evidence of the speakers' state: a mind that breaks off mid-thought. The Qur'an is often least rhetorical where it seems most rhetorical; the gap in this sentence is the gap in their reasoning.

Their second clause supplies what the syntax lacks: *dhālika rujūʿun baʿīd*, "that is a return far off." *Baʿīd* is a spatial adjective — a thing is *baʿīd* when it is far away from where you stand. The rejectors are describing a possibility the way they would describe a distant valley, and the whole sūrah will spend its effort showing how a far place can be near for the One who made distance.

**Dust: The Word They Use as an Insult**

*Turāb* is not neutral in the Qur'an. It is the anthropological claim of the Book itself: "God — He made you from dust, then from a drop, then He made you pairs" (35:11); "Truly the likeness of Jesus in God's sight is as the likeness of Adam: He created him from dust, then said to him *Be*, and he was" (3:59); and in the garden parable of Sūrah al-Kahf the disbeliever reasons precisely as the Quraysh do here — "I do not think the Hour will ever come, and if I am returned to my Lord I will find better than this as a return" (18:36) — and is told that the man who "disbelieved in his Lord" reasoned "from my dust?" (18:37). The Qur'an's reply is not to deny the dust. It is to deny the inference. What is dismissed as residue is, in the Book's own vocabulary, the raw material from which God has already made something: dust, water, a living thing, and then a moral agent. A thing's origin is not its ceiling — the sentence that verse 3 needs and does not contain.

**The Same Objection, in Nine Other Mouths**

The Qur'an preserves this argument so often that its rejection becomes a kind of evidence about the argument itself. "They say: What! When we are dead and become dust and bones, are we indeed to be resurrected? And our former fathers? Say: You and the former evildoers will be gathered together" (37:16-21); "Are we indeed to be paid back for our deeds of old? … Are we indeed to be sent back to life, or our former fathers?" (56:47-48); "They say: What! When we are bones, decayed, shall we indeed be resurrected as a new creation? Say: Be stones or iron, or a creation greater in your estimate — and they will say: Who will return us? Say: He who made you the first time" (17:49-51; cf. 17:98, 79:11, 36:78-79, 23:82-83, 45:24, 34:7). The answer given to Pharaoh's magicians, to Hud's people, to the Meccans and to every generation is a single sentence, memorably short: "He who made you the first time" (17:51) — *al-ladhī faṭarakum awwala marrah*. This is not an assertion of power in the abstract but a historical claim the addressees already grant: they concede that they were once not. The argument moves from the accepted (origination) to the disputed (return), and it is labelled elsewhere as the reasoning of the sūrah of Ya-Sin: "He gives you a parable and forgets his own creation; he says: Who can give life to bones that are rotten? Say: He who gave them life the first time will give them life, and He has knowledge of every creation" (36:78-79). The rejector's own body is the exhibit.

**Why the Objection Overreaches Its Evidence**

Logically, verse 3's claim is not an observation but an inference from an absence of observation: nobody has seen a dead man return to life; therefore return to life will not occur; therefore the Qur'an's promise is false. The sūrah treats the first premise as true and the second as a non sequitur, and it does so in a way worth spelling out for a modern reader, because this is the very move contemporary materialist arguments repeat.

- The first premise reports what *has been observed within a field of view*. Human beings do not see resurrections; they also do not see the interior of the earth, the beginning of a thought, or the far side of the moon as it was before the photographs. Absence of sight within a limited field is not evidence of absence of the thing.
- The second premise converts "no one has seen X" into "X cannot occur," which requires knowing the full range of what is possible — a claim of total knowledge. The sūrah's answer is that the only candidate for such knowledge is precisely the One they are denying: "Well do We know what the earth takes from them" (v. 4).
- The third premise is the quiet one, and the most important: they suppose that what they see is the *mechanism*, so that if the mechanism is not currently running (bodies dissolve), the result cannot recur. The Qur'an grants the mechanism and disputes the ownership. Dissolution is not a law that binds God; it is a habit He maintains, and verses 6-11 exist to show that He changes habits at will — "He revives the earth after its death" (v. 11, 30:19, 41:39, 57:17).

This is also why verse 15, the verse that closes the section, is framed as a question: "Did We then tire at the first creation?" The sūrah's argument against resurrection-denial is never "the dead will rise because you cannot disprove it." It is: the power you have already admitted — the power that made you, that made the sky, that makes grain out of dust and water — is the power that returns you. The dispute is about the Agent's willingness, not about the Agent's capacity, and no argument about capacity is required from those who concede v. 6.

**Denial as Self-Protection**

Yet the sūrah does not describe them as making a logical error. It describes them as *fleeing*: "That is what you were avoiding" (v. 19, *dhālika mā minhu kunum tuḥiddūn*, "this is that from which you used to shrink"). The word *baʿīd* in our verse and the root *b-ʿ-d* of *baʿīd* are the same letters that appear in *buʿd*, remoteness as a moral state — "far" describes both a place and a disposition. The verse's real content is therefore not physics but posture: a return is "far" to the person who does not want to arrive. This is confirmed by the immediate sequel — they did not weigh the claim of resurrection; they "denied the truth when it came to them, so they are in a confused state" (v. 5). Rejection came first; the astonishment and the argument were supplied afterwards. Al-Rāzī puts it plainly in his discussion of these verses: the difficulty was not in the proof but in the soul's reluctance to submit, and a soul reluctant to submit will find every proof insufficient and every opponent's proof convincing.

The psychological observation is a durable one. Fear of death tends to produce not only consolation-seeking but *denial of consequence*; the more a person believes that nothing follows, the less he must fear. If resurrection is "far off," then so is accountability, and so is the need to change anything. The sūrah will make the same point again at the level of individual death: "the stupor of death will come in reality" (v. 19), which is exactly what was being kept at a distance, arriving now at arm's length.

**The Modern Shape of the Sentence**

"What! When we are dead and have become dust?" has not aged. It is the sentence of every worldview that reduces the human being to what can be counted in the corpse, and it recurs in modern idiom as a confident philosophical conclusion: consciousness is what the brain does; when the brain stops, nothing is left. The interesting feature is that the argument is presented as empirical, whereas the empirical facts are all conceded by both sides. The materialist and the believer agree about decomposition. What divides them is a metaphysical prior — whether the person is only the organised matter, and whether an organiser exists. Verse 3's function in the sūrah is to show what happens when that prior is smuggled into an observation, and verses 6-11 are then the counter-demonstration: a careful look at a world that does not fit the prior. The desert listener is asked to look at water, seed, spike and palm; the modern reader is offered the same exhibit at a different scale — the same atoms that were a dinosaur, a fern and a star now arranged as a hand, a memory and a question about God. Nothing in the dust resents being used again.

**What Changes If the Return Is Real**

Finally, the sūrah never treats this verse as an academic dispute, and the reason is visible in its structure: the resurrection claim is what makes the rest of the chapter not literature but testimony. If "that is a far-fetched return" is true, then the recording angels of verse 18 are a fable, the companion's speech in verses 23 and 27 is a fiction, "Cast into Hell every stubborn disbeliever" (v. 24) is noise, and the promise "for every oft-returning guardian" (v. 32) is a comfort with no backing. If it is false — if the return is near for the One who says *kun*, then be — then every line from verse 16 onward is a description of an ongoing arrangement in which each person is already accompanied, recorded, and known. The sūrah stakes its whole moral grammar on this single point, and it asks the reader to decide the point before deciding anything else, because almost nothing in the chapter can be weighed neutrally while this remains open. The next verse begins the answer, and begins it, as the Qur'an habitually does, with knowledge rather than with power: "We know best what the earth diminishes from them, and with Us is a preserving Book" (v. 4).

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:4

> **Well do We know what the earth diminishes from them, and with Us there is a preserving Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Qad ʿAlimnā": Certainty Stated in the Grammar Itself**

Arabic marks completed, settled knowledge with *qad* plus the perfect verb: not "we know" in the manner of someone acquiring information, but *qad ʿalimnā* — "We have already, definitively, known." The rebuttal to "that is a far-fetched return" (v. 3) does not begin with power but with knowledge, and the order matters. The objection assumes that the difficulty of resurrection lies in finding and reassembling scattered parts. Verse 4 removes that assumption at its root: nothing is scattered from the divine knowledge, and therefore the question "but how will the parts be found?" is malformed from the start. Al-Rāzī observes that in this sūrah God answers the resurrection-deniers mainly with two attributes — knowledge and power — and that He sets knowledge first here because power without knowledge would be blind force; the God who can rebuild is the God who knows exactly what is to be rebuilt.

**"What the Earth Diminishes From Them"**

The verb *tanquṣu* is from *nuqṣ*, diminution: the earth reduces them, takes a lessening from them, consumes. The classical exegetes gloss it with the plain facts of decomposition — what the earth takes of their flesh, bones and remains, whether by decay, by being eaten, by dispersal into soil, plants and the bodies of other creatures, so that nothing remains fixed, intact, or locatable. Al-Ṭabarī reports from Ibn ʿAbbās the sense: "what the earth eats of them." A variant reading attested among the Seven and the extra-canonical readers — Yaʿqūb al-Ḥaḍramī read *mā taqṭufu*, "what the earth gleans" — sharpens the image: the earth picks up their parts the way a bird picks up grain, item by item, with no residue and no register. That the verse should be read either as slow consumption or as rapid gleaning shows the point it is making is the same: the earth is not a warehouse; it is a solvent.

The verse's force is best seen against the disbelief it answers. The Quraysh did not deny that bodies disappear; they inferred from disappearance that the disappeared are lost. The Qur'an's reply is that loss is a function of a limited recorder. For a creature who must search, what is dispersed is lost; for One whose knowledge does not proceed by search, dispersion is only rearrangement.

**A Preserving Book: The Record That Precedes the Ruin**

*Wa-ʿindanā kitābun ḥafīẓ* — "and with Us is a preserving Book." The participle *ḥafīẓ* in Arabic can be read actively ("that which preserves, guards, keeps count") or passively ("preserved, kept safe from corruption"); the Qur'an uses the same root for both, "a Book preserving (all things)" here, and "a guarded Tablet" (85:22) where the guarding is upon it. The commentators list three referents: the Preserved Tablet in which all that will exist is written before it exists; the book of each person's deeds, which the angels write (vv. 17-18, 50:23); and, in a reading reported from some of the Salaf, a book of the fates and remains of bodies, so that nothing that belongs to a person is omitted from what will be returned.

The two first are enough for the argument, and they are corroborated elsewhere in the Qur'an in phrasing so close that it reads as a commentary on this verse: "And no one is granted length of life, nor is aught curtailed of its span, but it is in a Book; truly that is easy for God" (35:11); "Do you not know that God knows what is in heaven and earth? Truly it is in a Book; truly that is easy for God" (22:70); "And there falls no leaf but that He knows it, nor a grain in the darknesses of the earth, nor a thing moist or withered, but that it is in a Clear Book" (6:59); and, of the reckoning itself: "What kind of book is this, that leaves neither small thing nor great but has enumerated it? And they will find what they did present before them" (18:49).

**The Real Difficulty: Continuity of the Same Person**

Something subtler is at work in the verse, and it is what makes it philosophically interesting rather than merely reassuring. The objection in verse 3 is not only "the parts are scattered" but "which parts were me?" The matter that composes a human body is in continuous exchange with the world; a body rebuilt from the correct total of atoms would still need an account of why this set, in this order, is the same person who lived. The Qur'an does not offer a material criterion of identity. It offers a personal one: a record. God's knowledge — "We know" — and God's book preserve not the atoms but the *person*, whose deeds, intentions, and span are written. The reassembly is therefore not an archaeological reconstruction but a re-creation on the basis of a true description, and the description is already complete.

The analogy is not far from ordinary practice. A demolished building is no obstacle to an architect who retains the drawings and the specifications; a burned manuscript can be rewritten by a copyist who keeps a duplicate; a server may be destroyed without loss by a firm that keeps its archive elsewhere. The sūrah's claim is that the archive is not elsewhere in space but elsewhere in mode: "with Us" — *ʿindanā* — a phrase of possession and presence, which the classical writers gloss as knowledge, and which Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have explained as "a book which nothing is dropped and nothing forgotten."

**The Tailbone: A Report the Tradition Transmits Cautiously**

Linked by the hadith collections to the very problem of this verse is the report from Abū Hurayra:

> "Every part of the son of Adam is consumed by the earth except the tailbone: from it he was created, and from it he will be reconstructed on the Day of Resurrection." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, Book of the Beginning of Creation)

The exegetes cite it as a specification of what survives dissolution, and some jurists pondered its physiological oddity with characteristic candour: al-Qurṭubī records discussions of whether the exception refers to a bone in the modern anatomical sense, and Ibn Ḥajar remarks that the meaning may be "the most enduring part," while other critics noted that observation does not disclose any bone that never decays. The sound discipline with such a text is to affirm what it asserts — that God keeps a basis for reconstruction — without pressing it into a claim about anatomy that could embarrass the doctrine if falsified. The Qur'anic verse itself requires no such basis: the guarantee is knowledge and a record, not a surviving fragment.

**What the Verse Asks of the Person Who Reads It**

Verse 4 is short and easily passed over, but it installs a premise that the rest of the sūrah uses repeatedly: nothing in you is lost, and nothing about you is unrecorded. This has a double effect, and the sūrah lets both stand without softening either. As reassurance it is unmatched — the mourner, the mutilated, the drowned, the cremated, the person whose body became dust centuries ago, are all equally present to a knowledge that "diminishes" nothing. As a moral fact it is unsettling, and verses 17-18 draw the conclusion at once: the same preservation that guarantees resurrection guarantees an audit. The believer's self-understanding is thereby located not in what can be recovered from the ground but in what is held with God — a position that explains a great deal of Qur'anic vocabulary, where the language of being "written," "recorded," and "returned" (32:11: "Say: the angel of death, who is given charge of you, will take you, then to your Lord you will be returned") does duty for what other traditions express as the survival of a soul-substance. On this account what survives death is not a residue but a person, known by name, kept in a Book, and summoned.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:5

> **Nay, they denied the truth when it came unto them; so they are now in a confounded situation.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Diagnosis in Place of Rebuttal**

After three verses of the rejectors' objections and one divine answer, the sūrah stops arguing with them and describes them. *Kadhdhabū bi-l-ḥaqqi lammā jāʾahum* — "they denied the truth when it came to them" — locates the failure at the moment of arrival: the truth is designated *al-ḥaqq*, a term the Qur'an reserves for what corresponds to reality and, as a divine name, for God Himself ("because God is the Truth, and what they invoke besides Him is falsehood," 22:6). The verb *kadhdhaba*, to call false, is used here without specifying an objection; the rejection is not analysed because, the sūrah implies, there is no analysis to give. What came, was denied. Al-Qurṭubī notes that this very construction — a bare statement of refusal — is itself part of the rebuke, as if to say: their position is so poorly grounded that it is best represented by its motive.

The consequence is the important half of the verse: *fa-hum fī amrin marīj* — "so they are in a confounded state."

**Marīj: The Word for a Mixture No One Can Sort**

*Marīj* is a passive-form participle from *marija*, to be stirred, churned, mixed until the components are indistinguishable; milk and water that have been agitated together are *marj*, and Arabic speakers used *amruhum marj* for an affair that has come to no order. The Qur'an uses the same root for a flame "of mingled fire" (55:15, *mārij min nār*) — a blaze in which fire and its smoke are so twined that neither can be isolated. Applied to a state of mind, the word is devastatingly precise: not error but *unsortedness*, a condition in which truth and falsehood have been churned together so that the sufferer cannot tell which is which.

The classical commentators spelled out what the phrase described, and their glosses converge. Al-Ṭabarī: "they are in confusion, in error, in dispute"; they cannot distinguish the true from the false. Others: they are in a "shaken and unstable" condition, believing one thing today and another tomorrow, as the source's note records — "they believe one thing and then another." Still others take it as deliberate moral disorder: *dāllīn āthimīn*, astray and sinful. All three are aspects of a single fact: the rejection of a given criterion does not produce sceptical calm; it produces churn.

**The Evidence of Their Own Labels**

That the Quraysh were in exactly this condition is shown by the sūrah's own silence about their reasons and by the reasons they gave elsewhere in the Qur'an, which are mutually incompatible. In one breath he is a soothsayer or a madman: "So remind, for you are not, by the favour of your Lord, a soothsayer or a madman" (52:29; cf. 15:6, "You are indeed one under a spell"). In another a poet: "Do we turn away from our gods for the sake of a mad poet?" (37:36), and "Forged dreams! Rather, he invented it; rather, he is a poet" (21:5; 52:30). In another a sorcerer teaching a trick he learned: "He frowned and scowled, and said: This is nothing but sorcery which is being passed on; this is nothing but the speech of a human being" (74:25; the same man is called *ʿanīd*, stubborn, two verses before — 74:16; cf. 14:10: "are we but a human being like you?")). In another a man encouraged by others: "They said: Legends of the former peoples, which he has caused to be written, so that they are dictated to him morning and evening" (25:5; cf. "Rather, he has invented it," 46:8). And in another the complaint that a worthier man was owed the commission: "Why was not this Qur'an sent down upon a man of consequence in one of the two towns?" (43:31). What the list demonstrates is not that the Quraysh were subtle but that they were unsorted: every label was tried, and no label was tested. That is exactly the state of *amrin marīj* — an affair in which the speaker says one thing today and its contradiction tomorrow, because nothing governs the choice.

The Qur'an also names them for this very incoherence: "They say: When we are dispersed in the earth, are we indeed then in a new creation?" (32:10; cf. 17:49, 98), and it puts a label on the phenomenon of the churning itself: "Those who divided their religion and became sects, each party rejoicing in what it has" (30:32; 6:159); "Like those to whom We gave the Book, and they divide the Book into factions" (15:90-91; 3:105). The point is not that the Meccans were foolish individuals; it is the structural claim of the verse: once the given criterion is refused, opinion has nothing to sort itself by, and every label that flatters an emotion becomes available — the poet for those who want art, the sorcerer for those who want fear, the madman for those who want dismissal. Confusion is the punishment that follows denial, and it arrives before the Day on which any other will be executed.

**The Order of the Verse: Sin First, Disorder After**

Notice the sequence *kadhdhabū … fa-hum fī*: the denial is first, the churn its effect. The Qur'an states the causal direction again and again in theological terms that a modern reader may recognise as psychology: "As for those who strive in Us, We will surely guide them to Our ways" (29:69) and, in the converse, "So when they deviated, God caused their hearts to deviate" (61:7); "And We shall turn their hearts and their eyes away, as they refused to believe in it the first time, and We shall leave them in their trespassing, wandering blindly" (6:110). The repeated idea is that intellect is not a neutral instrument sitting behind the will: what a person wants determines what he can see, and long refusal contracts the range of the visible. Al-Ghazālī built a whole diagnosis of the sciences upon this verse-cluster, arguing in the *Iḥyāʾ* that "the eye of the heart" is clouded by desire, and that purity is an organ of knowledge. The sūrah's own phrase for the same faculty is *qalb*, which will appear three verses of refusal later at the end of this movement: "a reminder for whoever has a heart" (v. 37).

**A Sectarian Hadith, Honestly Cited**

Against this background the commentators frequently cite the well-known report on division:

> "The Jews split into seventy-one or seventy-two sects; the Christians split into seventy-one or seventy-two sects; and this community will split into seventy-three — all of them in the Fire except one." They asked: Which is it, Messenger of God? He said: "What I am upon today, and my Companions." (Sunan al-Tirmidhī and Sunan Abī Dāwūd; graded *ḥasan* by some, criticised for interruption by others)

The hadith's chain is debated and its "all in the Fire except one" clause was carefully handled by scholars who refused to pronounce whole categories of Muslims into perdition; al-Ṭībī and others explained the threat as applying to innovators who make their innovation their religion. Whatever weight the reader assigns, the report is at minimum a confession that the *marīj* of verse 5 is not confined to the pagan Quraysh: the tradition's self-image includes its own churned-up condition, and it locates the remedy not in cleverness but in return to what the earliest community stood on.

**What Confusion Looks Like Now**

The verse speaks to a recognisably modern epistemic state. A society with no accepted criterion does not become free-thinking; it becomes fluctuating. Contemporary information environments have made *marīj* empirically obvious: the same person holds incompatible beliefs on unrelated topics with no discomfort, opinion swings with the temperature of the feed, and "the truth" is discussed as a matter of allegiance rather than correspondence. What Qur'anic diagnosis adds to that description is an account of *why*, and an account of *for whom*. The denial in question is not intellectual honesty exercised against a claim; it is the refusal of a truth that has already "come to them" — that is, of a truth in the mode of address, claiming obedience. And its consequence, on the Qur'an's telling, is not noble agnosticism but incoherence that leaks into every department of life.

The verse also, read with verse 4, forbids a comfortable conclusion. If the rejectors' state were merely "scepticism pending evidence," they would be the more cautious party. The sūrah insists their condition is *active*: they denied, and the confusion is a self-inflicted wound with an owner. This is why, in the next movement, God will not offer them more evidence in the abstract but will direct them to the sky and the earth (vv. 6-11): not to supply what is missing but to test whether they will look at all — since the fault, as verse 5 has just established, is in the wanting.

**The Pastoral Use of This Verse**

For the preacher, the verse is a liberation. If rejection were an evidential problem, the response would be more evidence and, failing that, frustration. Because it is a disorder of the will that produces confusion, the appropriate response is what the sūrah prescribes later: patience, praise, and a reminder offered without compulsion (vv. 39, 45). The confounded are not to be crushed by argument; they are to be shown the sky.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:6

> **Have they not looked upon the sky above them, how We built it and adorned it, and it has no rifts?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Argument Delivered as a Question**

*ʾA-falam yaraw* — "do they not then see/look?" The interrogative here is not a request for information but a rebuke for neglect: the thing is there, in front of them, and looking is free. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the Qur'an habitually answers unbelief not by introducing new objects but by re-directing attention to familiar ones; here the particle *fa* carries the reasoning: *then*, after all of the above, they still do not look. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said of this passage that God calls them to witness what they cannot deny, so that they have no plea left; and al-Rāzī observes that the sūrah's order is deliberate — first the Book, then the cosmos, then the soul — because each of the three answers a different denial: the Book answers the mockery of the warner (v. 2), the cosmos answers the denial of power (v. 3, 15), and the soul's nearness and its record answer the denial of reckoning (vv. 16-18).

The direction is also specified: *ilā al-samāʾi fawqa-hum*, "at the sky above them." Upward. Not down at the ground of their habits, not inward at their own cleverness, but up — the posture that physically produces humility, and the direction in which, in every language, "high" already means "worth attending to." The same invitation recurs with variations across the Qur'an: "Do they not look at the camels, how they are created; and at the sky, how it is lifted; and at the mountains, how they are set firm; and at the earth, how it is spread out?" (88:17-20); "And in your own selves — will you not then see?" (51:21); "Say: Travel through the earth and observe how He began creation" (29:20).

**"Bunāhā": A Sky That Is a Construction, Not a Heap**

*Banaynāhu*, "We built it," from *ibnāʾ*, implies a structure whose parts are joined with a purpose; a *bunyān* is a building, a coherent assembly. The word is used for the heavens where their cohesion is the point: "He who created seven heavens in layers; you see in the creation of the Merciful no lack of proportion" (67:3), and "The heaven — He raised its height and proportioned it" (79:28). Elsewhere the same reality is described as a protection: "We made the heaven a guarded ceiling, yet they turn away from its signs" (21:32), and as a canopy over a bed: "He who made for you the earth a carpet and the heaven a structure, and sent down water from the sky and brought forth thereby fruits as provision for you — so do not set up rivals to God, while you know" (2:22; cf. 13:3, 31:10, and "We adorned the nearest heaven with lamps, and made them a pelting against the devils" — 41:12; 67:5).

The word-choice answers the mood of verses 2-5. A *bunyān* has a builder; a sky is not an accident of gas and distance. The Qur'an's "argument from design" is not stated as a syllogism, and commentators from al-Ashʿarī onward noted that the Qur'an generally does not press logical technicalities but produces *consideration* (tafakkur) that culminates in an act of recognition — which is why it can say of the same sky, "and He has subjected for you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth: all is from Him" (45:13).

**"We Adorned It": Beauty as Evidence, Not Decoration**

The second act upon the sky is aesthetic: *zayyannāhā bi-l-kawākib*, "We adorned it with the stars." The same statement appears with explicit intentionality: "We have adorned the nearest heaven with ornaments, the stars, and as a means of driving off the devils" (41:12; 67:5, where the stars are also "pellets" for the devils — a function tied to revelation's protection, on the dominant reading). And in another place the adornment is addressed to a specific audience: "We placed in the heaven great stars, and We adorned it for those who look" (15:16) — *li-l-nāẓirīn*, for the beholders. That last verse, quoted by Ibn Kathīr here, is the key to this one: the beauty of the sky is not garnish added after the engineering; it is part of the design's purpose. A universe merely functional would be a machine; a universe that is also lovely is a *message*, because ornament that no one needed and everyone enjoys testifies to generosity rather than necessity.

There is a logical elegance in the pairing of "adorned" and "no rifts" within a single verse: beauty and reliability are presented together as evidence of the same attribute. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the pairing explicit in the most direct terms possible: "He who created seven heavens in layers. There is in the creation of the Merciful no discordance (*tafāwut*). Turn your sight again: do you see any crack (*fuṭūr*)? Then turn your sight twice more; your sight will return to you humiliated and exhausted" (67:3-4). Aesthetic coherence and structural integrity, says that passage, are one datum, and it is the datum that answers verse 5's *marīj*: the sky is the opposite of a confounded affair.

**"Mā Lahā min Furūj": The Absence of Cracks**

*Furūj* is the plural of *fatq*, a cleft, a rift, a split that opens where unity was. The claim is that the sky has none: no fissure through which its structure fails, no gap where its order gives out. Al-Ṭabarī glosses: "it has no splitting, no rending, no defect, no disproportion." The source's own note captures the moral register of the phrase: "that there is no disproportionality and that each thing is in its proper place, since He created the heavens and the earth in truth (6:73)." Creation is "in truth" — *bi-l-ḥaqq*, the very term verse 5 said they denied: the world is made of the same stuff as the message, and the refusal to see one is the refusal to see the other.

Two cautions belong here, and the classical exegetes did not need them but modern readers do. First, the plain sense is phenomenological and true to experience: a listener in the desert sees an unbroken vault, and the verse is addressed to him — it does not require a telescope to be verified. Second, the temptation to convert "no rifts" into a catalogue of modern physics claims (the stability of orbits, the integrity of the ozone layer, the absence of atmospheric rupture) should be handled as illustration, not as proof. The verse invites reflection on coherence; if it were contingent on a particular state of cosmology it would be refutable by science, which is exactly what the Qur'an's arguments are not, since they work by changing the observer rather than by out-predicting his instruments. Where the ancients did press a natural-philosophical point, they did it in the register of sustained dependence, which is safer and deeper: "If it were to fall there is none who can hold it except Him" (35:41); "God holds the heavens and the earth that they not shift, and if they were to shift, none could hold them after Him at all" (35:41), and "He wraps the night over the day and wraps the day over the night, driving both, each pursuing the other" (39:5; cf. 7:54, 13:2, 21:33, 22:61, 31:29, 35:13, 36:37). The heavens do not merely *lack* cracks; they are actively *kept* from cracking.

**Awe, and What a Society Loses Without It**

The command to look upward is not devotional decoration. Consider what the verse is doing to the person addressed in verses 2-5: he has been asserting that a return to life is "far off" and has been marvelling at a warner's pedigree. The sky is the antidote on both counts. It is the largest object available to his senses, it is unbroken, it is decorated for nobody's profit, and it is not a social rank: no one's family honour is attached to the stars, so no reactance is triggered. The sūrah is giving the confused man an exercise in which his ego cannot intervene — a thing to look at that asks nothing of his status except that he notice it.

That exercise has a measurable modern cost when it is unavailable. A child who has never seen the Milky Way has lost the most accessible school of humility in human history; and the loss is not incidental to the disbelief described in this sūrah but materially connected to it. The classical scholars recommended, precisely in this spirit, *tafakkur* — reflection in the open at night, where the vault is visible and the mind is quiet — and the devotional literature reports that standing under the stars was for the earliest Muslims an occasion of weeping rather than of astronomy. The Prophet ﷺ himself tied the sight of the sky to remembrance, teaching his community to say when the new moon appeared:

> "O God, bring it upon us with blessing, with faith, with safety and with Islam. My Lord and your Lord is God." (Sunan al-Tirmidhī, from Ṭalq ibn ʿAlī; graded *ṣaḥīḥ* by al-Ḥākim and accepted by al-Dhahabī)

**From Sky to Resurrection: The Reason This Verse Is Placed Here**

The placement is the final point and the one that binds this verse to its context. The sūrah is not composing a nature poem; it is answering verse 3. The line of thought runs: you call a return from dust far-off, yet you grant that everything you see exists by an act of making (vv. 6-11); the making has no cracks; the maker does not tire (v. 15); and the same making goes on around you every year as dead ground turns green (v. 11). The sky, then, is a demonstration not that God *can* raise the dead as an abstract possibility, but that the order in which you live is already, at every level, an act of deliberate, unbroken, generous construction — so that "return" is not an interruption of the world but the next movement of the same making. The verse ends on the absence of rifts; the reader who has taken it is left with a mind more capable of the claim of verse 3 than it was before. The argument changes nothing in the sky and something in the observer, which is, in this Book's account of proof, the same thing.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:7

> **The earth We spread out, and cast therein firm mountains, and caused every delightful kind to grow therein,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Object Placed First: "The Earth — We Spread It"**

The verse's syntax does the theology. Normally one says *daḥaḥnā al-arḍa*; here the object is fronted — *wa-al-arḍa daḥadhnāhā* — putting "the earth" in the position of emphasis, as if to say: as for the earth, look what has been done with it. Arabic uses this device to single out or to contrast; al-Zamakhsharī calls it *takhṣīṣ*, and the effect at this point in the sūrah is that the hearer's footing is suddenly made an exhibit. The sky was above them and beyond their claim; the earth is under them, and it belongs to no one. Between the two, the Quraysh's sense of ownership — the ground on which they stood as the wardens of the Sanctuary, the custodians of the House — is quietly dismantled: your earth is something done *to*, by Someone.

*Daḥa* occurs twice in the Qur'an, in our verse and in "and the earth, after that He spread it" (79:30), and in both places the sense is the same: made broad, levelled, prepared for use. The related idea is stated in the vocabulary of furnishings: "It is He who made for you the earth a bed (*firāshan*) and the sky a structure" (20:53; cf. 2:22; 15:19 "and the earth We spread out"; 71:19 "and God made for you the earth a carpet (*biṣāṭan*)"). The earth is described as furniture: prepared, spread, and made for sleeping and walking on.

**Bed, Carpet, Cradle: The Psychology of a Prepared World**

The metaphor deserves attention because it answers a need the sūrah has not mentioned. A person who cannot sleep well is a person under threat; a world that functions as a bed is a world in which an animal that must lie down twice a day is protected while unconscious. Ibn Kathīr, explaining 20:53, stresses the sequence of grace in that verse — bed, then roof over the head, then water, then fruit — "these are the four things a human being cannot do without," and the same sequence is what our verses 6-11 lay out in the same order: sky, earth, water, plants. The order is not poetic but existential, from what shelters to what feeds. The sūrah thus grounds its argument for resurrection in the one thing even the sceptic cannot dispute: he has been sleeping, walking, drinking and eating in a world that was ready for him before he arrived and will feed him whether or not he thanks anyone.

A modern observer might add the scale of the engineering implied by the verse's single word *rawāsī*: the planet's axial stability, its magnetosphere, its atmosphere's thickness, the water cycle's balance. The exegetes, lacking those instruments, read the same word in terms of the ground beneath their feet not liquefying, which is exactly what the verse itself names as the purpose: "He cast on the earth firm mountains lest it should shake with you" (16:15; cf. 21:31, 31:10, 78:7 "awtan", pegs).

**"Lest It Shake With You": Mercy in the Language of Geology**

The mountains are mentioned not for their sublimity but for their function as *qarār*, steadiness. *Rawāsiya* is from *rawāsa/irusā*, firmness and permanence — the same root gives *ar-rawāsiyyah* used for men of settled character; the word therefore carries a moral resonance the commentators exploit: what is *rāsī* does not sway. Al-Qurṭubī notes that God names the mountains "as a mercy," for had the ground not been fixed, "one could not build, nor till, nor dwell in tranquillity." Ibn ʿAbbās's famous gloss, preserved by al-Ṭabarī, is that the earth began to move, so God created mountains and "took them by the roots and set them upon it," which is why they are called *rawāsī*: they *ruwisat* — were made to settle — the earth.

The image of a world that would otherwise shake is one of the sūrah's most powerful, and it prepares the reader for something else: the Day when that steadiness is revoked. "On the Day when the earth is changed into another earth" (14:48); "when the earth is shaken with its [final] earthquake, and the earth discharges its burdens, and man says: What is wrong with it?" (99:1-3); "and the mountains will be like carded wool" (101:5); "and you will see the mountains and think them solid, but they will pass as clouds pass" (27:88). The stability described in verse 7 is thus not a property of matter but a loan, which is why it can be recalled at will — the same act that steadies the ground is the act that will empty the graves.

**"Every Delightful Kind": The Word That Refuses to Be Narrow**

*Wa-anbatnā fīhā min kulli zawjin banīq* — "and We grew therein of every *zawj* that is *banīq*." Both nouns are rich. *Zawj* properly means a pair or mate, and by extension a kind or category whose members belong together; the verse's phrase *kulli zawjin* is therefore better than "every species": it says that things come in matched sets, in complementarity. This is why the Qur'an, in a closely parallel passage, uses the same vocabulary with an explicit conclusion: "Glory be to Him who created the pairs (*al-azwāj*) all of them — of what the earth grows, of themselves, and of what they do not know" (36:36; cf. 51:49, "of every thing We created pairs, so that you may be reminded," and 13:3, "He caused therein of every fruit two kinds (*zawjayn*)"). The logic of the pair is the logic of the argument: sexual reproduction, day and night, male and female, sea and land — the world is not a heap of individuals but of complements, and complements imply a design that requires both.

*Banīq* is the harder word, and its range is the point. Derived from *banq*, prominence and distinction, it is glossed by Ibn Qutaybah and by most of the commentators as "splendid, excellent, beautiful," and by others — on a report from Ibn ʿAbbās — as "long-statured" or "abundant." Al-Ṭabarī prefers the sense of beauty and excellence: "of every beautiful, splendid kind, pleasing to the eyes." Choosing among them is unnecessary: the verse is describing vegetation under three aspects at once — pleasing, generous, and impressive in stature — and the next clause of verse 10 will do exactly that with a single plant (the date-palm, which is tall, generous, and, to a desert eye, beautiful).

**"Delightful" as a Theological Category**

Why insist that plants are *banīq* and not merely useful? Because the sūrah's opponent is not asking for calories; he is asking what the world is *for*. A world that supplies food could be a machine; a world that supplies food in beautiful variety is an act of address. The Qur'an draws the inference explicitly elsewhere: "Have you seen that which you drink? Did you send it down from the clouds, or are We the senders? If We had willed, We could have made it bitter, so why do you not give thanks?" (56:68-70) and "It is He who sends down rain for you, from it is drink and from it is vegetation in which you pasture your flocks. He grows for you thereby grain, and olives, and dates, and grapes, and of every fruit; truly in that is a sign for a people who reflect" (16:10-11). The taste, colour and form of what is merely necessary is the surplus that carries the message. The classical writers had a phrase for it: *mālā ḥājata ilayhi fī ḥifẓ al-baqāʾ, wa-kāna min tahṣīl al-ladh-dhah* — what is not needed for survival yet obtained as delight, which is not explainable of a purposeless nature.

**The Ostrich-Egg Rumour, and How Not to Read a Word**

The verb *daḥāhā* has become famous through a claim widely repeated in modern preaching: that the word "contains" the image of an ostrich egg, since the Arabs say *daḥat al-naʿāmatu bayḍahā* ("the ostrich deposited her egg"), and that this anticipates the earth's sphericity. The responsible philological verdict is that the claim is a modern construction built on a phonetic resemblance. *Daḥa* in the dictionaries means to spread and extend; the ostrich's act of making a hollow and depositing an egg is a related but distinct sense, and no classical exegete — neither al-Ṭabarī, nor Ibn Kathīr, nor al-Qurṭubī, nor al-Zamakhsharī in his linguistic notes — reads our verse as "the earth was round like an ostrich egg." Nor does the verse require it: the sphericity of the earth was known to educated Muslim astronomers from the ninth century onward, and the commentators reconciled it with the language of "spreading" without difficulty, since what is spread is the habitable surface, experienced as plane. Al-Qurṭubī states plainly that the earth is round like a sphere, and that the "spreading" is spoken of in terms of how it is for people — a fine illustration of the principle that a revealed text describes the world as its first audience meets it, and that this is not a defect but the condition of the text's universality.

The lesson of the rumour is methodological, and it matters more than the etymology. When a verse is made to yield a modern fact, the verse becomes hostage to the modern fact: today's physics is tomorrow's error, and each correction would then count as a correction of the revelation. The sūrah's procedure is safer and more penetrating. It points at a ground that does not shake, a sky that does not split, and plants that come in pairs, and then asks what these things mean. The reader who follows is not being shown a data point; he is being asked to re-see the place he is standing in.

**The Earth as a Second Witness**

Verse 6 gave the sky and its absence of rifts; verse 7 gives the earth and its abundance. Together they bracket the human being: overhead, unbroken; under foot, spread, steadied, and crowded with kinds. This is the sūrah's response to "how can we be brought back?" — not because a green plant answers a metaphysical question directly, but because a person who genuinely accepts that the ground he walks on was *prepared for him*, in measured pairs, with beauty thrown in, has already conceded the existence of intentional generosity on a scale dwarfing his objection.
