Once that is conceded, the claim that the same hand will restore its clients to judgement is not a leap into the incredible; it is the only thing consistent with what has been seen. Verse 8 will make the same point from the other side: all of this was made as "insight and a reminder for every penitent servant" — that is, its purpose is not to overwhelm but to address, and it addresses the one who is already turning.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:8

> **as a source of insight and a reminder for every penitent servant.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Purposes in One Verse**

*Baṣīratin wa-dhikrā li-kulli ʿabdin muniib* — the syntax is a pair of verbal nouns in the accusative, in apposition to the acts just described: the sky built and uncracked, the earth spread, the mountains cast, the kinds grown, the water sent down — all of it is "an insight and a reminder." Two functions, and the order is not interchangeable. *Baṣīrah* is sight *with the mind*: from the root *b-ṣ-r*, it names not the eye's reception but the heart's discrimination, and the Qur'an uses it for that graded faculty: "Say: This is my way; I call to God with clear insight (*ʿalā baṣīrah*), I and whoever follows me" (12:108); "Here are clear proofs (*bayyināt*) from your Lord; so whoever sees, does so for his own self, and whoever is blind does so against it" (6:104); "This is an illumination (*baṣāʾir*) for mankind, and a guidance and a mercy for a people who are certain" (45:20). *Dhikrā* is the other half: not seeing but remembering — the recovery of something already known, which is precisely what *tadhakkur* means in Arabic, as *dhikr* opposed to *ghafla* (heedlessness) rather than to ignorance. Creation, then, does two things to a human being: it shows him what he did not know, and it recalls to him what he had forgotten.

That pair corresponds exactly to the two denials that opened the sūrah. The one who marvels at a human warner needs *dhikrā*, because the covenant he is rejecting is the one he witnessed before he was born ("Am I not your Lord? They said: Yes, we have testified," 7:172). The one who calls resurrection "far-fetched" needs *baṣīrah*, because his objection is a failure of sight, not of information: "Do they not look at what is before them and behind them of the heavens and earth?" (34:9).

**A Universe That Functions Only for a Certain Reader**

The verse's restriction is easily misread. "For every penitent servant" does not say that the cosmos exists only for the godly, nor that it is meaningless to others; it says that as *insight and reminder* it operates on the heart that is turning. The Qur'an states the principle in the plainest terms in a verse almost word-for-word parallel to ours: "Remind them, for the reminder benefits the believers" (51:55) — and, more sharply, "So remind, for the reminder benefits whoever fears it, and the most wretched will avoid it, who will enter the greatest Fire" (87:9-12). The same rain falls on three soils and produces three results, as the Prophet ﷺ taught:

> "The parable of what God has sent me with guidance and knowledge is that of rain which falls upon the earth. A portion of the earth was absorbent: it retained the water and brought forth much pasture. A portion was hard, holding water by which God benefited people, who drank from it, irrigated and sowed. Another portion was barren, holding neither water nor growing plant. That is the parable of the one who understands God's religion and benefits from what God sent me with, and of the one who raises not his head for it and does not accept God's guidance with which I was sent." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Mūsā)

The exhibit in the parable is the exhibit of our verses 7-11 — soil, water, growth — and its teaching is the same: the evidence is undiminished; the capacities of the receivers differ.

**Munīb: The Servant Defined by Direction**

*Muniib* is the active participle of *anāba*, to turn back, and its grammatical form signals repetition — not one act of repentance but a habitual orientation of return. The classical lexicographers distinguish it from *tāʾib* (one who repents) and *awwāb* (one who often returns, used at verse 32 of this sūrah) with subtle gradations: *tawba* is the turning from the sin, *ināba* the turning to God, and the *munīb* is described by Ibn al-Qayyim as one who returns to God after each slipping, and returns *to* Him in every state — so that his distinguishing trait is not sinlessness but directional stability. This is a doctrine of enormous pastoral weight, and it is placed here, at the head of the sūrah's cosmology: the intended reader of the universe is not the perfected but the returning. The Qur'an's most consoling verse on this subject belongs to the same vocabulary: "Do they not know that God accepts repentance from His servants and takes the alms?" (9:104), and the Prophet ﷺ said:

> "God is more pleased with the repentance of one of you than one of you who, in a desolate land, has his camel loaded with food and drink, loses it, and despairs of it, then lies down in the shade to die — and finds it standing beside him, so he grasps its halter and in overwhelming joy says: O God, You are my servant and I am Your Lord! He made a mistake through excess of joy." (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Anas)

Al-Rāzī notes that the Qur'an elsewhere joins to the penitent servant the quality of long forbearance — "Abraham was forbearing, compassionate, penitent (*munīb*)" (11:75) — as though to teach that the man of return is also the man of endurance; and the pairing of the two words here ("penitent servant") holds the servant's station and the sinner's history together: *ʿabd* is the rank, *munīb* is the movement.

**"ʿAbd": The Title the Sūrah Keeps Returning To**

Verse 8 calls the recipient "every servant," and the chapter has already named the Prophet ﷺ as "Our servant" in the register of warning, and will do so again at the end where he is told only to remind (v. 45). The word *ʿabd* in the Qur'an is not a euphemism for subjection but the highest claim a human being can make: it denotes one whose ownership has been transferred, so that his own choices are no longer the governing variable. Theologically this is a delicate theme, and the sūrah handles it in the same order as the classical *ʿaqīdah* literature: God creates, provides and revives (vv. 6-11); the human being is recorded and responsible (vv. 17-18, 22-26); and the one who ends well is the one who "comes with a heart that has *turned*" (v. 33). The *munīb* is thus neither a puppet, for the turning is his act, nor an owner, for he is called servant. This is the middle position the Ashʿarī and Māturīdī schools defended against both *Qadariyyah* (who made the human being the creator of his acts) and the strict necessitarians, and it is stated in the Qur'an in a single line: "You do not will except that God wills" (76:30; 81:29) beside "For everyone is a pledge against what he has earned" (74:38).

**Why Insight Is Placed Before Remembrance**

One more structural observation. In most of the Qur'an the *dhikr* comes first, since reminder is revelation's own name (*al-Dhikr*, 15:9; 21:50, "This is a Reminder, blessed, that We have sent down"). Here, in a passage addressed to people arguing about the resurrection of bodies, the seeing comes first. The reason is in the grain of the argument: a person who denies the unseen cannot be handed a text and told to remember; he must first be made to *look* at what he already grants — the sky, the ground, the water, the palm. Once the visible has been read as made, the unseen ceases to be an insult and becomes a continuation, and only then can *dhikrā* do its work. The verse is thus a piece of applied pedagogy: order your evidences to the state of the student. It is also a rule of life. A believer who has lost his sense of wonder will not recover it by repetition of slogans; he will recover it by looking, attentively and without agenda, at the world — which is what this sūrah, from verse 6 onward, is one long demonstration of.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:9

> **And We sent down blessed water from the sky whereby We grew gardens and the harvested grain,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Only Verse That Calls Water "Blessed"**

The source's note records the striking singularity: many passages speak of God sending water down, but this is the only one that calls it *mubārak*. *Tabāraka* and its adjective *mubārak* come from *b-r-k*, whose basic sense is fixity and constancy — a camel *baraka* at the watering place, settles and stays. From that concrete image the language of blessing is built: *barakah* is good that settles, spreads, and keeps spreading; the dictionaries define it as "the fixity of divine mercy and grace in a thing, and its multiplications and enlargements." Water is the perfect instance: it is the least of liquids in value and the greatest in effect, and every one of the sūrah's following exhibits — gardens, grain, palms, the revived land, and in the next life the rivers of Paradise — is water doing something else.

The pairing of "sent down" and "blessed" is the sūrah's own vocabulary for revelation. God says of the Qur'an: "Blessed (*tabāraka*) is He who sent down the Discrimination upon His servant, that he might be a warner of the worlds" (25:1), and "This is a Reminder, blessed (*mubārak*), that We have sent down" (21:50), and, most exactly, the nearest echo of our own wording is 6:99, where rain sent down from the sky produces "the growth of all things... grain stacked in layers... and from the date-palm and its spathes, clusters hanging low," while 6:141, repeating the exhibit, attaches a duty to it: "Eat of its fruit when it bears fruit, and give its due on the day of its harvest, and do not be extravagant." The Qur'an therefore describes both water and revelation as blessed, and both as sent down; the sūrah's audience is being shown, in the vocabulary of farming, how a gift becomes an obligation.

**"From the Sky": Descent With a Measure**

*Min al-samāʾ* is deliberately generic: rain, snow, dew, the water-table lifted by transpiration — whatever the hearer's sky delivers. What matters is the direction (down, from a source not owned) and the fact that it is "sent," not merely fallen: *anzala*, in Qur'anic usage, is transitive with God as subject and always implies purpose. The Qur'an states the accompanying discipline: "And there is no thing but that with Us are its treasuries, and We send it down only in a known measure" (15:21); "We sent down water from the sky in measure, and We gave it to you to drink, and you are not for it keepers" (23:18); "And He it is who sends the winds as glad tidings before His mercy, and We sent down from the sky pure water, that We may give life thereby to a dead land and give to drink of it much of the cattle and the people He has created" (25:48-49). The phrase *bi-māʾin muṭahhar* in 25:48 gives water a second function besides irrigation: cleansing — the reason the Prophet ﷺ said that purification is half of faith (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Mālik al-Ashʿarī), and why the same word covers both the washing of the body and the washing of the soul.

The theology compressed in "in a known measure" deserves the modern reader's notice, because it is the sūrah's answer to two opposite anxieties. The first is scarcity: water is not the property of those who hold the well, the reservoir or the technology, and the verse's frank warning in another place — "Say: Have you considered: if your water were to sink into the earth, who then can bring you flowing water?" (67:30) — is addressed to a people who thought their wells secured them. The second anxiety is abundance without accountability: what is given in measure can be withdrawn in measure, and 18:45 makes the same point with a plant metaphor: "And present to them the parable of the life of this world: it is like water We send down from the sky, so that the vegetation of the earth mingles with it, and becomes dry stubble which the winds scatter."

**Gardens and the Grain of the Harvest**

*Jannātin* — enclosed gardens, the word that will name Paradise itself; and *ḥabb al-ḥaṣīd*, which the commentators took two ways: the grain that is cut and gathered (with *ḥaṣīd* as the verbal noun of harvesting), or, on the reading of Ibn ʿAbbās and others, "what is enclosed in a *ḥaṣīd*" — a hedge — i.e., grain growing in cultivated, bounded plots. Al-Ṭabarī prefers the second sense, al-Qurṭubī reports both and inclines to the first, and the difference does not touch the point: the verse names the two great categories of human food, fruit and staple, the luxury and the necessity, both traceable to a descent from above. That pairing is exactly what the sūrah needs for its argument about the resurrection: not one impressive phenomenon but the whole system of feeding, from the date to the wheat, every item of which depends on the cycle of evaporation, cloud, fall and soaking, and none of which anyone can claim to have engineered.

There is also a quiet polemic against the idolatry that surrounds the verse's audience. Water and growth were the very goods the Arabs attached to their tutelary gods and sacred sites; the sūrah, without naming anyone, re-routes the causal chain entirely to God, and the Qur'an elsewhere does this explicitly after the same exhibit: "It is He who sent down for you water from the sky — for you from it is drink, and from it is brushwood on which you pasture your animals. He causes to grow thereby for you grain, and the olives, and the date-palms, and the vines, and of every fruit. Truly in that is a sign for a people who reflect" (16:10-11) — a verse that ends, in its next clause, with the rebuke: "and you set up equals to God." Ibn Kathīr notes here, as he does repeatedly, that the display of creation is given so that "the heart is drawn to the one true Benefactor, since the idols can neither create nor provide."

**The Tithe Built Into the Verse**

Jewish and Christian scripture ties first-fruits to gratitude; so does Islam, and the link between "blessed water" and obligation was worked out by the jurists from the Prophet's ﷺ practice in exactly this domain. The obligation of the *ʿushr* (one tenth) on rain-fed land, and of half of it on irrigated land, rests on his ruling:

> "On what is watered by the rain and by springs, or that which the sky waters, there is a tenth; and on what is irrigated with labour and cost, half a tenth." (Sunan Abī Dāwūd no. 1594, Sunan al-Tirmidhī no. 634 and Sunan Ibn Mājah no. 1830, from Ibn ʿUmar; al-Tirmidhī graded it ḥasan, and the rates are the settled practice of the Companions)

The logic of the two rates is the logic of our verse: the more directly the provision is God's, without human cost, the larger the share that returns to His servants. Blessedness, in this economy, is not a quality a thing has for its owner's enjoyment; it is a quality that increases when the thing is shared, which is why the same root is used for a meal when people gather to eat it (the *walīmah* of the wedding, at which the Prophet ﷺ said, "The worst of foods is the food of a wedding, to which the rich are invited and the poor left behind" — Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr). Read in this light, verse 9 is doing two jobs at once: proving the power that gives life and modelling the conduct that keeps blessing from evaporating.

**"Blessed": What the Adjective Does to an Economy**

*Māʾan mubārakan* — the participle is a technical term, not a flourish. The root *b-r-k* names a good that increases without being depleted and stays where it is given: pastures a people encamp in and whose grass returns, a beast that kneels and remains. An *mubārak* thing, on this etymology, is a thing that does not run out when it is used up, and that is exactly the property the verse needs, because the water it describes is used by everybody and everything, and is nonetheless "sent down in measure" — "and We sent down water from the sky in measure, and We settled it in the earth, and We are indeed able to take it away" (23:18) — to the last drop.

The Qur'an attaches the adjective to very few objects, and the list is worth assembling beside this verse. Water as such is called blessed here and nowhere else in the Book, as the source's note observes: other sūrahs speak of water sent down, of water "in measure" settled in the earth and liable to be withheld (23:18; 67:30), of gardens and grain and date-palms — but the epithet *mubārak* is attached to the sky's gift in this one sentence alone. The other *mubārak* objects are of a piece with it: the night of revelation — "We sent it down on a blessed night" (44:3); the ground of the burning bush, "blessed is whoever is in the fire, and whoever is around it" (27:8; cf. 28:30); and the Book itself, called *mubārak* in the sūrah of the Prophets ("this is a reminder sent down, blessed," 21:50) and in the sūrah of Ṣād ("a Book We have sent down to you, blessed," 38:29). The implication for the structure of Sūrah Qāf is not subtle and is entirely intended: the chapter that calls rain blessed ends by commanding the Prophet ﷺ to remind "with the Qur'an," and the two blessed things are treated as analogous media of revival — one for the land, one for the heart. Verse 11 draws the line in the sūrah's own words: "and We revive a dead land therewith — likewise shall be the coming forth" — a sentence in which the revival of the soil and the revival of the bodies are one act.

Which is why the jurists and the ascetics both found in *baraka* an economics rather than a miracle story: a share of blessed water, divided among a hundred households, is not reduced, and a Book recited in a hundred hearts is not worn thin. The Prophet's ﷺ own supplication for his community names the same principle in its temporal form, and it is the wording the classical collections give for the opening of the day: "O God, bless my Ummah in their early morning" (Sunan Abī Dāwūd, Sunan al-Tirmidhī and Sunan Ibn Mājah, from Ṣakhr ibn Wāʾil al-Ghāmidī) — so that the blessed hour, like the blessed water, is a gift one is expected to get up early and collect.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:10

> **and the date palms towering, with packed, tiered spathes,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Plant Chosen Out of All Kinds**

After "every delightful kind," the sūrah slows down to a single species: *wa-al-nakhla bāsiqātin lahā ṭalʿun naḍīd*. That it does so is a fact of rhetoric and of economics. In the Hijaz the date-palm was not one crop among others; it was the structure of survival — its fruit the staple, its trunk the timber and the roofing, its fibre the rope and the matting, its kernel the camel-feed, its shade the garden-microclimate for every other plant beneath it. When the Qur'an wants an image of a good word standing firm and giving yield, it reaches for this tree: "Have you not considered how God struck a parable of a good word? A goodly tree whose root is firm and whose branch is in the sky, giving its yield every season by its Lord's leave" (14:24-25) — and the great majority of the exegetes, from Ibn ʿAbbās onward, identified the tree in the parable as the palm, precisely because the Arab listener would have understood it without being told.

The verse, in fact, has given the sūrah one of its two names in the classical lists: *Sūrat al-Bāsiqāt*, "the Chapter of the Towering," according to a report from Ibn ʿAbbās recorded in al-Itqān and by the compilers of the sūrahs' virtues.

**"Bāsiqāt": Height That Is Also a Signal**

*Bāsiq* is from *basaqa*, to rise up, to project, to stand out from its surroundings; the noun *busūq* is height, and the same root gives *burūj*, the tall towers and the great stars ("We placed in the heaven great stars (*burūj*), and adorned it for those who look," 15:16), and *ṣawāmiʿ*, the watchtowers and monastic cells of 22:40, whose name comes from the same idea of a raised lookout. So *bāsiqāt* carries more than stature: it says conspicuous, unmistakable, hard to miss. The image of palms rising out of a flat desert floor, visible from a day's march, is the image of something meant to be noticed — which is exactly why the sūrah places it in a passage about *looking*. The palms are advertising; they are the landmarks by which a people orient themselves, and the verse makes the point that they are also the landmarks by which a soul might orient itself.

The choice of a plural feminine form (*bāsiqāt*) is also, in Arabic, a marking of individual and specific height rather than a mass quality — the trees are tall each in their own right, and the grammar of the phrase has the effect of a forest of verticals rather than a field of vegetation.

**"Ṭalʿun Naḍīd": The Detail That Proves Attention**

*Ṭalʿ* is the date-palm's spathe, the enclosing sheath from which the inflorescence emerges, and by extension the young, unopened fruit-cluster. *Naḍīd* is from *naḍʿ*/*tanāḍud* — packed one atop another, layered, contiguous; the translators render it "tiered," "stacked," "close-packed," "compound." Al-Ṭabarī gives the sense as "a fruit-cluster stacked one above another, thick with it," and Ibn Qutaybah glosses *naḍīd* as "abundant, heaped." The precision is not botanical decoration. It is the most particular observation in the sūrah, and its function is to establish the speaker's credentials as a witness: a general talk of "plants" could have been written from anywhere, but the specific knowledge that the palm's *ṭalʿ* comes out in a sheath and sets in stacked tiers is knowledge of somebody's palms, in somebody's town, at the season of pollination.

Arabian agricultural practice supplies the rest. The palms of Makkah and Madinah were, as they still are, manually pollinated: the male spathe was taken and its pollen shaken or tied into the female bunch, without which no fruit sets. The practice itself is recorded in an authentic report from Anas ibn Mālik: the Prophet ﷺ passed by a people who were pollinating their palms, remarked that perhaps they need not, and the crop came out poor; when they told him, he said:

> "You know better the affairs of your world; and when something I tell you from God is concerned, then take it, for I shall not lie about God." (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, Book of the Merits, no. 2363)

That answer is one of the Prophet's ﷺ most quoted statements about the boundary of his authority: "You know better the affairs of your world." Placed in this sūrah's argument, its significance is considerable: a chapter whose subject is resurrection and the limits of coercion in belief rests part of its case on *farmers' knowledge* — on crops, pollination and seasons — and honours that knowledge as theirs.

**The Palm as Answer to Verse 3**

The verse sits one line after "blessed water" and one line before "provision for His servants," and its placement in the resurrection argument should not be lost. The palm is, for the desert mind, the most dramatic demonstration of life from apparent death available anywhere. Its origin is a stone: the date's hard pit, dried, thrown down, seemingly the least alive of objects, from which — with water, and nothing else — rises a tree that will bear for decades. And the sūrah's hearers lived with a second marvel: the palm revived after being cut back, after a dry year, after frost; its crown, if left intact, throws out new *ṭalʿ* the next season. That a hard kernel yields a tower was the Qur'an's own chosen parable for the good word — "its root is firm, its branch is in the sky" (14:24) — and elsewhere the growth of a plant from a seed is used exactly as the analogy of resurrection: "Did you see that which you sow? Did you make it sprout, or are We the sprouter? Had We willed, We could have made it debris, and you would wonder: 'We are burdened with debt; rather, we are deprived'" (56:63-67); "Have they not seen how We created for them of what Our hands have made, cattle, so that they own them? And We subdued them for them... Did they not see the dead earth — how We give it life and bring forth from it grain, so that they eat of it?" (36:71-73, 30:19, 41:39, 57:17).

**"My Aunt": A Report, and Its Limits**

Arab feeling for the palm is preserved in a narration from Ibn ʿAbbās in which the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said: "Honour your maternal aunt, the date-palm, for it was created from the clay God took for Adam; so no tie of kinship is more deserving of maintenance than a tie owed to her" (al-Ḥākim in his *Mustadrak*, who judged it sound, and Ibn Mājah and others from other chains; several critics found its chain interrupted, and al-Albānī reckoned it of good standing in *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Jāmiʿ*). Its reasoning is a piece of folk piety in the service of ethics — the tree that shares Adam's clay is a kinsman, and kinsmen are to be treated well — and the sober commentators cite it as wisdom rather than as proof. What is securely attested is the palm's place in the Prophet's ﷺ household and table: "A house in which there are no dates is a house with no food," he said (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from ʿĀʾisha), and Muslim records that "the Messenger of God ﷺ would not go out on the day of the Breaking of the Fast until he had eaten some dates, and he would eat them in odd numbers" (from Burayda), and Bukhārī preserves the special regard for the *ʿajwa* of Madinah: "Whoever eats seven *ʿajwa* dates at the beginning of the morning, poison will not harm him that day, nor enchantment" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ).

These reports are minor, and they are worth recording only because they show how thoroughly this sūrah's imagery was at home in the Prophet's ﷺ daily life: the chapter that swears by the Glorious Qur'an speaks of the food he actually ate. There is a lesson of method there. The sūrah's proofs are not drawn from rarities but from the ordinary, and the same will be true of its ethics: the date, small as it is, becomes the currency of rescue in the most famous of its short sentences: "Protect yourselves from the Fire, even if with half a date" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim). The palm of verse 10, in other words, is not ornament between two agricultural lines. It is the sūrah's own miniature: height, generosity, and a fruit that even the poorest can use to buy himself out of the threat the chapter is named for.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:11

> **as provision for His servants. And We revive a dead land therewith — likewise shall be the coming forth.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Provision, Not Possession**

*Rizqan li-l-ʿibād* — the accusative of purpose, completing the sequence: sky, earth, water, palms, all of it "as provision for the servants." *Rizq*, in Qur'anic Arabic, is not income or capital but that which is received, and it is used indiscriminately for the good and the wicked, for animals and for human beings: "And there is no creature on earth but that upon God is its provision (*rizquhū*)" (11:6); "And how many a creature does not carry its provision — God feeds it and you" (29:60). Two facts of the language matter here. First, the possessor is never the one who provides: provision is a transfer, and the sūrah keeps the transfer in view by calling the recipients *ʿibād* rather than by their deeds or claims. Second, *rizq* in the Qur'an is measured and guaranteed together: "God extends provision for whom He wills and restricts it" (29:62; 13:26; 17:30), a verse the commentators cite to defuse the two opposite reactions people have to their income — elation and panic.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the same doctrine its most quoted image:

> "Were you to rely upon God with true reliance, you would be provided for as the bird is provided for: it goes out in the morning with an empty crop and returns in the evening with a full one." (Sunan al-Tirmidhī, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb; graded *ḥasan* by many, and strengthened by chains in Ibn Ḥibbān and al-Ḥākim)

Note that the bird *goes out*: trust in provision is not an argument for sitting still. The sūrah's own sequence is the same — rain falls, and the servant ploughs, harvests, and pays the tithe (v. 9, and 6:141 "give its due on the day of its harvest").

**"We Revive a Dead Land": The Argument in One Clause**

*Wa-ka-dhālika nuḥyī al-mawtā* — the sentence is a simile, and Arabic similes of this form (*ka-dhālika*) are elliptical: "and thus We revive the dead [land]," i.e., in the same manner as the growth you are now being shown. In 41:39 the imagery is more graphic: "And among His signs is that you see the earth humbled, and when We send down water upon it, it stirs and swells." The verb there, *tarmū wa-tabṭur*, is the language of a body quickening — it moves, it rises, it bulges; the earth is treated as something that was stiff and is now supple. And 36:33 makes the inference explicit, in words that could serve as a footnote to our verse: "And a sign for them is the dead earth: We give it life and We bring forth from it grain, so that they eat of it." The same argument is drawn out by Ibn Kathīr here: if the ground, which is the greater and the more inaccessible, is reopened year after year at God's word, the resurrection of bodies is not a new kind of act for the One who does this, but a repetition of an act already witnessed — a *qiyās awlā*, an inference from the more difficult case to the easier, which the Qur'an states in its own comparative: "and that is easier for Him" (30:27; cf. 50:15, 46:33).

Whether anything is literally "easier" for God was debated by the mutakallimūn: al-Zamakhsharī, in the Muʿtazilī register, glosses "easier" as "easier in your estimation and more worthy of being done," while Sunnī commentators such as al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr accept a plain comparative sense and attach to it a moral: the return is not merely possible but *fitting*, corresponding to what they already expect of Him. The dispute is worth remembering when a reader imagines that resurrection requires an extraordinary exception in God; the sūrah's presentation is the opposite — resurrection is ordinary for the One who does it every spring.

**"Likewise Shall Be the Coming Forth"**

*Fa-khadhālika al-khurūj* — "so thus is the going-forth." Two features of this short clause carry the verse. First, the word: *khurūj* is the sūrah's own technical term for the resurrection, repeated in its middle at verse 42, "that is the Day of Coming Forth" (*yawmu al-khurūj*). The reader who has walked through the agricultural argument from verse 9 arrives, thirty verses later, at the same noun, and the echo is the sūrah's way of closing the ring: the going-forth from the graves is to the going-forth of the shoot from the seed what the whole passage has been claiming. Second, the tense: the clause uses a noun, not a verb, and in Arabic a nominal sentence states a settled reality rather than an event to be waited for — "thus: the coming-forth," presented as a fact already in the account books, like the "preserving Book" of verse 4.

The verb used elsewhere for the same moment is *yakhrujūna min al-ajdāth* — "they will issue forth from the graves, hastening" (36:51; 54:7, "as if they were locusts spread abroad"), and the sūrah will describe it in verse 44 as an earth that splits open around them. The image the present clause invites is therefore not of a slow digging-up but of a rising: what comes out of the ground in verse 11 — the grain, the shoot, the palm — is what will come out of the ground in verse 44. The two *khurūj*s are one verb, and the desert listener, who knows that the second is certain, is being asked why he doubts the first.

**The Land That Is the Heart**

Muslim exegesis has never confined verses like this to agriculture, and the source's note records the same: "the revival of the earth can also be taken as a reference to the manner in which God revives the hearts of those who are spiritually dead." The licence for the reading is the Qur'an's own: "Is one who was dead, and We gave him life, and made for him a light by which to walk among the people, like one who is in darkness, never to emerge from it?" (6:122); "O you who believe, respond to God and the Messenger when he calls you to what gives you life" (8:24); "It is He who sent down the Discernment upon His servant… that He may give life thereby to a dead land" (25:48, with the rain imagery applied to revelation); and the plainest of all: "God has certainly shown kindness to the believers, when He sent among them a Messenger from themselves, reciting to them His signs, and purifying them, and teaching them the Book and the Wisdom" (3:164). The parallel between soil and heart is not an allegorical flourish but the reason the sūrah's natural theology is put to work at all: the water that makes a palm bear is the reminder that makes a person return, and verse 8 has already told us on which soil either of them takes effect.

**A Closing Practical Note: Living Inside a Cycle**

The three verses 9-11, read together, describe an economy in which human beings receive, use, give back, and are then taken to account — and in which none of the four steps is optional. The tithe at the harvest, the sharing of the date, the refusal to hoard water, the memory that the same One who revives the land can withhold the rain (67:30; 23:18-19) compose a worldview in which scarcity is not the primary problem and abundance is not the primary success. A modern reader, whose agriculture is industrial and whose anxiety is priced in futures, may find the framing naive or may find, more uncomfortably, that his own system is only the same cycle with the gratitude removed — desiccated aquifers, exhausted soils, and a language of "resources" that never asks who made them or for whom. The sūrah answers with the calmness of its sequence: provision is sent down, the dead land revives, the coming forth is certain. None of those three is negotiable, and the only open question in the passage is what the servant does with what he was given, which is the subject of the verses that follow, beginning with the record that never loses anything (v. 18) and ending with a threat that cannot be dodged (v. 19).

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:12

> **Before them the people of Noah, the inhabitants of al-Rass, and Thamūd denied,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**History Entered as Evidence**

The sūrah has moved from the rejectors' astonishment (vv. 2-3) to the cosmos (vv. 6-11), and now, at verse 12, it turns to the past. *Qablahum* — "before them" — is a chronological argument as well as a narrative: the Quraysh are not the first to refuse; they are the latest in a sequence, and the sequence has an outcome. Al-Ṭabarī glosses the phrase, as the source's note records, "before the idolatrous people of Muhammad who denied what he brought," and then adds the point that matters: God mentions the ancients here not to console the Prophet ﷺ alone, but to establish that the fate of denial is a known, repeatable pattern — a *sunnah* that does not shuffle: "You will find no change in God's way, and you will find no shifting in God's way" (35:43; 48:23; 6:115). The argument from precedent is not emotional. It claims that the moral order of history is as steady as the physical order of verses 6-11, and that a hearer who is willing to reason from the visible regularities of nature must be willing to reason from the recorded regularities of nations.

The Qur'an states this method explicitly in verses the classical exegetes set beside our passage: "Have they not travelled through the land and seen how the end of the former people was? They were stronger than they in strength, and they had mastered the heavens and earth and the creatures thereon; but they were destroyed for their sins. And there was none to save them from God. "And We sent no messenger before you except that We revealed to him: there is no god except Me, so worship Me" (21:25; cf. 30:9, 35:44, 40:21, 47:13, 3:137, 6:11, 7:4, 12:109, 14:45, 22:45-46, 27:52, 29:38, 40:82, 41:25, 43:23, 46:24, 50:36, 54:9-16, 69:6-8, 89:6-14, 91:11-15, 53:50-55).

**The Grammar of an Unnamed Denial**

The verb is feminine: *kadhdhabat* — "denied" — although the subjects that follow are masculine nouns or unnamed collectives. Arabic permits the predicate to agree with the *meaning* rather than the surface noun, and here the sense is "the people (qawm) of Noah denied," with the feminine carried by the implied word for the community. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both note the elegance of the resulting economy: a single verb governs three names, and the sentence does not even say *whom* they denied. The object is supplied by verse 14, "every one denied the messengers," so that the three verses function as one sentence stretched over three lines — a structure that keeps the listener leaning forward for the predicate's object, and which the sūrah exploits for its rhetorical close.

**Noah's People: The First Denial in the Sequence**

The Qur'an's account of Noah runs across dozens of passages (11:25-48; 23:23-30; 26:105-122; 54:9-16; 71:1-28; 7:59-64; 29:14-15; 10:71-73), and it is worth noticing which details the sūrah of Qāf assumes. Noah's people did not misunderstand him; they dismissed his person: "Shall a human being like you follow us, when the lowest of us, in our judgement, is with you?" (26:111) and "Do we believe that you are only followed by the most worthless of them?" (11:27; 26:111). They charged him with madness and rebuke: "He is a madman, repelled" (54:9), and they pressed the one question the preacher cannot answer for them: "We see you only as a weak man, and we do not think that the rejectors would follow you" (11:27-28; 26:111). The Qur'an's verdict on their destruction is framed as an act of divine remembrance rather than of divine anger: "We carried him upon a thing of planks and nails, running before Our eyes" (54:13-14), and "We left it as a sign: is there any who will take heed?" (54:15). And the ark is expressly called a surviving sign, which our sūrah's next verse-群 echoes in its own vocabulary of traces: "And We left it as a sign; is there any who will take heed?" (54:15; cf. 29:15, 37:75-82).

The verse's placement is therefore deliberate. A chapter that will insist on a "preserving Book" (v. 4) and on leaving visible traces for later onlookers (v. 36; cf. 54:15, "We left it as a sign: is there any who will take heed?", and 43:56, "We made them a precedent and an example to the aftercomers") opens its historical catalogue with the one catastrophe whose artefact was still, in the popular imagination of Arabia, locatable: "So We saved him and those with him in the ship, and We made them successors, and We drowned those who denied Our signs. Look, then, at the outcome of those who were warned" (10:73).

**Al-Rass: A Name Whose Uncertainty Teaches Something**

*Aṣḥāb al-Rass* — "the people of the Rass" — is the most enigmatic item in the list, occurring also at 25:38 in a triplet with ʿĀd and Thamūd: "And ʿĀd and Thamūd, and the people of al-Rass, and many generations between all that." Philologically *rass* means a well that has no water, or a well whose stones have collapsed (hence the Arabic idiom *biʾrun rass*, and the expression *rasrās* for a heap of rubbish). The exegetes preserved several identifications, and their disagreement is itself instructive. Al-Ṭabarī reports that they were a community near Kufa or in the region between Madinah and Syria, who killed the prophet sent to them and threw him into a well, whereupon God destroyed them; he also reports that *al-Rass* was a well of Thamūd, and that some said it was the "companions of the village" of Sūrah Yā-Sīn (36:13-21), the people to whom two messengers were sent and a third added, and who killed a believing man described there as "the one who came from the farthest part of the city, running." Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr incline to the view that al-Rass is a place-name whose people cannot be identified with certainty, and Ibn Kathīr states that no sound, specific report from the Prophet ﷺ has reached us about them, and that this is where the discussion should stop.

Two consequences are worth drawing out. First, an honest exegesis distinguishes what the text guarantees — a destroyed people — from what legend supplies (which well, which century). Second, the pairing with 25:37-38 in Sūrah al-Furqān shows the verse's function: al-Rass is mentioned in the company of the great named sinners, so the lesson does not depend on the identification. And there is a third, quiet point. The name means a *dry, ruined well* — a place where water once was and no longer is. A sūrah that has just described blessed water and revived land (vv. 9-11) chooses as the second nation in its list a people whose very name is the absence of water. The desert listener hears the choice.

**Thamūd: Monuments and the Murder of a Vulnerable Creature**

Thamūd are mentioned more than twenty times in the Qur'an (7:73-79; 11:61-68; 26:141-159; 27:45-53; 41:13-17; 51:45; 54:23-31; 91:11-15; 15:80-84; 17:59), and their story supplies the sūrah's own word for the outcome: "the people of al-Rass — and Thamūd; and God destroyed each of them for its sin" (cf. 11:66-67, 69). Their sin, in the Qur'an's telling, has two faces. One is monumental vanity: "Do you build on every high place a monument for amusement, and take castles as though you were to abide forever?" (26:128-129); "You hew out the mountains with skill" (26:149; cf. 7:74). The second is cruelty to a creature that could not defend itself: the she-camel, sent as a test with a share of water fixed in turn, and killed by "the most wretched of them" (91:12-14; 7:77; 54:26-29): "So they called upon their companion, and he seized and slaughtered her. And how terrible then was My punishment and My warning! We sent upon them a single shriek, so that they became like the dry stubble of the pen-keeper" (54:27-29). The moral link between pride in buildings and violence against the weak is not a connection the Qur'an draws explicitly, but it is embedded in the Thamūd narrative: those who build as if immortal and who crush what belongs to God for their own gain share one assumption — that the world is a resource with no owner.

Ibn Kathīr notes that Thamūd were the people of the Hijaz's stone country, al-Ḥijr, well known to the Quraysh's trade routes: "And Thamūd, and the dwellers of al-Rass, and many generations between" (25:38), and the point the Qur'an presses on a people whose caravans ran past their stones is that the evidence was visible: the denial of "the people of al-Hijr" is named, and the sequel adds that the signs remain for whoever reads them — "truly in that are signs for those who discern" (15:80, 84; cf. 15:79, "and both of them are upon a clear road"). A sūrah delivered in Makkah, whose merchants travelled through their ruins, is not asking the Quraysh to accept a report about strangers.

**What a List of Names Is For**

Read together, verses 12-14 constitute a roll-call that a Makkan audience could audit by eye. Its function is not to frighten with antiquity but to establish a causal claim: denial of a messenger is followed by destruction, and the sequence has been observed often enough to count as evidence (53:50-55: "And it is He who made the people of ʿĀd perish of old, and Thamūd — and they He destroyed utterly; and the people of Noah before that — they were wrongdoing and rebellious; and the overthrown towns He covered with what covered them…"). Verse 15 will then draw the inference the list is meant to license: if you think resurrection is a marvel, look at the record of what happens to those who make the impossible their argument.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:13

> **as did ʿĀd, Pharaoh, and the brethren of Lot,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Names That Map the Whole Range of Refusal**

The list now expands to three further entries, and the choice is not arbitrary. ʿĀd stand for arrogance in the body — strength, size, ancestry; Pharaoh for arrogance in political power — sovereignty, army, self-deification; the people of Lot for arrogance in appetite — the demand that desire be free of every limit, and that the community's gatherings be a law unto themselves. A sūrah whose hearers are being told that they will be returned to a God who knows what the earth takes from them (v. 4) and what the soul whispers (v. 16) thus enumerates the three great excuses: *we are strong*, *we rule*, *we want*. Ibn ʿAbbās, in the reports transmitted by al-Ṭabarī on the sins of these peoples, names each in its characteristic register — for ʿĀd, might; for Pharaoh, tyranny; for Lot's people, "the deed which no one before you and none after you will be forgiven for imitating" — a phrase the Prophet ﷺ used in a well-known narration: "Do not look at the smallness of the sin, but at the majesty of the One against whom you have sinned" — a saying of ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd reported by al-Bayhaqī in Shuʿab al-Īmān and by Abū Nuʿaym in Ḥilyat al-Awliyāʾ, whose chain the critics debate but whose sense they quote without reservation).

**ʿĀd: Strength, and the Question It Cannot Answer**

The Qur'an preserves ʿĀd's own boast almost as a quotation: "As for ʿĀd, they acted insolently in the land without right, and said: Who is stronger than us? Do they not see that God, who created them, is stronger than them in might" (41:15). The verse answers their question with a second question, and the answer is left to them: "Do they not see that God, who created them — He is stronger than them in might?" Here, in Sūrah Qāf, that argument has just been made at length (vv. 6-11) before the historical section opens, so the mention of ʿĀd is a callback: the very act of looking at the sky is what refutes "who is stronger than us." Their description in another place as "Iram of the pillars, the like of which has not been created in the lands" (89:7-8) is not a compliment; the chapter's next line makes the point — "and ʿĀd, destroyed by a violent wind" (69:6-7; and cf. 89:6-8, "Have you not seen how your Lord dealt with ʿĀd, Iram of the pillars, the like of which has not been created in the lands?" As for ʿĀd, they were destroyed by a screaming wind, which He imposed upon them seven nights and eight days of successive misfortune, so you would see the people therein as though they were hollow trunks of palm-trees").

There is a bitter precision in the punishment: the wind — the very element no human strength can resist — was sent against men who measured everything by resistance, and the simile used for their corpses is a palm-tree trunk, hollowed and worthless. The same tree the sūrah has just held up as a sign of life (v. 10) supplies the image of the death of the powerful. Ibn Kathīr notes this correspondence of image and fate as a feature of the Qur'an's method: the thing admired becomes the figure of the warning.

And ʿĀd were, in the Qur'an's telling, invited to repent before they were destroyed — a fact the sūrah of Qāf presupposes at verse 8: "Hūd said to his people: Remember God's favours upon you, when He made you successors after Noah's people, and increased you in stature — so remember God's favours, that you may prosper" (7:69; cf. 46:21, "when he warned the people of the Sandhills" — a place-name within reach of the Makkan trade routes; and 29:38: "And ʿĀd and Thamūd — indeed it is clear to you from their dwellings; Satan made their deeds fair-seeming to them, and turned them from the path, though they were seeing").

**Pharaoh: Denial as a Political Institution**

Pharaoh's entry in the list is the most concentrated. The Qur'an gives his claim in his own words — "I am your lord, the most high" (79:23); "O nobles, I know no god for you other than me" (28:38) — and the Qur'an gives its answer to the claim in the same sūrah's next breath: "So God seized him with the punishment of the Hereafter and the First" (79:25). In our sūrah, which has described the earth as spreading, firm, and productive (v. 7), Pharaoh's fate is the one fact the audience would have conceded: "We parted the sea for you, and We saved you, and We drowned Pharaoh's people before your eyes" (2:50), and "So today We will preserve you in your body, that you may be a sign to those after you" (10:92).

That last verse is the sharpest single rejoinder to the denial this sūrah is answering. The claim of verses 2-3 was that a return from dust is far-fetched. Pharaoh's end is the sūrah's demonstration that the body, whether preserved as a lesson or dispersed as dust, is precisely what God disposes of, and that He disposes of it publicly. The phrase *litakūna liman khalfaka āyah* ("that you may be a sign to those after you") was read by some of the commentators, as Ibn Kathīr records, as addressed to Israel or to the preserved corpse's onlookers; the point stands either way: denial of accountability does not exempt one from being an exhibit. Al-Rāzī adds the theological annotation: Pharaoh was left *a body* to teach that kingship is a covering, not a nature.

There is another strand of tradition about Pharaoh that Muslim exegetes mention with reserve: the reports that he was given a moment of faith at the sight of death, and that his belief was not accepted because it arrived at the point of no return (10:90-91, and cf. 4:18: "repentance is not for those who persist in evil deeds until, when death appears to one of them, he says: Now I repent"). The verse is quoted in this connection by al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr to mark the rule the sūrah of Qāf is about to insist on from the other side: the time of reminding is *this* life, and there is a "near" moment when the cover is lifted and the lifting arrives too late for the will to be tested (50:22).

**The Brethren of Lot: "Brother" as an Accusation**

The phrase *ikhwān Lūṭ*, "the brethren of Lot," follows the Qur'an's regular idiom by which every prophet is called his people's "brother" (7:65, 7:73, 7:85; 11:50, 61, 78, 84): a claim of kinship and of shared standing, not of shared religion. Lot is their brother in the sense that he belongs to them, was resident among them, and had rights upon them and they upon him — which is why their conduct is framed not as private failing but as a breach of the bonds of a common life. The catalogue of their crimes in the Qur'an is correspondingly social: "You approach males, cut off the road [rob the traveller], and commit evil in your gatherings" (29:29). The third item is the one most often overlooked in preaching, and the most relevant to this sūrah's context: their *nādī* — the assembly, the civic space, the forum where reputations are made — was itself a place of *munkar*. The destruction of Sodom, "We turned its upside down and rained upon them stones of clay, layered" (15:74; cf. 11:82), is thus presented in the Qur'an as the end of a society that had made indecency a matter of public culture, not of hidden impulse.

The link to Makkah is explicit in the sūrah's surroundings: "and you pass by them in the morning, and by night: will you not then reason?" (37:137-138); and "you have come upon the town upon which rained the rain of evil — do they not see it?" (25:40). The audience's commercial route to Syria took them past the Dead Sea and the settlements of ʿĀd and Thamūd; three of the six destroyed peoples listed in verses 12-14 were geography as well as news.

**Why the Sūrah Names So Many, and Identifies So Few**

Verse 13 is one long list without a single detail of narrative, and readers sometimes find that flat. It is not flat. In the sūrah's design, the effect of the enumeration is to convert individual stories into a *class*, and to place the Quraysh inside that class by the simplest means: names, not tales. This is the same technique the chapter uses at verse 36 ("How many a generation before them have We destroyed") and it is the technique of the closing warning of verse 45 — remind, "and the most wretched will avoid it; he will enter the greatest Fire, then neither die nor live" (87:10-13). The audience is not being told a story about foreigners; they are being placed on a list, in the last open position.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:14

> **the inhabitants of the thicket, and the people of Tubbaʿ — each denied the messengers; so My Threat came due.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Woodland People and the Sin of the Scale**

*Aṣḥāb al-Aykah* — "the companions of the Thicket," a name the source's note records as possibly a proper noun rather than a description — are mentioned in the Qur'an three times (15:78; 26:176-189; 38:13), and each time in a list that links them with Thamūd, which is why most classical exegetes identify them with the people of Madyan and their prophet Shuʿayb, whose story is told in 7:85-93 and 11:84-95. Ibn Kathīr reports the alternative view that the "Companions of the Thicket" were a distinct community to whom Shuʿayb was sent after Madyan's destruction, and he cites the majority against it; al-Ṭabarī transmits both and gives the linguistic argument for treating *al-Aykah* as a wooded place-name, "for *ayk* is any thicket of trees."

What is decisive for our purposes is the sin the Qur'an attributes to them, because it is the one item in this whole catalogue that is commercial rather than sexual, cultic or military. Their crime in 26:183 is "diminishing the measure and the weight," and in 11:85 Shuʿayb's sermon names the same cluster: "O my people, worship God: you have no other god than Him. And do not give short measure and weight. Indeed I see you in prosperity, and indeed I fear for you the punishment of an all-encompassing Day. And O my people, give full measure and weight in justice, and do not withhold from people their things" — and, later in the same sermon, "corrupting on earth" is added, with the warning: "the curse of God is upon the wrongdoers who turn people from God's path and desire it crooked" (11:88; cf. 83:1-3: "Woe to those who give less — who, when they take a measure from people, take it in full, and when they measure or weigh for them, give less"). Fraud in the scale is, in the Qur'an's moral grammar, the ordinary and respectable corruption of settled people: the defrauding of strangers and customers in the daily market. Its inclusion between Lot's civic indecency and the killing of Thamūd's camel makes the list a ladder of sins from the spectacular to the mundane — and it is the mundane rung the sūrah's own audience, who lived by the caravan trade and by the Sanctuary's prestige, would have felt as aimed at them.

**Tubbaʿ: A Title, a King, and a People**

*Qawm Tubbaʿ* — "the people of Tubbaʿ" — is mentioned only here and in Sūrah al-Dukhān: "Are they better, or the people of Tubbaʿ and those before them? We destroyed them, for they were criminals" (44:37). *Tubbaʿ* is not a personal name but a title of the kings of Ḥimyar in southern Arabia, as *Pharaoh*, *Caesar*, *Najashī* and *Kisrā* are titles; the Arab poetic tradition knew a Tubbaʿ (or Tubbaʾ) who marched east and west, and the sīra literature preserves long narratives about him, including a tradition that he came to Makkah, veiled the Kaʿbah with cloth — a practice remembered as the origin of the *kiswah* — and spoke in verse of a prophet to arise from his town, urging his people to believe in him and then returning to Yemen.

Muslim scholars differed about how much of that lore to credit. A report transmitted by Ibn Isḥāq, al-Ḥākim, and Aḥmad has the Prophet ﷺ say: "Do not curse Tubbaʿ, for he had become Muslim," and some of the exegetes (Ibn Kathīr among them) quote traditions making Tubbaʿ a believer who warned his people and was rejected by them — which, if accepted, explains the sūrah's unusual phrasing "the *people* of Tubbaʿ" rather than "Tubbaʿ": the destroyed party is his nation, not necessarily the man. The chains of these reports are weak or interrupted by the hadith critics' standards, and the cautious verdict is the one al-Ṭabarī illustrates: the verse establishes that a people bearing this name were destroyed for denial, and the pious legends about their king belong to history, not to revelation. The honesty of that distinction is not a minor scholarly tic: the Qur'an itself twice declares that it does not recount the past for curiosity's sake — "That is of the news of the unseen: We reveal it to you. You were not with them when they cast their pens, as to which of them would marry Mary, nor were you present when they disputed" (3:44; cf. 11:49, 12:102, 28:46), and "therein is a sign for whoever fears the punishment of the Hereafter; that is a Day for which mankind will be gathered, and that is a Day of witnessing. And We do not delay it save for a known term" (11:103-104).

**"Each Denied the Messengers": The Escalation Built Into the Object**

The verse does not say each denied *its own* messenger but *the messengers* (*al-rusula*) — plural, definite. The rule the Qur'an derives from that phrasing is stated in its own terms: "The messengers are one family, children of one father; their mothers differ, and their religion is one," and, in the Qur'an's language of belief: "The Messenger believes in what has been sent down to him from his Lord, and the believers; each believes in God, His angels, His Books, and His messengers: We make no distinction between any of His messengers" (2:285); and, in the converse, as a statement of unbelief: "Those who disbelieve in God and His messengers, and wish to separate God from His messengers, saying 'We believe in some and disbelieve in some,' and intend to take a course between that — those are the true disbelievers, and We have prepared for the disbelievers a humiliating punishment" (4:150-151). To reject the messenger who comes to *you* is, on this account, to reject the office that brought every messenger; and the Qur'an's self-testimony is consistent: the Meccan rejection is placed on the same ledger as the rejection of Noah (41:13: "if they turn away, say: I have warned you of a thunderbolt like the thunderbolt of ʿĀd and Thamūd," and 22:42: "if they deny you, then peoples before them denied — the people of Noah, ʿĀd and Thamūd, the people of Abraham, the people of Lot, the dwellers of Madyan, and the discredited ones; and Moses was denied — but I respite the disbelievers, then I seized them; and how was My denial!").

**"So My Threat Came Due": The Grammar of Divine Self-Binding**

*Fa-ḥaqqa waʿīd* — "so My threat became due," literally "became true/necessary." The verb *ḥaqqa* is the same root as *al-ḥaqq*, and it belongs to the language of obligation, of a debt that has fallen due. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to gloss it: "My punishment became binding upon them," and al-Ṭabarī explains the phrase as "what I had promised them by My Book and by the mouths of My messengers was fulfilled for them." This is a delicate piece of ʿaqīdah and the sūrah states it in four words, in the same movement as verse 29: "With Me the Word is not altered, and I do not wrong My servants."

The doctrine is twofold. First, God's threat is not caprice and not the momentary anger of a powerful being; it is information that is already "due" in the sense that it is entailed by what He has declared — and it may equally be averted, because His mercy is likewise self-binding: "Say: Peace be upon you; your Lord has prescribed mercy upon Himself" (6:54; cf. 7:156, 39:53). Second, the moral force of the sentence lies in its impersonality: the destruction of these peoples is not narrated as a divine temper but as the working-out of a public declaration. The Qur'an insists on the corollary with the plainest of its formulas: "That is because your Lord would not destroy the towns for wrongdoing while their people were unaware" (6:131; and the rule in God's own words: "We do not punish until We send a messenger" (17:15; cf. 28:59; 35:24)). Verse 14's threat, that is, comes due only where warning has first come — which is the reason the sūrah's final word is a command to warn (v. 45).

**The First Person Singular, and Why It Matters**

One last feature of the verse is easy to miss because it is a pronoun. After eleven verses in which the cosmic acts are attributed to the royal "We," the threat is spoken in the first person singular: *waʿīd* — "My threat." The same shift recurs at verses 31 ("My servants"), 35 ("with Us there is more"), and 45 ("My threat"), and the effect is what the rhetorical theorists of the Qur'an call *iltifāt*, an address-turn: the detached register of the cosmos suddenly becomes personal at the points where a person's fate is decided. A hearer who has been shown a sky and a crop is now spoken to in the first person. The chapter's threat, in short, is not reported about God; it is delivered by Him, and the same verse that ends the list of the destroyed ends with an "I" that will not let the reader remain a spectator.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:15

> **Did We then weary in the first creation? Nay, but they are in doubt regarding a new creation.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Rules Out Its Own Answer**

*ʾA-faʿayinā ʿani al-khalqi al-awwal* — the interrogative is a rebuke, and the verb is *ʿāya*, to be at a loss, exhausted, unable to find a way out; the nominal is *ʿayy*, incapacity. So the sense is: "Did We fail, did We tire, did We come up short at the first creation?" Al-Zamakhsharī points out the sarcasm of the choice of verb: they had not suggested that God was tired; they had suggested that a return was impossible. The verse answers a claim they did not make by asserting the one thing they cannot deny — and the rhetorical logic is exact. The rejectors of resurrection never argue "God is too weak"; they argue "resurrection is too improbable." Verse 15 closes the gap between the two: improbability is a property of creaturely capacity, and the question under discussion is not about the creature.

The classical commentators uniformly record the verse's answer as supplied by the hearer's own concession: no one who believes he was made from nothing thinks his Maker ran out of strength. Elsewhere the Qur'an states the reasoning in a complete sentence: "Have they not considered that God, who created the heavens and the earth and did not tire at their creation, is able to give life to the dead? Yes, indeed: He is powerful over all things" (46:33); cf. also 67:14, "He who created knows — and He is the Subtle, the Aware," and 21:23, "He is not asked about what He does, but they are asked." Al-Ṭabarī's gloss on our verse is simply: "Did the first creation overwhelm Us, so that the second was too heavy for Us?" — and the answer, in the sūrah's own words, is verse 44: "That is a gathering easy for Us."

**"The First Creation": An Exhibit They Were Told to Inspect**

The phrase *al-khalq al-awwal* points at two things in the Qur'an's usage, and both are meant. It is the creation of the world, and it is their own individual beginning: "Does man not recall that We created him before, when he was nothing?" (19:67); "Has there not passed over man an era of time when he was not a thing mentioned? — Truly We created man from a mixed drop, trying him, and made him hearing and seeing" (76:1-2); and the sūrah's own echo of its opening is a help here, since the same "noble Qur'an" that begins this chapter is described elsewhere as "a glorious Qur'an in a preserved Tablet" (85:21-22) — the record of the decree and the record of creation being, in this book's vocabulary, one book. The embryological passage in al-Ḥajj is recited by the exegetes at exactly this point, because it converts the abstract claim into a memory the hearer can test: "O people, if you are in doubt about the Resurrection — truly We created you from dust, then from a drop, then from a clot, then from a lump of flesh, formed and unformed, so that We make clear to you. And We settle in the wombs whom We will, until an appointed term, then We bring you out as infants" (22:5; cf. 23:12-16, 75:37-39, 86:5-7, 56:57-59). The argument is not that bodies assemble themselves; it is that each addressee was, stage by stage, assembled by Someone, and cannot have forgotten it without effort.

The Qur'an elsewhere invites a walking inspection of the record: "Say: Travel through the earth and observe how He began creation, and God will produce the final creation. Truly God is powerful over all things" (29:20); and "He begins creation, then repeats it, and that is easiest for Him" (30:27). On "easiest," the commentators split: al-Zamakhsharī glosses "easiest in your reckoning and most fitting to be described as easy," while Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī accept the plain comparative and treat it as an allowance to human speech. Both are agreed on the underlying theology, and it is a subtle point worth preserving: for the Necessary Being there is no difficulty to be overcome, so "easy" and "hard" can only describe the order of created causes — and the verse's function is not to measure God but to deflate the claim that the return is a strain too great for Him to undertake.

**"A New Creation": Resurrection, and Something Wider**

*Khalqin jadīd* — "a new creation" — is used in the Qur'an of the Resurrection in the very objection-form of our rejectors: "If you wonder, wondrous indeed is their saying: What! When we are scattered dust, are we in a new creation? Those are the ones who disbelieve in their Lord, and those are the ones with chains on their necks" (13:5; cf. 32:10, 17:49, 98); and of the world-to-come transformed: "On the day when the earth is changed into another earth, and the heavens, and they appear before God, the One, the Subduing" (14:48; cf. 56:61, "so that We may replace your kind with others like you and create you in a form you do not know"). Al-Ṭabarī explains our verse thus: they are not denying an old story; they are in doubt about a thing they have never seen, and they judge it impossible because it is unprecedented — which, he remarks, mistakes the measure of possibility, since the precedent of their own non-existence is on the record.

The source's note records a second, wider sense taken from the mystical commentators (al-Kāshānī): the "new creation" is the perpetual re-creation of the world, "in which God's creative act is renewed at every instant." This is not an eccentric gloss. It is the doctrine of the Ashʿarī school in kalām, whose standard maxim is that accidents do not persist through two consecutive instants and that the world is therefore *mukhtraʿ* — freshly invented — at every moment; al-Ghazālī defends the position in the *Tahāfut al-Falāsifah* against the philosophers' necessary emanation, and al-Ījī states it in the *Mawāqif* as the Sunnī view. Its Qur'anic anchor is verse 55:29: "Everyone in the heavens and earth asks Him; every day He is in some affair" — *shaʾan* read by the commentators as a state, an act, a new configuration. Ibn ʿArabī and his school drew from this the conclusion that resurrection is not an interruption of the world's order but its continuation: if you are being created now, you will be created again, and the "new creation" of the grave is the same verb with a different object. Whether one reads verse 15 as referring to the Hour or to the renewal of every instant, the sūrah's answer to the "far-fetched return" is unchanged: novelty is not a defect in a thing, it is the way the world is made.

**Shakk, Not Jahl: The Diagnosis of Doubt**

The end of the verse is the sharpest clause: *bal hum fī shakkin min khalqin jadīd* — "rather, they are in a doubt about a new creation." Not in ignorance, not in enquiry, not in suspended judgement: in *shakk*. The word is repeated in the Qur'an for precisely this state, which the sūrah's audience would have heard as a correction of their own vocabulary, since "we are not certain" is what polite disbelief prefers to say: "They say: Is it when we are in the body that we shall be in a new creation? Have they not considered that God who created the heavens and earth… ?" (32:10; cf. 45:24-25, "They say: There is only our life of this world; we die and live, and nothing destroys us but time. They have no knowledge of that; they only conjecture. And when Our clear signs are recited to them, their argument is only: Bring back our fathers, if you are truthful"). The other verses on the subject are equally frank about its origin: "We know that what they say grieves you; it is not you they call a liar, but the wrongdoers deny God's signs" (6:33; cf. 27:14); and "those who were made to inherit the Book after it are in a doubting disagreement about it" (42:14).

There is a linguistic point in the source's own note worth keeping: *marīj* in verse 5, applied to their state, is a passive participle ("mixed up"), and some lexicographers read it as active — "that which throws into confusion." The two halves of this opening section then lock: they are in a *confounded* affair (v. 5) because they are in a *doubt* (v. 15), and their doubt is not the innocent suspension of judgement but a habit of mind that refuses to weigh what it has been shown. The sūrah will name the only disposition that can cure it: not more information, but the turning of the will — "a reminder for whoever has a heart" (v. 37).

**The Verse as Hinge**

Finally, see what verse 15 does structurally, for its position is the reason the passage works. It answers verse 3 (the "far-fetched return"), it closes the argument from the cosmos (vv. 6-11) and from history (vv. 12-14), and it hands the reader to verse 16, where the subject changes decisively from what God has made outside a person to what He knows and does inside one: "We created man, and We know what his soul whispers." The argument has now been run in three registers — the objection is illogical, it is contradicted by nature, and it is contradicted by history — and it has not yet touched the hearer's own case. The next thirty verses will spend themselves doing exactly that, beginning with the claim that no distance separates the doubter from the One whose power he doubts. The "far-fetched return" of verse 3 meets, at verse 16, a God who is nearer than the jugular vein; and the sūrah's whole architecture is built to make the reader feel the collision of those two images of near and far.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:16

> **We did indeed create man, and We know what his soul whispers to him; and We are nearer to him than his jugular vein.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Lens Turns From the Sky to the Throat**

After ten verses of cosmology and four of history, the lens closes on a single human being. *Wa-la-qad khalaqnā al-insāna wa-ʿalimnā mā tuwaswisu bihī nafsuh* — "We created man, and We know what his self whispers to him." The verb of creating is the same one the sūrah has used for the sky's building and the grain's sprouting; the object is now "the human," and the sūrah's argument arrives at its point of contact. Everything before verse 16 was evidence placed outside the hearer; from verse 16 onward the evidence is the hearer. The conjunction *wa-la-qad* ("and indeed We…") is asseverative, the register of a solemn confirmation, and it is used here where a mere statement of fact would not prepare the listener for the claims that follow — recording angels, the stupor of death, a lifted cover.

*Al-Insān* here is the human being as such, not a particular individual; al-Ṭabarī reports the view that it means man in his kind, and the source's note records that some, with Qatādah and in a reading al-Qurṭubī transmits, take it of Adam, whose inclination toward the forbidden tree was known to God before it happened. Both readings converge on the same theology: the One who made the species is not surprised by its behaviour, and no action arising from within it is hidden from Him.

**What "Whisper" Means in Arabic**

*Waswās* is onomatopoeic: the root *w-s-w-s* denotes a faint repeated sound — footsteps, the rustle of ornaments, a murmur — and in the Qur'an it becomes the technical word for the mind's inner chatter. It is used of Satan, "who whispers into the breasts of mankind, from among jinn and mankind" (114:4-5), and of the hardening that follows when the whisper is obeyed: "they believed, then disbelieved, so God sealed their hearts and they do not understand" (63:3). The whisperer himself is described by his retreat: "from the evil of the whisperer who withdraws, who whispers in the breasts of mankind, from among jinn and mankind" (114:4-6). Applied here to *nafs* rather than to shayṭān, the verb says something carefully: God knows not only the intention you have settled on but the half-formed suggestion that has not yet become a decision. The verse's second half is thus a statement of knowledge so fine-grained that no self-deception survives it, and the source's note preserves a variant reading, "and We know that of which his soul whispers," which brings out the same claim.

The Qur'an's anthropology of this level is explicit elsewhere: "I do not declare myself free of blame; truly the self is a commandress to evil (*amārah bi-l-sūʾ*), except as my Lord has mercy" (12:53); "and I swear by the self that blames" (*nafs lawwāmah*, 75:2); and, at the other end of the ascent, "O soul at rest, return to your Lord, well-pleased and pleasing" (89:27-28). Ibn al-Qayyim, building on these three verses, set out the soul's stages — commanding to evil, self-reproaching, and at peace — and placed our verse at the beginning of the analysis: the *waswās* is the raw material of the first stage, the *inābah* of verse 8 is the work of the second, and the *ṭumaʾnīnah* of the third is what the Garden addresses in verse 34. In another place the same fact is given as a moral endowment rather than a liability: "And a soul, and what shaped it evenly, and inspired into it its wickedness and its God-warring care" (91:7-8) — a verse in the same sūrah that goes on to destroy Thamūd for the killing of a camel (91:11-15), which is the exact people named in our sūrah at verse 12.

**"Nearer Than His Jugular Vein": The Sentence That Made Commentators Careful**

*Wa-naḥnu aqrabu ilayhi min ḥabl al-warīd* — literally, "and We are nearer to him than the vein-cord of the throat." *Ḥabl al-warīd* is an idiom for the two great veins running down either side of the neck; some of the lexicographers take *ḥabl* here as "vein" itself (so the phrase is "the cord-vein"), and al-Ṭabarī reports both. The image is of the most intimate internal adjacency imaginable: something inside you, carrying the blood on which every thought depends, is declared less close than God.

Because the sentence is so vivid, it has been read with unusual care, and the readings fall into three families.

- *The nearness of knowledge, sight, and power.* This is the reading of the majority, and it is anchored by parallel verses where the same claim is made without any language of proximity of place: "He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the breasts conceal" (40:19); "God knows what is in the breasts" (64:4; cf. 2:284; 57:6; 24:64); "Do you not see that God knows what is in the heavens and the earth? There is no secret conversation of three but that He is their fourth, nor of five but that He is their sixth, nor less than that or more, but that He is with them wherever they are" (58:7); "He is with you wherever you are" (57:4). To these the commentators add 2:186: "When My servants ask you about Me — truly I am near; I answer the call of the caller when he calls upon Me."
- *The nearness of two angels.* Al-Ṭabarī transmits from a number of the Salaf that "We" here refers to the angels who are sent to the human being and are closer to him than his jugular — a grammatically possible reading of the royal "We" and one that a few early exegetes preferred. The source's note records Ibn Kathīr transmitting this, and the standard criticism of it is decisive: the verse's own second clause is about God's *knowledge* of the whisper, and verse 17 then introduces the angels as a new item; making "We" mean angels in verse 16 robs the sentence of its point, and the sūrah's whole register from verse 4 onward is divine first person.
- *The nearness of the Real to every reality.* This is the reading the source's own note adopts, and it is the classical position of the mystical commentators: "the Divine Reality is the substrate of every reality, and God is the nearest and closest Reality to the human being," so that the verse states an ontological priority, not a location.

Ibn Taymiyyah, addressing precisely this verse in his writings on divine attributes, formulates a middle way between a literal bodily proximity and a bare metaphor: he distinguishes a nearness that is general — God "with" His creatures by knowledge, power and sustaining, as in 57:4 and 58:7 — from a nearness that is special and elective, the nearness of help, love and answer, as in "He is with you" for the believers, and from the nearness of approach on the Day itself ("and your Lord comes, and the angels, rank upon rank," 89:22). On his account, verse 16 belongs to the first two kinds and says nothing about movement or distance; what it denies is that any place is nearer to a person than God's knowledge and power, and what it promises is that the One who is thus near is the One who answers.

**Why the Verse Is Placed Where It Is**

The location is not decoration. Three lines earlier the rejectors had called the return "far off" (*baʿīd*, v. 3), and the sūrah answered that their remoteness was a moral, not a spatial, judgement (vv. 4-5). Now it produces the counter-image: nothing is far, because the One they are fleeing is nearer than the vein that keeps them alive while they argue. The verse thus completes the argument of verses 6-15 at its weakest joint: it is not that resurrection is "possible for a powerful being" in the abstract, but that the agent of return is already, at this moment, the nearest reality to the person who denies it. Distance was never the issue; attention was.

The same placement explains the next verse. If God is nearer than the jugular, why are there scribes at the right and left hand (v. 17)? Verse 16 answers in advance: the angels do not inform God. They are for the human being's benefit — the record he will need on the Day when his own memory is not enough (17:13-14, "We have fastened every man's fate to his neck, and We will bring out for him on the Day of Resurrection a book he will meet spread open; read your book! Today your own self is enough as a reckoner against you"). The two verses together hold the sūrah's whole doctrine of accountability: knowledge belongs to God, and record belongs to the servant.

**A Practical Discipline: Knowing That One Is Known**

The verse's effect in Islamic spirituality has been enormous, and the technical term for it is *murāqaba* — self-watchfulness, from the same root as the *raqīb* of verse 18. Al-Muḥāsibī's *Kitāb al-Riʿāya* and al-Ghazālī's discussions of *muraqaba* treat this verse as the scriptural basis for an exercise: to live as one who is already witnessed. The exercise is not morbid, and it is not the same as anxiety. A person who is watched by a camera behaves differently at the surface; a person who knows he is known behaves differently at the depth, because the knowledge in question reaches the *waswās* itself. The result, if the tradition is to be believed, is a strange lightening: the secrets one has to keep from every other human being — the unconfessed resentment, the wish one is ashamed of, the intention one cannot avow — are already, without confession, fully known. The Qur'an makes this the ground of repentance's ease rather than of its difficulty: "He knows the treachery of eyes and what the breasts conceal" (40:19); and "He it is who created the heavens and the earth in six days, and His throne was upon the water, that He might try which of you is best in deed" (11:7).
