Note also the complementary permission: "God overlooks in this community the whisperings of the self so long as they are not acted upon or spoken" (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra), which prevents the verse from being read as a demand that every involuntary thought be criminal. Being known is a mercy at the same moment as it is a warning, because the knowledge that reaches my worst whisper is the same knowledge that reaches my unspoken repentance — and verse 32 will end with an address to exactly that person: "This is what is promised to every oft-returning keeper."

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:17

> **When the two receivers receive, seated on the right and on the left,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Who Catch What Falls From the Mouth**

*Idh yatalaqqā al-mutalaqqiyyāni ʿan al-yamīni wa-ʿan al-shimāli maqʿad* — the root is *l-q-y*, to meet something and take it as it arrives; *mutalaqqī* is "one who receives and takes in hand," and the doubled form makes the pair explicit. The verb's imagery is worth lingering on: a spoken word is a thing that leaves the mouth and falls, and the two angels are set to catch each one before it drops. The sūrah will say of the record in verse 23 "this is what I have ready" (*hādhā ladayya ʿatīd*), and in verse 18 that the watcher is *ʿatīd* — in position, waiting.

The phrase *ʿan al-yamīni wa-ʿan al-shimāli maqʿad* is where the translators part company. Most render it "one on the right and one on the left, seated," taking *maqʿad* as the place of sitting; the source's note records al-Ṭabarī's transmission of a different parse, in which *maqʿad* is read as *quʿūd*, i.e. "in a state of sitting," and "sitting" itself is glossed by the lexicographers as "observing, keeping watch" — for a watcher in Arabic is *qāʿid*, as the raider on the lookout is *qāʿid* and the one who goes out is *qāʾid*. The two readings merge in sense: the angels are stationed, permanently in place, and their station is watchfulness.

**What the Right and the Left Mean**

The arrangement is functional and symbolic at once. In the reports the commentators preserve, the angel on the right is in charge of good deeds and the one on the left of bad; the right is, in Qur'anic and Arab usage alike, the side of honour and of the Book given "in the right hand" (69:19: "Then as for one who is given his book in his right hand, he will say: Here, read my book!"; and 69:25, "as for one given his book in his left hand…"), and the left the side of loss. Al-Ṭabarī transmits from Ibn ʿAbbās and others that the angel at the left is subordinate to the angel at the right, and that when a servant is about to sin the one on the left is told to wait — "leave him, perhaps he will glorify God" — in the hope that the deed will not be written. The narration is a *mawqūf* report with a contested chain and should be cited as an edifying tradition about God's mercy rather than as a fact about angelic procedure; its theological content, however, is Qur'anic to the letter, since the record is expressly said to be capable of erasure and replacement: "God blots out what He wills and confirms, and with Him is the Mother of the Book" (13:39; cf. 25:70, "except those who repent, believe and do a new good — for them God replaces their evil deeds with good").

**Not Because God Needs the Report**

A reader may ask why scribes are necessary at all after verse 16. The sūrah answers by its own ordering, and the source's note records the same answer from al-Zamakhsharī (Z): God has appointed two receivers, yet He is not in need of them; the verse 17 is attached to 16 to teach that the divine knowledge continues to encompass both the agents and what they record. The Qur'an states the point directly: "Do they not think that We do not hear their secret counsel and their private talk? Yes indeed, and Our messengers are with them, writing" (43:80); "You are not in any affair, nor do you recite of it any recitation, nor do any people any deed, but that We are witness over them when they are engaged in it; and not hidden from your Lord is the weight of a dust-speck on earth or in heaven, nor anything smaller than that or greater, but it is in a clear register" (10:61). From these the commentators draw a distinction that matters for the whole topic of the record: God's *shuhūd* (witnessing) is immediate and total; the angels' *kitāba* (writing) is delegated and functional. Writing is the human-facing form of the divine knowledge, adapted to the way human beings need evidence, because a person can dispute with his own memory on the Day when memory is no help — and so the Book is produced, and the sūrah's own verse 18 will make the record cover *words*, the items a person most easily forgets and most readily excuses.

**The Four Directions and the One Missing Pair**

The seating on right and left should be set beside the verse where Shayṭān announces his own line of approach: "Then I will come at them from before them and from behind them, from their right and from their left, and You will not find most of them thankful" (7:17). The classical exegetes — al-Rāzī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī among them — note the omission: he does not say "from above them" or "from below them." Above is the direction of mercy, inspiration and the descent of the spirit ("the angels and the Spirit descend therein, by their Lord's leave, from every affair; peace it is, until the break of dawn" (97:4-5; cf. 26:193, "the trustworthy Spirit has brought it down upon your heart"), and below is the direction of humility, the dust from which the human was made and the ground on which the forehead is placed in prostration. Al-Rāzī's gloss is a whole counsel of practice in one line: the assaults come horizontally, from people, circumstances, past and future, while what comes to a human being vertically — from above and from below — is protection. And the two angels of our verse, one on each flank, answer the two flanks of Shayṭān's promised attack: the right, where he says he will come, is the very seat of the recorder of good.

**"Hāfiẓūn": Keeping You and Keeping Count**

The angels of this office are named elsewhere *ḥafaẓa* and *ḥāfiẓūn*: "For each one are successive [angels] before and behind, guarding by God's command" (13:11); "He sends over you guardians" (6:61); "And truly over you are guardians, noble recorders, who know what you do" (82:10-12). The root *ḥ-f-ẓ* is the same one used at verse 4 for the "preserving Book" (*kitābun ḥafīẓ*), and the double meaning is not incidental in Arabic: to *ḥafaẓa* is to guard something from being lost and to keep count of what it contains. The sūrah is describing one operation in two registers — a heaven that preserves, and servants who preserve, the person and his acts alike. The moral psychology follows at once: nothing you do will be lost to the universe, and nothing will be lost to you, because your own record will be handed to you when you need it most. That is a very different anthropology from the modern therapeutic one in which the past is dissolved by forgetting. Here the past is *stored*, and the difference turns on whether what you have done belongs to oblivion or to a mercy that keeps, since "He who created death and life that He might try which of you is best in deed" (67:2) and "whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it" (99:7-8).

**A Modern Analogy Worth Taking Seriously**

The nearest contemporary equivalent of *al-mutalaqqiyyān* is not a court but a log. A life now generates an archive its owner cannot read: searches, messages, locations, drafts, deleted items held by servers, everything timestamped and cross-indexed, so that a person's history is more knowable to institutions than to himself. The analogy should be pressed only so far and then dropped, because a log is inert and a record of this kind is alive, interpretive, and destined for a judgement that will explain it — and, as the tradition insists, that judgement is run by "the Most Merciful of the merciful," who "has prescribed mercy upon Himself" (6:12, 6:54) and whose prophet was sent "only as a mercy to the worlds" (21:107). The verse's real purpose is neither reassurance nor intimidation but the removal of an illusion: the illusion that an act can be performed and then be gone. Two receivers are set at the mouth for the same reason a notary is set at a contract — not because the parties have forgotten, but because the day of settlement will come, and the settlement requires a document that neither side can dispute.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:18

> **no word does he utter without a ready watcher beside him.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why Words, When Deeds Would Do**

*Mā yalfiẓu min qawlin illā ladayhi raqībun ʿatīd* — "he does not utter a word without having beside him a watcher, ready to hand." *Yalfiẓu* is from *lafẓ*, the casting out of what the mouth holds; the verb is physical, and it is the word a lexicographer would use for a man spitting out a date-stone. The verse could have said "no deed does he do," and it does not. Speech is picked out because it is the act human beings count least and regret most. The Qur'an elsewhere puts the same emphasis on the mouth as the organ of consequence: "When the two receivers sit receiving, one on the right and one on the left, a man does not utter a word without having a watcher ready" — our verse — and, in a parallel: "And do not follow that of which you have no knowledge; truly the hearing, the sight and the heart, all of those will be questioned about" (17:36); "and their mouths will speak to Us; their hands will speak, and their feet will testify to what they used to earn" (24:65; cf. 41:21, "until, when they come to it, their hearing, their sight and their skins testify against them to what they used to do," and 36:65, "that Day We seal over their mouths, and their hands speak to Us and their feet testify to what they used to earn").

The hadith literature makes the point concrete and frightening:

> "A man speaks a word that earns God's displeasure, thinking it of no account, and because of it he slides into the Fire farther than the distance between the East and the West." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, from Abū Hurayra)

And in its other form: "and truly a man speaks a word in which he sees no harm, and God raises him by it, in Paradise, a hundred degrees" — the pair is transmitted by Ibn Mājah and Aḥmad, and both halves are quoted by the exegetes here because our verse is about the neutral-seeming word: the one nobody thinks to repent of.

**"Raqīb": The Watcher, and What the Root Implies**

*Raqīb* is from *r-q-b*, to wait, to attend, to watch for; it is the root of the divine-attribute language of watchfulness and of the Sufi term *murāqaba* (see verse 16 above), and it names a vigilance that expects something — not a passive camera but an attention that waits for its object. *ʿAtīd* ("ready") completes the description: the watcher is already in position when the word begins. The same adjective is used in the Qur'an of the Fire as that which is prepared and awaiting: "a Fire whose fuel is people and stones, prepared (*uʿiddat*) for the wrongdoers" (2:24); and of the Hour itself, "they see it far off, while We see it near" (70:6-7). The two "readinesses" in one sūrah — the watcher ready at my mouth, and the Fire ready at the end of my choices — is the sūrah's own rhetoric of immediacy, and it is the reason verses 17-18 sit exactly between the claim "We are nearer to him than his jugular vein" (v. 16) and the arrival of "the stupor of death in truth" (v. 19). Nothing in this account of the human situation is distant; the language of nearness is what the sūrah is doing with its verbs.

**The Record Is of What You Say and Do, Not of Every Flicker**

An exegetical and pastoral balance is required here, and the tradition supplies it, so that verse 18 is not turned into a scrupulosity that paralyses. Verse 16 says God *knows* the whisper; verse 18 says the angel *records* the uttered word. The gap between the two is mercy, and the Prophet ﷺ put it in words:

> "God passes over in my community what is whispered in their selves, so long as they do not act upon it or speak it." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra)

And, in the ḥadīth qudsī that explains how the pen moves when a deed is only intended:

> "My servant intends a good deed and does not do it: God writes it for Him as one complete good deed. And if he intends it and does it, God writes it for him tenfold up to seven hundred, and multiplies it beyond that. And he intends an evil deed and does not do it: God writes it for him as a complete good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God writes it only as one evil deed." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

The commentators notice that this teaching matches our sūrah's own sequence: the near knowledge of verse 16 is total; the recording of verses 17-18 is deliberately restrained, weighted toward the good and slow toward the evil. It is also why al-Ṭabarī reports the view, transmitted also by Zayd ibn Aslam and quoted by the source's note, that the angel on the left is instructed to wait, in the hope that a bad deed will be cancelled by repentance before it is written. The system described in these verses is not a panopticon; it is an audit conducted by a judge who has already announced that mercy precedes the demand.

**The Two Angels' Daily Speech**

That angels are assigned to the affairs of a person, and speak about him, is established in a narration that the exegetes recite in this context:

> "No day dawns upon servants but that two angels descend, and one of them says: O God, give to the one who spends, compensation; and the other says: O God, give destruction to the one who withholds." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra)

The report is instructive for the way it frames our verses: the angels are not silent registrars but participants in the moral life of the person they attend, and their speech runs on the same axis as the record — the generous entry, the withholding entry. It is also a reminder that a being's fate is described in this Book in terms of what it *does with what it has been given*, a theme the sūrah has already raised with provision and the tithe (vv. 9-11) and will raise again at verse 35 ("therein they shall have whatever they will, and with Us there is more").

**The Ethics that Follow From a Kept Tongue**

The Prophet ﷺ turned the very idea of verse 18 into a rule of conversation, and his wording is the practical form of this verse:

> "Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him say good or be silent." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra)

The commentators of this sūrah quote that ḥadīth here because it converts the doctrine of the record into a decision procedure available in the half-second before speech: if the word is good, it is worth the entry; if it is not, silence keeps the page clean. In a tradition that also warns that "a Muslim is the one from whose tongue and hand other Muslims are safe" (Bukhārī and Muslim), the emphasis on speech is not asceticism for its own sake but a judgement about damage: of all the things a person utters each day, some wound people who will still be there tomorrow, and the record of those words will still be open on a day that has no tomorrow. This is the connection the sūrah of Qāf is making, and the same one Sūrat al-Ḥujurāt (49) had just made in the chapter before: mockery, suspicion, backbiting and name-calling are not small social manners but entries in a book — hence the pair of sūrahs, the community's ethics first (49), the individual's ledger here (50:17-18).

**The Watcher and the Self**

A final note on the psychology of the verse, because the objection is sometimes raised that such a doctrine produces a divided, anxious person, always performing. The tradition's own practice is the opposite, and the reason is that the watcher is described as *raqīb*, and *raqība* in Arabic carries the sense of attentive care, not of surveillance for the sake of catching. Read with verse 16, the pair of verses describes a being who is fully known and fully attended to; the scribe is not waiting to trip him but, in the reports above, waiting to see whether the sin will be withdrawn. And for the believer, the aim is not to have nothing in the record but to have a record that can be read without shame — because the happiness of that Day is described in the Qur'an not as a verdict survived but as a document recognised: "then as for one who is given his book in his right hand, he will say: Here, read my book!" (69:19) — and the heart that has lived before God has, in the end, nothing to hide even from itself: "the Day when neither wealth nor children will avail, but only one who comes to God with a sound heart" (26:88-89). When the verse says "no word does he utter but that there is beside him a ready watcher," the word beside him — *ladayhi* — is the word of proximity: the watcher is close enough to be trusted, and this sūrah, which ends by telling the Prophet ﷺ to remind and not to compel, assumes that a person convinced by verses 16-18 will do what compulsion could never achieve, and take hold of his own tongue.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:19

> **And the agony of death comes with the truth. That is what you were avoiding.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verb in the Past Tense for an Event Still to Come**

*Wa-jāʾat sakratu al-mawti bi-l-ḥaqq* — the clause is a perfect verb, and death has not yet arrived for the first hearers. Arabic uses the past tense of future events to express their certainty, and the rhetorical tradition calls the device *ḥukm al-wuqūʿ li-tahaqquqihi*, "judging something to have occurred because its occurrence is sure." The Qur'an does it with the Hour as a whole ("the Hour has drawn near, and the moon split," 54:1) and with the individual's end: "a Day when every soul will come arguing for itself, and every soul will be paid in full for what it did, and they will not be wronged" (16:111). The grammar is a theology: in the divine speech the future is not a region of probability but a place already visited. The same idiom will recur at verse 21 ("and every soul comes") and at verse 22 ("today your sight is piercing"), and it is why this section reads less like prediction than like reportage.

**Sakrah: The Drunkenness That No Wine Caused**

*Sakra* denotes intoxication, the covering of the intellect by something that takes it hostage; the source's note correctly links it to *sukr* and *sukrān*. The Qur'an uses the same word for the stupeur of the Day itself: "O mankind, fear your Lord: truly the shaking of the Hour is a tremendous thing. The Day you see it, every nursing mother will forget what she nursed, and every pregnant one will deliver her burden, and you will see the people as though drunk, though they are not drunk — but the punishment of God is severe" (22:1-2). Against that background, our phrase *sakratu al-mawt* is not a euphemism for pain but a description of an epistemic condition: the mind is overwhelmed, and the ordinary instruments of thought — planning, excuse, self-image, distraction — stop working. Al-Ṭabarī glosses it as "the severity and the confusion of death," and adds that it is a "sakrah" in the sense that the dying one is made unaware of what he thought he was.

Two consequences follow that the commentators draw out. First, the sūrah's own prescription of prayer (vv. 39-40) belongs in this frame: a worshipper is forbidden to approach prayer in a state of intoxication ("do not approach prayer while you are drunk (*sukrān*) until you know what you say," 4:43), and the stupor of death is the one intoxication from which no one will recover to know what he said. The whole sūrah's devotional instruction is, in effect, training for sobriety in advance. Second, the *sakrah* is a *transition*, not an annihilation: something that intoxicates presupposes a subject who continues to exist while the instrument is disabled — which is precisely why the sūrah can put the next words in the mouth of the dying scene.

**"With the Truth": Three Glosses, One Point**

*Bil-ḥaqq* is read in three ways, all preserved in the source's note. (a) "In reality, in truth": what death brings is the real, not the imagined; the metaphor of the sūrah's earlier *kadhdhabū bi-l-ḥaqq* (v. 5) returns — the truth they denied as a message is now met as a condition. (b) "By God's decree": al-Zamakhsharī takes it as "through the truth of the affair," i.e. what God had told them would happen; the verse is then a divine statement of fact, not a threat. (c) "With God": since *al-Ḥaqq* is among the names, the phrase means "the stupor of death comes with the Truth Himself present" — a reading the mystical commentators favour and which the source records from al-Ṭabarī. Al-Rāzī in his commentary lists them all without preferring among them, and the plurality serves the verse: what arrives at death is the real, the decreed, and the near God, at once.

Ibn Zayd, quoted by al-Ṭabarī, adds a note of mercy to the same phrase: the stupor is "with the truth" in that it *clarifies*, the way a crisis clarifies for a family what years of comfort had concealed. A death-bed, in that reading, is not only an ending but an unveiling: "the cover is removed" (v. 22) belongs to the same image-cluster, and the two verses should be read as one scene.

**"That Is What You Were Avoiding": The Far Thing at Hand**

*dhālika alladhī minhu kun tum tuḥaddūn* — the verb is read two ways, and both are recorded by the exegetes: from *al-waḥīd*, dread and flight ("that from which you used to shrink and flee"), and from the root of threatening ("that with which you used to be threatened"). That the two readings coexist is no accident of transmission: in the sūrah's moral world the threat and the object of flight are identical, as the four uses of *waʿīd* in this chapter keep demonstrating (vv. 14, 20, 28, 45). The rejectors had called the return *baʿīd*, "far off" (v. 3); verse 19 reports its arrival, and the sūrah lets the distance collapse without argument. The rhetorical structure is a mirror: v. 2 marvelled at a warner "from among them"; v. 3 pronounced the return far; v. 19 announces that what was pronounced far is now the speaker's subject.

That the "man" of verse 16 — *al-insān*, human being in general — is the one addressed here is important for the sūrah's design. The section beginning at verse 16 has been running an argument with the Quraysh, and at verse 19 the argument stops, because the case is no longer arguable: an interlocutor in the stupor of death is the last person on earth who can dispute. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the same point with its most quoted sentence on the subject: "Say: Truly the death from which you flee will certainly overtake you; then you will be returned to the Knower of the unseen and the witnessed, and He will tell you what you used to do" (62:7). And: "Wherever you are, death will overtake you, though you be in towers raised and fortified" (4:78).

**What the Moment Is Like: Two Reports the Tradition Preserves**

The sūrah describes; the ḥadīth literature narrates, and the two are usually read side by side in the commentaries at this verse. Of the direction of the soul's last movement, al-Bukhārī and Muslim transmit from ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit:

> "Whoever loves to meet God, God loves to meet him; and whoever hates to meet God, God hates to meet him." ʿĀʾisha — or some of his wives — said: "O Messenger of God, do we not all hate death?" He said: "That is not the case. Rather, when the believer is given the good news of God's pleasure and His honour, his soul is at rest and he loves to meet God and God loves to meet him; and when the sinner is given the good news of God's punishment and His anger, his soul is in anguish and he hates to meet God, and God hates to meet him." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim)

The ḥadīth is a commentary on our verse in the strictest sense: the *sakrah* is one, its quality two, and the difference between them is not the absence of pain but the presence or absence of welcome. The long narration of al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib, transmitted by Aḥmad and Abū Dāwūd and judged *ḥasan* by a number of critics, elaborates the same contrast in images: the soul of the believer drawn out "like a drop flowing from a waterskin's mouth," and the soul of the disbeliever torn out so that "every vein and nerve follows it." Such narratives should be read as the tradition's way of making the *sakrah* imaginable rather than as anatomical claims, and their theological content is exactly our verse: death is a *meeting*, not a cessation.

**The Verse as the Pivot of the Whole Sūrah**

Everything in Qāf turns on verse 19. Verses 16-18 described the machinery that runs during life — nearness, whisperings known, words recorded — and verses 20-30 describe the court that follows; verse 19 is the door between them, and the door is not an event in the future but the one certainty inside the present. The consequence for the reader is what the sūrah's verb *tuḥaddūn* exposes: the whole of the disbelief examined in verses 2-15 had, at its base, not an argument but an avoidance. It is possible to find a case against resurrection unanswerable and to be simply unwilling to arrive; and verse 19 says that the arriving is not optional.

This is also why the sūrah will refuse coercion at its end (v. 45): no one can be compelled toward an event that will compel him anyway. And it is the reason the Prophet ﷺ's own counsel on the subject is practical rather than morbid:

> "Be in this world as though you were a stranger or a traveller crossing it." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, from Ibn ʿUmar)

The companion ḥadīth quoted by the same collections — "the grave is either a garden of the gardens of Paradise or a pit of the pits of Hell" (Sunan al-Tirmidhī, from Anas; its chain is debated though its meaning is widely received) — belongs to the same teaching: the *sakrah* is followed at once by consequences, so the meaningful question is not "will I die?" but "what will I be doing when I do?" It is that question the sūrah has been asking since the first letter.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:20

> **And the trumpet is blown. That is the Day of the Threat.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Single Death to the Common Summons**

The verse of death is immediately followed by a verse of the horn: *wa-nufikha fī al-ṣūri dhālika yawmu al-waʿīd*. The juxtaposition compresses eschatology into two sentences — each person dies alone (v. 19), and all are called at once (v. 20) — and the compression is the sūrah's answer to a particular modern and ancient mistake: treating death as a private catastrophe and the resurrection as a public myth. In this sūrah's logic they are one sequence: the private passage leads to the summons, and the summons is nothing other than what was said in the private passage.

The passive verb, *nufikha*, "there was blown," is used without naming either the blower or the instrument's owner, and the exegetes treat that as deliberate: the agent is God, the actor a servant, and the point is not the mechanics but the fact of a call that cannot be missed.

**The Two Blasts, and the Vocabulary of Alarm**

The Qur'an speaks of the trumpet (*ṣūr*) in some ten places, and its descriptions make clear that the blowing is twice repeated, the second blast being the summons itself: "And the trumpet is blown, and all who are in the heavens and all who are on the earth fall senseless, except whom God wills; then it is blown again, and at once they stand, looking about" (39:68). To the second blowing the sūrah attaches the standing for judgement: "the Day He blows the trumpet, We gather the guilty on that Day, blue-eyed" (20:102); "the trumpet is blown, and at once they come from their graves to their Lord, hastening" (36:51); "His is the dominion on the Day the trumpet is blown — the Knower of the unseen and the witnessed, the Wise, the Aware" (6:73); "there is no tie of kinship between them on that Day, and they will not ask about one another" (23:101); "on the Day the trumpet is blown you come forth in companies" (78:18); "it is but a single blast, and at once they are on the open plain" (79:13-14); and "when the trumpet is blown, that Day will be a day of distress, for the disbelievers, not easy at all" (74:8-10). The two blasts of 39:68 are also the frame of a ḥadīth of the trumpet's blower transmitted by al-Ḥākim and graded sound by him, which the commentators quote here:

> "How can I take my ease, when the one who bears the horn has pressed it to his lips, bowed his forehead, and listens for permission to blow?" (al-Ḥākim in his *Mustadrak*, from Abū Hurayra; he graded it *ṣaḥīḥ* and al-Dhahabī concurred)

The sūrah uses the other words of alarm as well, and the collection of them is instructive: the *ṣayḥa*, the single cry that "seizes the wrongdoers" (11:67; "We sent upon them a single shriek," 54:31); the *qāriʿa*, "the Striker," which gives a whole sūrah its name (101:1-3); the *ṭāmma al-kubrā*, "the overwhelming Great One" (79:34); the *rājifa* that "shakes," followed by the *rādi fa* that pursues (79:6-7). The variety is not redundancy: each word isolates one feature — suddenness, totality, irreversibility, pursuit — and together they describe an event no one outruns.

**Who Blows, and What the Tradition Will Not Guess**

Popular retelling names the angel Isrāfīl. The name does not occur in the Qur'an, and no sound text establishes it. Ibn Kathīr states the rule that governs such matters: the names of the angels are received from the texts and not from inference, and a name found only in weak reports or in *Isrāʾīliyyāt* is neither affirmed as doctrine nor treated as a denial. That is why the sound exegesis of verse 20 has nothing to say about the identity of the blower. Its silence is a method: the sūrah's point is the summons, and speculation about the summoner's staff would dilute it. (The same discipline explains why the Qur'an leaves "the preserving Book" of verse 4 and "the thicket" of verse 14 in a certain opacity: the text supplies what is needed for guidance and withholds what is not.)

**"The Day of the Threat": The Fourth and Sharpest Occurrence**

*Yawmu al-waʿīd* — the fourth use of the chapter's title-word. At verse 14 the threat "came due" upon the nations, and it was past; here the threat has a *Day*, and it is future; at verse 28 the accused will be told that the warning had already been delivered; and at verse 45 the reminder is addressed to "whoever fears My threat." The four uses trace a single argument across the sūrah: a threat is only a threat if it has (1) a spoken warning, (2) a warner who delivers it, (3) a time at which it is executed, and (4) a hearer capable of fear. Verse 20 supplies (3), and it is the piece the Quraysh were prepared to laugh at.

Note also the naming style: the Day is labelled by what it contains, in a family of phrases the Qur'an uses repeatedly — "the Day of Judgement" (82:17-19; 82:18, "and what will convey to you what the Day of Judgement is"), "the Day of Distinction" (*yawm al-faṣl*, 44:40; 77:13), "the Day of Account" (14:41), "the Day of Recompense" (37:25-26), "the Day of Gathering" (64:9), "the Day of Coming Forth" (50:42), and "the Day the caller calls from a near place" (50:41). To name the Day by its function is to teach that its terror is not arbitrary violence but procedure: an audit, a separation, an accounting — words the sūrah can use because it has already established the record of verses 17-18 and the preserving Book of verse 4.

**Easy for Us, Hard for Them**

The sūrah will say, twenty-four verses later, of the selfsame event: "That is a gathering easy for Us" (v. 44), and of the same Day another sūrah says: "That Day will be a day of distress, for the disbelievers, not easy" (74:9-10). The pair is the sūrah's whole theodicy in miniature, and it disposes of the standard modern objection that an ordeal of the whole species must be a strain upon a merciful or omnipotent God. Difficulty, on the Qur'an's account, is a property of the accountable, not of the Agent: the Day is heavy in proportion to what a person has to answer, and weightless for the One who does not depend on evidence. The reader is therefore not being invited to imagine a cosmic event but to estimate his own readiness — and the ḥadīth that the sūrah's preachers attach to these verses makes the calculation explicit:

> "The intelligent person is the one who calls himself to account and works for what is after death; the helpless one is the one who follows his own desire and wishes of God unfulfilled hopes." (Sunan al-Tirmidhī and Sunan Ibn Mājah, from Shaddād ibn Aws; graded *ḥasan*)

**The Blowing and the Business of the Hour**

Finally, it is characteristic of the Qur'an — and of this sūrah — that the trumpet's introduction is followed not by description of the music of the spheres but by the arrival of a driver and a witness for each individual (v. 21). The eschatology is personal at once. A sermon delivered on a Friday from a minbar, as Muslim records the Prophet ﷺ delivering this very sūrah, has exactly one thing to accomplish in a hearer: to make the summons feel addressed. Verse 20 accomplishes it by removing the interval. Between the stupor of verse 19 and the blast of verse 20 there is no paragraph, no respite, and no second chance of the kind verse 19's hearers had been counting on: "and when death appears to one of them, he says: My Lord, send me back, that I may do right in what I have left — no indeed! It is a word he will merely say, and behind them is a barrier until the Day they are raised" (23:99-100).

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:21

> **Then every soul comes, with it a driver and a witness:**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Escort Already Assigned**

*Wa-jāʾat kullu nafsin sāʾiquhā wa-shahīduhā* — "and every soul comes; its driver and its witness are with it." The Arabic is a nominal sentence of accompaniment rather than a verbal clause of action: the two attendants are not described as arriving but as *already attached*, the possessive suffixes making them each soul's own. The construction is the sūrah's quiet stroke: a person does not walk into judgement alone, nor is he met there for the first time by the record — the escort has been with him throughout, and its arrival is only the end of a journey the individual has been unconscious of making. Al-Ṭabarī explains: "every soul comes, and with it an angel who drives it to the place of standing, and an angel who bears witness against it for what God commanded him to testify to."

The pairing *sāʾiq wa-shāhid* has a juridical ring, and that is the point: the driver is the one who executes a summons, the witness is the one who produces evidence. A defendant in a court that has both has no procedural room left, and the sūrah has already closed the substantive door — the "preserving Book" of verse 4 and the watchers of verses 17-18 mean that the testimony is documentary rather than contested.

**"Every Soul": The Sentence without Privilege**

*Kullu nafsin* is the most important two words of the verse, and the sūrah's audience would have heard them as a social statement. A tribe whose order was built on lineage and on the honour of the clan is told that what comes is *every* soul, and that each is accompanied by an escort assigned to it singly. No delegation, no intercession arranged in advance, no family name to stand in for the individual; the Qur'an insists on the same point at the Day's other descriptions: "and no bearer of burdens will bear the burden of another" (6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38), "every soul will be paid in full for what it did" (3:25; 52:21), "you will be brought forth, one by one, as We created you the first time" (18:48; 6:94). The equality of the scene — a pharaoh and a shepherd driven and witnessed in exactly the same manner — is not a rhetorical decoration but one of the Qur'an's structural claims about the world to come.

Some of the commentators, however, record a restriction: al-Ṭabarī preserves the view that "every soul comes, its driver and witness" refers specifically to the souls of the disbelievers, on the ground that a driver implies compulsion and a witness implies accusation. The source's note records this, and the majority answer that the verse is general and its tone determined by the context that follows: verses 22-26 speak of the disbeliever, and verses 31-35 of the believer, each receiving what v. 21 announces. The plain reading is the sound one: everyone arrives escorted, and what the escort testifies to differs.

**Who the Witness Is**

The candidates offered by the exegetes are four, and each is anchored by another text. (a) An angel: as in verse 23, where "his companion" presents the file. (b) The self: "and your Lord is not wronging the servants" (41:46), with 17:14 — "Today your own self is enough as a reckoner against you." (c) The prophets: "How will it be when We bring from every community a witness, and bring you as a witness over these?" (4:41; 16:89). (d) The limbs and the skin: "until, when they arrive at it, their hearing, their sights and their skins testify against them about what they used to do" (41:21; 24:24; 41:23). The sūrah's own later verses keep the ambiguity alive: "this is what I have ready" (v. 23) presents a companion with a record, and "on that Day, your self suffices as a reckoner" belongs to the reckoning's own vocabulary (17:13-14). The lesson the plurality of readings teaches is the same one the verse itself presses: the evidence will be comprehensive and unimpeachable, whoever delivers it.

**"A Driver": What Compulsion at the End of Freedom Means**

The existence of a driver raises a question that the sūrah itself has prepared, and that Muslim theology took seriously: if a soul is *driven*, where has the freedom gone that made it liable in the first place? The answer is embedded in the verse's tense structure. Compulsion begins after the verdict, not before the deed. In this life the human being is addressed as one who chooses ("so remind by the Qur'an whoever fears My threat," v. 45, precisely because he may or may not fear), and the record of verses 17-18 is a record of what he said and willed; the driver at verse 21 is the consequence, and a consequence that a person chose is not a contradiction of choice but its ratification. The parables the sūrah uses elsewhere show the same logic: "and when they are thrown into it, they will hear it breathing as it boils, almost bursting with rage" (67:7-8); "the Day when the hypocrites, men and women, will say to those who believed: Look at us, that we may take from your light — it will be said: Go back and seek light" (57:13). Nobody is driven anywhere the door of which was locked from the inside.

There is a pastoral corollary that the devotional tradition drew from this verse, and it is not in the direction of despair. The report of *sāʾiq* belongs to the Day; in this world the same angels of verse 17 are described as guardians and as intercessors for the servant's good: "For him are angels in succession before him and behind him, guarding him by God's command" (13:11), and the hadith of the angels who pray for the one who waits for prayer: "The angels keep asking God's forgiveness for one of you, as long as he remains in his place of prayer, saying: O God, forgive him; O God, have mercy on him" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra). The escort that will one day drive is the same company that today prays.

**The Footsteps That Will Not Move Until**

The commentators close the discussion of verse 21 with a ḥadīth that supplies the agenda of the interrogation the driver has come to enforce:

> "The feet of the son of Adam will not move on the Day of Resurrection until he is asked about five things: about his life — how he spent it; his youth — how he consumed it; his wealth — where he earned it and where he spent it; and what he learned — did he act upon it?" (Sunan al-Tirmidhī, from Abū Barza al-Aslamī; graded *ḥasan*, and its wording appears in other collections with additions)

Read in the sūrah's own order, the five items correspond exactly to what the chapter has been describing: a life (vv. 16-18), a body and its strength (vv. 3, 6-11, 36), provision (vv. 9-11), and knowledge received as reminder (vv. 8, 37, 45). The ḥadīth is often cited as a summary of this sūrah's subject matter, and the fit is close enough that the preachers who used Qāf on Fridays could deliver the entire syllabus of the Day from a single verse.

**Why This Verse Is the Sūrah's Own Warning to Its Reader**

Verse 21 has no vocative, no command and no threat: an announcement in the plainest narrative voice. That restraint is its force. The sūrah has spent four verses making the reader look at the sky, the earth, the water and the palm; four more at the nations who said no; and now it says simply: every soul arrives, escorted and witnessed. There is no escape in numbers, no adjournment, and no anonymity — and, since the driver and the witness are *sāʾiquhā wa-shahīduhā*, "its" driver and "its" witness, there is no substitute. The next three verses will record the first words spoken in that scene, and they are words of exposure: "You were heedless of this" (v. 22).

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:22

> **"You were indeed heedless of this. Now We have removed from you your cover; so today your sight is piercing."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Sentences, One Operation**

The verse is built as a before, a now, and an after. *La-qad kunta fī ghaflatin min hādhā* — "you were, indeed, in heedlessness of this"; *fa-kashafnā ʿanka ghithāʾak* — "so We have lifted from you your cover"; *fa-baṣaruka al-yawma ḥadīd* — "so today your sight is sharp." The three verbs fix the human condition in a single sequence: a person lives screened, the screen is removed, and what he sees cannot be unseen. The sūrah has said nothing of punishment in this clause. It has simply described a change of visibility, and the horror and the relief of the scene are both contained in that.

*Ghafla* is the Qur'an's technical term for the state of the unbeliever that is not unbelief: the failure to attend. "The reckoning for mankind has drawn near, yet they are in heedlessness, turning away" (21:1); "they know only an outward thing of the life of this world, while of the Hereafter they are heedless" (30:7); and, most devastatingly: "We have destined for Hell many of the jinn and mankind: they have hearts with which they do not understand, eyes with which they do not see, and ears with which they do not hear; they are like cattle — nay, they are further astray. Those are the heedless" (7:179). Against that last verse, our verse is its reversal: the heedless are given, at the last, exactly the sight, hearing and understanding they declined.

**"The Cover": What the Veil Is Made Of**

*Ghiṭāʾ* is a covering that is wrapped over something — from *ghaṭā*, to cover, and the form *fiʿāl* often names the thing that does the covering (as *likām*, a binding). The Qur'an uses the image for the opacity God creates over hearts that have persisted: "and We place over their hearts coverings so that they do not understand it, and over their ears a heaviness" (6:25); "they said: our hearts are in coverings from what you call us to, and in our ears a heaviness" (41:5); and it uses the same verb for the world's own seductiveness: "the life of this world is nothing but a delight of delusion" (3:185; 57:20). From these the commentators draw the verse's implication: the cover was not an obstacle placed in front of an eager seeker, but the very element the seeker chose. What is lifted, at the end, is not a veil from his eyes but the world he had been looking *through*.

This is why the source's note records the mystical reading attached to the verse, and why it is not an importation but an extraction. Ibn ʿArabī, on this verse in the *Fuṭūḥāt al-Makkīyya* (III 388): "the believer who is granted the unveiling of things as they are is granted the return from the many to the One… and this is before he leaves this world." Mullā Ṣadrā, in his philosophical commentary on Sūrah Yā-Sīn quoted by the source, makes the same claim about the soul's faculties becoming "powerful and piercing" through its perception of the next world. Their inference from the wording is sound as an argument: if the *ghafla* is a disposition and the *ghitāʾ* is a covering that can be removed *from* a person (*ʿanka*), then removal is not necessarily tied to biological death, and a purified soul may see now what the dying man will be made to see then. The classical orthodoxy accepts the possibility while insisting on its limits: unveiling in this life is granted, not achieved; it confirms faith rather than replacing it; and it does not suspend accountability, which is exactly why the sūrah's next words are a sentence and not an invitation.

**"Your Sight Is Piercing": The Word for Iron**

*Ḥadīd* is "iron," and as an adjective of the eye it means sharp, cutting, unblunted: "so today your sight is iron." The Qur'an uses the same root once more in a way the exegetes set beside this verse: "We sent Our messengers with clear proofs, and sent down with them the Book and the *ḥadīd* [iron], mighty in war and containing benefit for people, so that God may know who helps Him and His messengers unseen" (57:25) — a pairing of the two "sharps": the instrument that cuts in the world and the sight that cuts at the end.

What the sharpened sight sees is specified elsewhere: "They see it far off, while We see it near" (70:6); "on that Day man will say: where is the flight?" (75:10); "that Day every soul will find the good it did present before it, and the evil it did: it will wish that a long span lay between it and that" (3:30). The vision of the verse is therefore not neutral perception but appraisal: a person sees, at last, the shape of his own life, and the seeing is itself part of the consequence. The sūrah's earlier words about the sky and the earth (vv. 6-11) used *baṣīrah* for the insight available in this life; here *baṣar* is raw and total, and there is no interpretation left to dispute.

**The Hadith That Makes the Verse Pastoral Rather Than Only Terrifying**

The tradition reads this lifting-of-the-cover in two directions at once, and the second is a mercy. That the world is the veil is stated in a ḥadīth whose phrasing our verse almost glosses:

> "The world is a prison for the believer and a paradise for the disbeliever." (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra)

For the believer the world restrains — the very *ghafla* he is told he was in is what his faith kept from becoming his whole mind — so the removal of the covering is release; for the disbeliever the world is the paradise, and its removal is loss. Al-Qurṭubī cites the same ḥadīth here to show that the verse's tone is not uniform: "today your sight is piercing" is good news for one and bad for the other, and the sūrah will make the two endings explicit in the pair of scenes that follow (vv. 24-26 and 31-35).

There is also a ḥadīth that gives the eye itself a history with God, and it is quoted by the preachers of this verse:

> "Two eyes that the Fire will not touch: an eye that wept from the fear of God, and an eye that stayed awake in God's path." (Sunan al-Tirmidhī, from Ibn ʿAbbās; graded *ḥasan*)

The pairing matters for the sūrah's argument about *looking*. Qāf has been asking its hearers to look — at the sky, at the earth, at the palm, at the ruined camps of the past — and the tradition's answer is that the eye is the organ whose fate is decided by what it chooses to see and by what makes it weep. The eye that weeps has already seen through the cover; the eye that stands watch has already refused the sleep the verse calls *ghafla*.

**An Objection Worth Meeting**

A reader may feel the verse is unfair: how can a person be blamed for what was hidden from him, and then have the hidden thing shown to him at the moment when seeing no longer changes anything? The answer the sūrah gives is spread across its own structure, and it is worth assembling. (1) The cover was not opaque to him: the sky without rifts, the earth with its pairs, the revived land, "a reminder for every penitent servant" (vv. 6-11) — the veil is described in this chapter as thin, and the sūrah's accusation is not that they were blinded but that they were *heedless* (v. 22) and "marvelling" (v. 2) instead of looking. (2) The removal happens at a moment whose character is chosen by the person: the report cited above distinguishes the believer's ease from the sinner's anguish at the very same event, so the "too late" is self-inflicted. (3) And the sūrah repeatedly denies that the situation is irreversible *before* that moment: "Say: O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of God's mercy; truly God forgives all sins — He is the Forgiving, the Merciful" (39:53), and verse 32 of this chapter reserves the Garden for the *awwāb*, the "oft-returning," which is a category of person defined by returning *late* and still being accepted.

**Reading Verse 22 as a Present Tense**

The most durable use of this verse in Muslim piety is the least dramatic: a tool for honesty now. If the cover is one's own inattention, then the discipline of the believer consists in lifting what can be lifted in this life — by remembrance, by looking, by reading the chapter's own exhibits in order — and the sūrah's promise is that sight gained this way is not wasted: it is the same sight, sharpened. That is the logic of the verse's place in a Friday sermon, in which the hearer sits in the very state the verse describes and is told, in the same breath, that the state is chosen and that the lifting has begun. When the Prophet ﷺ recited this chapter from the minbar, the line about the cover removed was not an eschatological report about somebody else; it was an instruction about what the hour of hearing was for.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:23

> **And his companion says, "This is what I have ready."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Attendant Who Has Kept the File**

*Wa-qāla qarīnuhu hādhā ladayya ʿatīd* — "and his companion says: this, with me, is ready." *Qarīn* is from *q-r-n*, the tie that binds two things together, and it names a companion in the sense of one assigned to a person, inseparable from him: the same word describes the jinn attendant in the sūrah of the Cave — "and his companion (*qārīnahu*) said to him, in the midst of the discussion: 'I do not think that this will ever perish'" (18:34-37; the word occurs there in the form *qārīn*) — and, in the language of the ḥadīth, the constant attendant of every human being:

> "There is no one of you but that God has appointed for him a companion (*qarīn*) from among the jinn and a companion from among the angels." They said: And you, Messenger of God? He said: And me, except that God helped me against him, so he became Muslim: he does not command me but what is right. (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd)

The identity of the speaker in our verse is debated, and the three candidates the source's note records are all defensible: the angel appointed to record (al-Ṭabarī, al-Jūzbajānī), the driver of verse 21 (also al-Ṭabarī), or the jinn companion (al-Zamakhsharī cites 43:36: "whoever turns away blind from the remembrance of the Merciful, We assign to him a devil, and he becomes a companion (*qarīn*) to him"). On the second reading the verse belongs to the disbeliever's scene only, since the evil companion's presentation is an accusation; on the first it belongs to every person, because the file is every person's. The sūrah itself tips the balance toward the general reading, since verse 21 said *every* soul comes with a driver and a witness, and verse 27 will give the companion's next speech — "I did not make him rebel" — which only makes sense of an attendant who was present with a purpose, not of a prosecuting devil.

**"This Is What I Have Ready": The Rhetoric of a Document**

The speech is four words long, and its economy is its terror. The speaker offers no argument, no emotion, no character assessment: "here it is, ready." The Arabic *ʿatīd* is the same adjective used at verse 18 for the watcher "ready" beside the mouth, and the echo is not accidental: what was *at hand* in life is *at hand* in court. The record has been kept up to date, and the file is therefore not reconstructed at judgement; it is produced. The Qur'an says of that Day: "truly over you are guardians, noble scribes, who know what you do" (82:10-12); and, most exactly, "the Day We fold the heaven like the rolling of a scroll: as We began the first creation, so We will bring it about — a promise upon Us; We are indeed to do it" (21:104), so that the resumption of the record and the resumption of creation are one act.

That the witness produces a document, rather than a memory, addresses a problem the sūrah has been circling since verse 4. A person disputes with his own past: he forgets, minimises, re-narrates, and loses the motive of his own worst actions in the fog of circumstance ("rather, he was far astray," v. 27, is exactly such a re-narration, and the verse will reject it). A record answers the re-narrator. The Qur'an states the principle once and for all in the verse the preachers pair with ours: "We shall write what they say and the killing of the prophets without right, and We will say: Taste the punishment of the Blaze — that is what your hands sent ahead, for God is not a wrongdoer to the servants" (3:181; cf. 22:10, "that is because of what your hands have sent ahead, and because God is not a wrongdoer to the servants").

**The Scene as the Sūrah's Court**

Read with verses 21, 24 and 27, verse 23 sets the courtroom: the accused arrives escorted (v. 21), the exhibit is presented (v. 23), the sentence is pronounced (v. 24), and the defence is heard and rejected (v. 27, 28). Verse 23 thus supplies the procedural step that makes the rest just: no one is condemned before his file is read out. That is why the sūrah can, in the very same passage, insist that God does not wrong anyone (v. 29). The Qur'an's eschatology is repeatedly described in the language of law rather than of vengeance — the "book," the "witness," the "measurement," the "questioning" — and the sequence of verses 21-29 is the clearest single instance: a summons, an exhibit, a sentence, a plea, and a ruling that the Word with God does not change.

**The Terrible Kindness of a "Ready" File**

One more point about *ʿatīd*: the record's readiness is also an invitation, in the sūrah's own terms, because the same file can be lighter than its owner fears. The Qur'an promises that on the Day "as for one who is given his book in his right hand — he will say: Here! Read my book!" (69:19), and the hadith literature preserves the reason a file can be light: a man whose sins filled the pages was saved by a single card of testimony. In the narration of the man of nine hundred and ninety-nine records:

> "A man will be brought on the Day of Resurrection holding nine hundred and ninety-nine scrolls, each of them as far as the eye can see, and he will be told: do you deny any of this? … And there will be with him a card containing the testimony that there is no god but God, and he will say: what is this card doing here? And it will be said: you will not be wronged today. So his scrolls are put on one side of the scale and the card on the other, and the scrolls are light and the card heavy." (Sunan al-Tirmidhī no. 2640, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr; al-Tirmidhī called it *gharīb* and al-Ḥākim graded it *ṣaḥīḥ*, al-Dhahabī concurring)

Exegetes quote such reports at verse 23 for a reason: the companion who presents the file is not an adversary trying to win, but an officer of a court whose Judge has already declared that mercy is His own chosen disposition ("My mercy encompasses all things," 7:156; "He has prescribed mercy upon Himself," 6:12) and that the scale runs in the sinner's favour when he is sincere ("whoever brings a good deed shall have ten times the like thereof," 6:160). The lesson is the practical one the sūrah will press home in verse 39: attend to what you say, because it is being set down, and set down for you.

**An Ethical Afterthought About Our Own Records**

Verse 23 has a secondary use in Islamic ethics, in that the tradition applied the phrase *hādhā ladayya ʿatīd* to the discipline of self-examination before the divine one: "O you who believe, fear God, and let every soul look at what it has sent ahead for tomorrow" (59:18). The verb in that verse, *tanẓur*, is the same looking the sūrah of Qāf has been teaching (vv. 6, 8, 22), and the object is not the future but the past — what one has *sent*. An accountant does not discover his figures at the audit; he prepares for it. The verse's companion report in the hadith literature is the well-known: "Take account of yourselves before you are taken to account, and weigh what you have to offer" — a saying of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb recorded by al-Tirmidhī in his *Sunan* with a chain the hadith masters discussed, and quoted by the commentators here because it converts the file into a daily practice.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:24

> **"Cast you both into Hell every stubborn disbeliever,"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence Addressed to Two**

*Iṭraḥā māni kulli ʿanīdin kafīr* — the verb is dual: "cast, you two," and the exegetes ask why, since the one thrown in would seem to be a single person. Al-Ṭabarī's answer, adopted by most, is that the address is to the two angels of verse 21, driver and witness, or to the pair of executioners; another answer is that "both" refers to the man and his companion of verse 23, the *qarīn* whose file is what condemns him, so that the pair is thrown in together. The Qur'an elsewhere confirms the second sense explicitly: "and We will join together the evildoers with their counterparts (*azwājihim*) and whatever they worshipped besides God, and We will guide them to the path of the Hell, and stop them, for they are to be questioned" (37:23-24); and "We gather the guilty on that Day, blue-eyed" (20:102). The pairing itself is an eschatological event: "and when the souls are paired, and when the girl-child buried alive is asked for what sin she was killed, and when the scrolls are brought forth" (81:7-10) — and the Qur'an's other image of that same pairing is the reversal of friendship itself: "intimate friends on that Day will be enemies to one another, except the reverent" (43:67).

The duality is also a theological point in miniature: nobody goes to the Fire alone in the sense of being unsupported — the same *qarīn* who whispered (v. 16's register) testifies (v. 23) and shares the sentence. The one who made the companion his authority finds the companion a co-defendant.

**"ʿAnīd": The Sin of the Crooked Neck**

*ʿAnīd* comes from *ʿanada*, to turn one's neck away in pride and thereby turn the whole person; *ʿanūd* in Arabic is a beast that will not come to the trough because its head is set, and *muʿānid* is one who sets his face against you and will not be brought round. The lexicographers are agreed that the word implies not ignorance but obstinacy after knowledge — al-Farrāʾ and Ibn Qutaybah gloss "the one who knows the truth and turns away from it." Al-Ṭabarī: "the stubborn one who turns away from God's obedience." The choice of this adjective first, before *kāfir*, is the sūrah's diagnosis of its own audience: the Makkan refusal, as verses 2-5 recorded, was marvel and confusion, not inquiry. The Qur'an uses the cognate verb of the very people of the Book who "knew it as they know their own sons" yet concealed it (2:146, 6:20), and of those whose refusal is a deception they conduct against themselves: "they say: we believe in God and the Last Day, but they are not believing; they seek to deceive God and those who believe, and they deceive none but themselves, though they do not perceive it" (2:8-9). *ʿInād* is therefore a moral category, not an epistemic one, and it is the one the sūrah puts at the head of the list of the damned.

**"Kāfir": The One Who Buries What Was Sown**

*Kāfir* is from *k-f-r*, to cover, to strip away, and the same root names the farmer, who covers the seed with soil, in a verse the exegetes cite for the etymology: "like rain whose growth delights the sowers (*al-kuffār*)" (57:20). The Qur'an's use of the word for disbelief is therefore not primarily about denial of facts but about the covering of what has been made evident: the sky with no rifts, the earth with its pairs, the land revived — all of that is the seed the disbeliever buries. The pairing of *ʿanīd* and *kāfir* is thus a single portrait seen twice: the inward turning away (the neck) and the outward operation upon the truth (the cover). And the Day answers in kind: the cover that was chosen is removed (v. 22), and what was buried is brought forward, ready (v. 23).

**The Six Qualities, and Why the List Is Ordered**

Verses 24-26, which the sūrah itself binds into one sentence (as the source's grouping of them shows), enumerate six traits: *ʿanīd* (stubborn), *kāfir* (covering), *mannaʿ li-l-khayr* (hindering the good), *muʿtad* (transgressing), *murīb* (doubting, throwing into doubt), and the one who "sets up another god with God." The order is not a list of synonyms but an analysis: a disposition (stubbornness), a theological stance (covering the truth), a social obstruction (withholding good), an active aggression (transgression), a communicative corruption (spreading doubt), and finally the root sin of association. The progression from the inner refusal to the outer harm is exactly what the sūrah has been doing to its own audience, verse by verse: marvel (v. 2) → denial (v. 5) → the demand that the truth be reassigned to a "great man" (43:31) → the killing of the message by making it "marvellous" → and now the sentence. Al-Rāzī notes that the last item, association, is named in a clause of its own with its own punishment — "cast him into the severe punishment" (v. 26) — because it is the one that adds nothing to the world's sum of harm except the corruption of the human being's direction: the person who mis-aims at the level of God will be able to justify every other item on the list.

**A Note on "the Good" That Is Hindered**

The source's note explains *mannaʿ li-l-khayr* in the widest terms the exegetes used: al-Ṭabarī reports that it means withholding what God and people are owed — the obligatory alms on the divine side, the honouring of contracts and fair dealing on the human side. Ibn Kathīr cites, in this connection, the sūrah of al-Duhā's great social catalogue: "Do you see the one who denies the Recompense? That is the one who drives away the orphan and does not encourage the feeding of the poor" (107:1-3) — a verse that, read with our own sūrah's mention of provision (vv. 9-11), makes the point sharply: in this Book, blocking the good is the practical evidence of unbelief, because denial of the Day is what removes the reason to feed anybody. And the Prophet ﷺ set the standard of "the good" beyond the minimum: "Do not scorn any good thing, even meeting your brother with a relaxed face" (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Dharr) and "the best of you are the best to their families" (al-Tirmidhī, from ʿĀʾisha). The man who "hinders the good" is not defined by one great refusal but by a settled unhelpfulness — the small, repeated withholding that a file records as easily as a crime.

**"The Severe Punishment": The Word That Fits the Sin**

*Al-ʿadhābi al-saʿīr* — "the blazing punishment." The sūrah's phrasing, "cast him into the severe punishment," is left general at the end of the passage, and its generality is deliberate: the worst of the list gets the least description. That is not understatement but a rule the Qur'an states about itself: "and you see the people drunk, though they are not drunk; but the punishment of God is severe" (22:2), and "they will hear its breathing as it boils — it almost bursts with rage" (67:7-8), and "when they are thrown into a narrow place, shackled together, they will call for destruction" (25:13). The severity is proportional to the loss: the person who hindered the good is now himself cut off from the Good — the sūrah's own conclusion in verses 31-35, where the Garden is "brought near" to the reverent and the vision of God is what is "more" (v. 35). The punishment of verse 24, in this sūrah, is not primarily heat; it is the final, confirmed form of the cover that was lifted at verse 22: a person who got exactly what he insisted on.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:25

> **Hindering the good, a transgressor, full of doubt —**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait Gets Its Details**

*Manṭūʿan bi-l-khayri muʿtadin murīb(a)* — the three nouns are not a fourth, fifth and sixth accusation added to the last; in Arabic syntax they stand in apposition (*badal*) to "every stubborn disbeliever" of the previous verse, so that the whole verse is a description of the single type already named. The effect of the grammar is worth noticing: the Qur'an does not say "and he hinders good, and he transgresses, and he doubts," which would make of them separate crimes to be tallied. It says: *this* is what the stubborn denier looks like once you are inside his life. Unbelief here is not an abstract position about God; it is a social style, a way of handling other people's claims and one's own certainties.

This is also why the sūrah places the three at the end of a passage about the *qarīn*, the record, the lifted cover and the file. Everything it has said about the inner life of this type — the cover he chose (v. 22), the file he produced (v. 23) — now acquires content. If a hearer wants to know what the record contains, this verse is the sample.

**"Hindering the Good": The Sin of the Closed Hand**

*Manṭūʿ bi-l-khayr* is literally "one struck, smitten, with respect to good," and the passive participle is deliberate: the man has not merely failed to do good, he is *struck down* so that good cannot pass through him. Al-Ṭabarī glosses the phrase as "one to whom the good is prevented," and, as the source's note records, the exegetes spell out the two directions of the withholding: toward God, the refusal of what is owed to Him — zakāt among the Arabs being the first instance — and toward people, the refusal of what is owed to them, the honouring of contracts and the fairness of dealings. So the "good" that is hindered is not an exotic virtue but the ordinary currency of a decent society: money, honesty, the fulfilment of an agreement, the removal of harm from a road.

Read against the sūrah's earlier verses, the charge becomes almost obscene. Verses 6-11 have just described a universe that is nothing but outflow: the sky with no leak, the earth stretched out, the blessed water sent down, the palms yielding provision, provision "for the servants." The cosmos is generous, and its generosity is not contingent on the recipient's virtue. The man described here is the one anomaly in that economy: he is a passage that is closed.

The ethics of the phrase runs in both directions, and the Prophet ﷺ drew it out in a way the verse itself implies:

> "Every Muslim has a duty of charity." They asked: "And if he finds nothing?" He said: "Let him work with his hands, so that he benefits himself and gives charity." They asked: "And if he cannot?" He said: "Let him help the needy, the grieving." They asked: "And if he cannot?" He said: "Let him command what is right and forbid what is wrong, for that is charity for him." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī)

The ladder descends until it reaches the bottom rung, and the bottom rung is still *khayr*: the least a person can do is to refuse to make the wrong stand. The hinderer of good is the man who has climbed down past the last rung, not because he lacked strength but because he would not spend any. The same ladder appears in the famous narration of the duty of correction, and its bottom rung is the same: "whoever among you sees a wrong, let him change it with his hand; if he cannot, then with his tongue; if he cannot, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith" (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī). The hinderer of good of our verse is the man who has climbed down past that last rung, not because he lacked the strength but because he would not spend any.

**"A Transgressor": Crossing onto Someone Else's Ground**

*Muʿtad* comes from *ʿudwān*, aggression, and its root sense is passing beyond a limit that has been set. The Qur'an uses the verb most often in the language of limits: "do not transgress (*wa-lā taʿtadū*); truly God does not love the transgressors" (2:190); "whoever transgresses the limits of God has wronged himself" (65:1); and, in the verse that gives the word its social shape, "cooperate with one another in righteousness and God-warring care, and do not cooperate in sin and aggression" (5:2). A *muʿtad* is thus precisely a man who takes what is not his — and the source's note preserves the classical gloss: "he violates the rights of people and wrongs and oppresses them." The transgressor is not someone who merely sins; he is someone whose sin has a victim.

That distinction matters for the sūrah's ordering. The hinderer of good withholds; the transgressor seizes. The first is the closing of a hand, the second the laying on of another person's property, reputation or body. Between them lies the whole spectrum of injury, and the Qur'an names both.

**"Full of Doubt": The Man Who Will Not Let Anything Be Settled**

*Ṭabiran murīb(a)* — "doubtful, and one who makes others doubtful." The root *r-y-b* covers the whole family of unease: suspicion, misgiving, the itch that will not be scratched. In the Qur'an, the *murīb* is doubt in its active, infectious sense: "those who were given the Book after it are in disquieting doubt concerning it" (42:14), where the adjective describes a doubt that unsettles; the Qur'an's other word for the same condition is a doubt that bewilders: those whose hearts are sick are "in a doubting discord" about the Hour (22:53; cf. 2:219, where the questions about what they should spend and about orphans end with the words: "God knows what you do"). The word therefore carries a double meaning at once, passive and active: the man is himself irresolute, and he is a source of irresolution in others.

That is why this third charge is not a private matter. A doubter who keeps his doubts to himself is simply a man who has not yet decided; a *murīb* is a man who has made his indecision a policy and who works to spread it, His unbelief is a posture maintained for an audience, which is why the Qur'an classes it with hypocrisy: "they say: we believe in God and the Last Day, but they are not believing; they seek to deceive God and those who believe, and they deceive none but themselves, though they do not perceive it" (2:8-9). In the vocabulary of this sūrah he is the second species of the man of verse 5, "confounded (*marīj*)" — the same disease in two registers, the community's confusion and its propagation in one person.

**The Three Together: Anatomy of a Corrupt Public Life**

Take the three charges in order and you have a working description of how any society decays, and not only the one addressed by this sūrah.

- **The withholding of the good** is the first stage. Contracts are honoured only when it pays. Zakāt is argued away. The surplus of water is kept behind the fence. Public-spiritedness becomes a word people use about other people's behaviour.
- **Transgression** follows as a matter of course. Where the good is not volunteered, it must be extracted: "and they prevent from God's path" (11:19), the Qur'an says of this type, and the prevention of the good and the seizure of what is not one's own are regularly named together.
- **Doubt** is the third stage, and the most durable: the deliberate cultivation of uncertainty about the very categories — honesty, obligation, judgement — that would otherwise convict the first two. The man who takes, and who refuses to give, cannot afford a public that believes clearly.

The classical commentators' instinct, visible already in the note recorded above, was to read this triad as a single moral organism rather than as three separate sins: the closed hand, the grasping hand, and the clouded mind. Each of the three makes the other two survivable.

**The Believer's Share of the Verse**

The verse is about a type of disbeliever, but the sūrah is a *dhikr*, a reminder, "for every penitent and devoted servant" (v. 8), and its sentences are mirrors as much as they are portraits. On that reading, the verse asks three questions of the one who hears it, and they are not comfortable: what good have I stood in the way of this week — by my silence, my stinginess, my busyness? Whom have I crossed, in money, in speech, in the small injustices that leave no record in any court? And what have I done with the doubt I carry: have I argued with it, or have I passed it along to anyone who would listen?

The verse's answer, in the sūrah's own terms, is that none of the three is hidden. The record is complete (v. 18), the cover will be removed (v. 22), and the file will be produced (v. 23). What the hinderer of good cannot hinder, in the end, is the good of seeing exactly what he was — which, as the next verses will show, is the last mercy that reaches him.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:26

> **whoever sets up another god with God. Cast him into the Burning Fire.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last Item, by Itself**

The list of verses 24-25 ran across two lines and piled its charges, and here it stops, and the sentence changes shape: a single relative clause with a single crime — *wa-man jaʿala maʿa Allāhi ilāhan ākhara* — and, instead of further description, a command. The rhetoric is the point. Everything that came before was a portrait drawn in a crowd; the man who "sets up another god with God" is taken out of the crowd and named alone. The plural "cast them both" of verse 24 becomes the singular "cast him" here, and the change of number has a legal ring: this charge is tried by itself, on its own evidence, with its own sentence.

That is not the Qur'an's manner of exaggeration but of definition. It states elsewhere, in the plainest possible terms, why this crime stands apart: "Truly God does not forgive that anything be set up with Him, and He forgives what is besides that for whomever He wills" (4:48; repeated verbatim at 4:116); "whoever sets up anything with God, then it is as though he had fallen from the sky and the birds snatched him away, or the wind hurled him to a far distance" (22:31); "if you associate anything with God, your deeds will come to nothing, and you will surely be among the losers" (39:65).

**"Sets Up": The Verb That Exposes the Trick**

The verb is *jaʿala*, "to make, to appoint, to treat as." The polytheist is not described as someone who discovered a god; he is described as someone who made one. The same verb recurs in the Qur'an's own account of what idols are: "They worship besides Him only names which you have named, you and your fathers — God has sent down no authority for them" (53:23); "They worship besides Him nothing but names which you have named, you and your fathers; God has sent down no authority for them" (12:40); "and who is more astray than one who calls upon besides God those which will not answer him until the Day of Resurrection, and they are unaware of his call? And when the people are gathered, they are enemies to him and deny their worshipping of them" (46:5-6).

Two claims follow. First, the guilt of *shirk* lies in the act of appointment: what a person "makes" into an ultimate concern. Second, the appointed thing has no standing of its own, so that the whole arrangement is a loop of the human being's making, which is exactly the sūrah's charge from verse 2 onward — a warning issued by a warner from among themselves being treated as more incredible than a universe assembled out of nothing.

To the audience of this sūrah, that analysis would have had a specific edge. The gods of the Ḥaram were, in the Qur'an's phrase, names — and the names were inherited: "you and your fathers." The verse therefore denies the one thing the argument from ancestry offers, and its refusal is one with the sūrah's general attack on the mere repetition of what a people has always said.

**The Wider Definition: Obedience as Worship**

The Qur'an's own commentary on what "setting up another god" involves is broader than ritual. About the People of the Book it says: "they took their rabbis and their monks as lords besides God, and the Messiah, son of Mary, though they were commanded only to worship a God who is one" (9:31). In the well-known explanation of that verse, reported by al-Tirmidhī and Aḥmad in his *Musnad*, ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim protested that they had not worshipped them, and the Prophet ﷺ answered that their worship consisted in this: when the monks declared something forbidden, they forbade it, and when they declared it lawful, they made it lawful — "that was their worship of them."

Whether one grades that narration as strong or weak — and the hadith masters have differed about its chains — the principle it states is exactly the principle of this sūrah's passage. What is being listed in verses 24-26 is a structure of allegiance, and the sūrah's audience was being asked to look at their own: to whom they deferred, whose judgement they treated as final, whose approval they feared and whose they sought. *Shirk*, on this reading, is the last and largest instance of the *ʿinād* of verse 24 — the stiffening of the neck against what is evidently true, extended into the choice of an authority to answer to instead of the One who made it.

**"The Burning Fire": The Word the Verse Leaves Unexplained**

*Al-ʿadhābi al-saʿīr* — "the punishment that blazes." After the detailed charges of verses 24-25, the sentence about the punishment is almost offhand: a definite noun and a single adjective. The sūrah's practice elsewhere is the same (see the note on verse 24), and it is the more unnerving for that. The commentaries derive *saʿīr* from the fire's own activity — *saʿara*, to kindle, and so a fire that blazes upward — and the word carries its own explanation.

That one adjective does, however, connect this verse to the most remarkable scene of punishment anywhere in the Qur'an, and the connection is not accidental in a sūrah addressed to the descendants of Ibrāhīm's city: "They said: Build for him a structure and throw him into the blazing fire (*al-jahīm*). They desired a plot against him, and We made them the lowest" (37:97-98); "We said: O fire, be coolness and safety upon Ibrāhīm" (21:69). The fire that is by definition blazing was made cool for one man who refused to "make" anything a god. The same element is therefore not fixed in its behaviour: it is obedient to the One whose oneness the Quraysh were being asked to acknowledge. The man who "sets up another god with God" gets a fire that does exactly what fire does, and the sūrah's phrasing makes that the essence of the punishment rather than its detail.

**Why the Threat Is the Sūrah's, Not a General One**

Three features of the passage's design should be kept together.

*First, the sentence comes after the record.* This punishment is not presented as arbitrary wrath; verses 16-23 have already established that the man is fully known, fully recorded, and fully himself.
