The one who is cast in is the one whose own file testifies, and whose own companion says: "this is what is ready with me" (v. 23).

*Second, the threat is bounded by justice.* Two verses later the sūrah will state its own rule of procedure: "the Word is not changed with Me, and I do not wrong the servants" (v. 29) — a sentence that functions here as a limit on what the threat can mean. The one who casts in is the one who declared that He does not wrong anyone.

*Third, the threat is what makes the promise worth hearing.* The sūrah will close by commanding the Prophet ﷺ to remind "with the Qur'an whoever fears My threat" (v. 45). A threat that no one heeds would be a message no one needs. The severity of this verse is the ground of the last verse's gentleness.

**What the Verse Asks of the Reader**

It is easy to hear this verse as a sentence about somebody else — a distant idolater, a Pharaoh, a figure in a list. The sūrah itself forecloses that: it told its first hearers plainly that their "wonder" at a warner from among themselves was unbelief with a different label (v. 2), and that what they were saying about scattered bones was the same word as the denial of the people of Noah (v. 3). The question the verse puts is therefore not anthropological but personal, and it has a shape that any century can feel: what have I "made" into a god? What do I obey without question, whose judgement do I treat as final, whose disapproval do I organise my life around? Whatever that is, the sūrah says, is the *ilāh* — and the sentence at the end of this verse is addressed not to the man's defects but to his decision.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:27

> **His companion will say, "Our Lord, I did not make him go astray; rather he was far astray."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Voice**

Verse 23 gave us the companion's first speech, one line, delivered without emotion: "This is what is ready with me." Verse 27 gives us the second, and it is a plea. The change is the whole argument of the passage: the file is produced, the man is cast in, and now the man who carried the file is being questioned about how it came to contain what it contains — and he answers.

The sūrah leaves *qarīn* deliberately ambiguous, and the exegetes take it two ways. Some, with al-Ṭabarī, understand the companion here as the same being of verse 23, the assigned angel or the recording presence. Others take the *qarīn* as the evil prompting itself, following the verse that glosses this sūrah's word most directly: "and whoever is blinded from the remembrance of the All-Merciful — We assign to him a satan, and so he becomes for him an inseparable companion" (43:36). The ambiguity is a mercy to the reader, because either way the point is identical: no being was given authority over this man's will, and the one who came closest to him denies having made him what he is.

**"Our Lord": The Address That Comes Too Late**

Note how both parties speak in the court: "Our Lord" — *rabbanā*. The same phrase occurs at verse 30 of this sūrah when Hell is asked whether it is full, and the Qur'an's other courtroom scenes make the address a feature of the setting: "and if you could see when the criminals hang their heads before their Lord: 'Our Lord, we saw and heard, so send us back; we will do righteous deeds; we now are certain'" (32:12); "and they will say: Our Lord, we obeyed our leaders and our great ones, and they led us from the path" (33:67).

The address is polite, and it is useless. That is the sūrah's point about the last day of excuses: the language of reverence survives the loss of its opportunity. In this world, the same men called God "Lord" in a way that cost them nothing and committed them to nothing; in the court they say "Our Lord" to win an argument. The Qur'an says of exactly that switch: "and if you could see when the criminals hang their heads before their Lord: 'Our Lord, we saw and heard, so send us back; we will do righteous deeds; we now are certain'" (32:12); "and when they contend in the Fire, the weak will say to those who had been proud: we were your followers — can you then ward off from us a portion of the Fire? Those who had been proud will say: we are all in it; truly God has judged between the servants" (40:47). Whatever is said after the sentence is not an answer to what was said before it.

**The Argument About a Word: "I Did Not Make Him My Lord"**

There is a remarkable discussion about the verb of this clause. The received text reads *mā ʿawwaytuhu*, "I did not lead him astray," and that is the reading the translation follows. But from Ibn ʿAbbās down, the companions of the verse also transmitted the wording "I did not make him my lord" — that is, the verb from *ʿ-ad-d* rather than from the root of error; and it is on this that the reading of "stiff-necked" (*ʿanīd*) in verse 24 turns (see the note there). Al-Ṭabarī preserves the second reading and explains it: the man's error was that he obeyed his companion as though he were a lord, and the companion disclaims the relationship.

The two readings belong together. To lead someone astray and to be made into someone's lord are, in the Qur'an's anthropology, the same event seen from either end. And when the Qur'an defines *shirk* as "taking as lords" (9:31), it is describing precisely the transaction that this verse denies: whoever is called upon besides God, the Qur'an says, "have no power for harm or benefit to themselves, and no power over death or life or resurrection" (25:3) — a master who cannot do any of the three things a master is hired for.

**"Far Astray": The Distance That Was Already There**

The companion's second clause is the theological heart of the verse: *bal kāna fī ḍalālin baʿīd* — "rather, he was in an error far." The adjective "far" is the same word that opened the sūrah's list of the deniers' complaints: "that is a return far off" (v. 3). Distance, not force, is the sūrah's governing image of what went wrong. The man was already at a distance before anything was proposed to him; the whisper only arrived somewhere he was.

The Qur'an states the rule in a dozen places and never qualifies it. Satan's own testimony at the end of the world: "Satan will say, when the matter has been decided: God promised you a promise of truth, and I promised you and failed you. And I had no authority over you except that I called you, and you obeyed me. So do not blame me; blame yourselves" (14:22). And the divine answer to the claim that anyone was compelled: "My servants — you have no authority over them, except those who follow you among the deviators" (15:42; cf. 17:65). "I created jinn and humans only that they worship Me. I do not want any provision from them" (51:56-57) — and the verse quoted in the source's note for this sūrah, on the impossibility of being driven: "Whoever is guided is guided for himself, and whoever is astray, say: I am only one of the warners" (27:92; cf. 17:15: "We never punish until We send a messenger").

**The Question of Influence: What the Verse Does Not Absolve**

Read carelessly, verse 27 looks like a general amnesty for anyone who gave bad advice: persuasion is not coercion, so the persuaded are on their own. But the Qur'an balances the acquittal with two statements that keep the persuader in the frame.

First, the burden of misleading is itself counted: "so that they will carry their burdens in full on the Day of Resurrection, and something of the burdens of those they lead astray without knowledge" (16:25; cf. 29:13). Second, the sūrah's own court already established that influence is a matter of acts, not of intentions — every word is attended (v. 18). The two claims are not contradictory. A man is not punished for another's choice; he is punished for what he did in the making of the choice, and especially for the *doubt* he cultivated in others, which was the third charge against him at verse 25. The companion's defence succeeds because he is charged with *compulsion* — "did not make him transgress" — and no one in this court was ever accused of that.

This is the fine line the verse teaches about moral responsibility in a world of persuasion: responsibility is a chain that runs through a person, never around him. And it is the line that protects both parties from a cruel inference — that a victim's guilt is his abuser's, or that a weak man's weakness is his friend's. The verse's own logic is the opposite of both: "rather, he was far astray" — an appeal to something the man *was*, not to anything the companion *did*.

**What the Scene Would Have Meant in Makkah**

The verse also has an argumentative function inside the sūrah's situation. The Meccan hearers were, in the Qur'an's description, a collective, and they argued as a collective: "they say: we found our fathers upon a religion, and we are following in their footsteps" (43:23); "and when it is said to them: follow what God has sent down, they say: rather we follow what we found our fathers upon — even if Satan was calling them to the punishment of the Blaze?" (31:21). Every one of those verses ends with the same disclosure this verse makes: the fathers, the leaders, the *sadat* — all will disown the following.

And in the sūrah's immediate context the point lands on the Quraysh's *assemblies* rather than on their households: the *nādī*, the council-ring of the Harām, in which the decision to reject this message was made in public, argued in company, and reinforced by reputation. Verse 25 has just described the "doubter" — the man who spreads irresolution; verse 27 shows what his reward will be when his audience disowns him. The Qur'an's summary of that reversal, in the sūrah of the Firewood, is a single line: "the intimate friends on that Day are enemies to one another, except the God-warring" (43:67); "and the friend will not ask about his friend, though they are shown to one another" (70:10).

**The Friendship That Survives**

The exception in that last clause is the sūrah's own conclusion, and it is worth stating plainly in a passage about a companion who cannot vouch for the man he accompanied. The Qur'an's catalogue of surviving attachments is short: "the friend is on the religion of his intimate, so let one of you look carefully at whom he takes as an intimate" (a *ḥasan* report in Sunan Abī Dāwūd and Sunan al-Tirmidhī, from Abū Hurayra); "the example of the intimate companion is like the seller of musk and the smith: from the musk-seller you either buy perfume or at least enjoy its scent, and the smith's fire may burn your clothes" (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, from Abū Mūsā).

Verse 27 belongs to the same moral teaching, only seen from the other side. When a friendship has no content beyond itself — when it exists for the pleasure of being agreed with — it dissolves on the day when agreement is no longer cheap. That is what the companion's speech announces, and the sūrah holds the mirror up: the friendship you are in now, whose verdict will it be reciting?

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:28

> **He says, "Dispute not before Me, when I have already sent the Threat to you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Judge Ends the Hearing**

The verse is a single imperative with a clause of reason attached: stop contending — *why* — because the warning reached you. Nothing else is said. There is no argument, no recapitulation of the evidence, no answer to the objections the sūrah has just quoted in verses 2, 3, 7, 12 and 15. The court of verses 16-27 has finished its business: the record was produced (v. 23), the man was cast in (vv. 24-26), the companion washed his hands of the affair (v. 27). What remains is the noise the condemned make when the verdict has come down, and God's sentence upon the noise itself.

That is the reason the verb here is unusual. The Qur'an's ordinary words for argument — *jidal*, *miraʾ*, *khuṣūm* — do not appear in this clause; the exegetes gloss the verb as "do not dispute with one another, do not contend," and the rarity of the wording in this position is one more sign of how the sūrah treats this scene: as an interruption to be cut off rather than a case to be re-opened.

**What the Court Rules Out, and What It Does Not**

It is important to read the sentence exactly. God does not say "the evidence is false" and He does not say "the sentence is unjust." He says that re-argument is closed, because the one thing that could have changed the outcome — notice — already occurred: *wa-qad qaddamtu ilaykum bi-l-waʿīd*. The verse rules out relitigation, not appeal.

This is the same rule the Qur'an states in its own legal vocabulary elsewhere: "We never punish until We send a messenger" (17:15); "your Lord is not one to destroy the towns for wrong until He has sent to their mother-town a messenger reciting to them Our signs" (28:59); "and We never punish until we have sent a messenger" is the principle behind the verse quoted in the source's note here: "there has been no community but that a warner has passed among it" (35:24; cf. 16:36). Where notice has been given, the case is about what was done with it. Where it has not, God does not try the case at all.

Notice also what this does *for* the human being in the dock: it makes the trial about acts rather than about metaphysics. Nobody in this scene is asked why God permitted the possibility of unbelief, or how foreknowledge and freedom fit together. Those are the questions the sūrah's hearers were asking in Makkah (vv. 2-3, 12, 15, 19), and the verse answers by declaring them inadmissible: the only admissible question is whether the Threat arrived, and it arrived.

**"I Have Sent the Threat to You": The Double Meaning of *Qaddamtu***

The verb *qaddama* means to send ahead, to place before, and the same root supplies the Qur'an's regular word for a person's own accumulated deeds: "whatever good you send ahead (*fa-mā tuqaddimū*) for yourselves you will find with God, better and greater in reward" (73:20); "that Day every soul will find whatever good it sent ahead, and whatever evil it did" (3:30; cf. 18:49, "and they will find their deeds present before them"). The overlap is not decoration. The God who "sent ahead" the warning is addressing people who "sent ahead" their lives, and the two sendings are the two halves of the trial. Each side has already put on the table everything it has.

That is also why the phrase "the Threat" (*bi-l-waʿīd*) is left with such breadth. Some read it here as the Qur'an itself, and the source's note preserves that reading, because in this sūrah *waʿīd* is already a book-word: "so the Threat came due" (v. 13) refers to what was announced, and the sūrah ends by commanding the Prophet ﷺ to remind with the Qur'an "whoever fears the Threat" (v. 45). The Qur'an calls itself, in one breath, "a Book whose verses are made firm — a Qur'an in Arabic, as a warning and a glad tiding" (41:3-4), and it reproaches those who treat it as a thing to be out-argued: "shall We then turn the reminder away from you because you are a people who exceed?" (43:5).

**The Qur'an's Own Word for Endless Contention**

The verse's brevity about the trial has a reason visible elsewhere in the Qur'an: contention about the signs is described not as a search but as a habit, and the habit has a name. "They say: there is only our life of this world... they have no knowledge of that; they only conjecture" (45:24); "and when Our signs are recited to them, they say: we have heard this before — if We willed, We could say the like of it; this is nothing but fables of the ancients" (8:31); and about the same audience: "so leave them to play and tumble until they meet their Day which they are promised" (21:51), and "leave them to eat and enjoy themselves, and let hope occupy them: soon they will know" (15:3). The verse at 50:28 is God's version of the same weariness: "leave them" is the instruction to the Prophet in this world, and "dispute not before Me" is the instruction in the next. The two sentences belong to each other, and both of them refuse to treat unbelief as an intellectual position in need of a better argument.

What the sūrah offers instead of argument is seeing — the sky, the earth, the water, the palms, the dead land revived (vv. 6-11), and the whispering self (v. 16). That is why the court scene can be so short: the evidence was presented in the first half of the chapter, in the world, and the file has now been read from the record. Verse 28 is not the end of a debate; it is the end of a delay.

**The Verse Addressed to the Living**

The audience this sūrah is trying to reach is not in the court; it is in Makkah, still able to turn (v. 8), still un-covered (v. 22 in prospect). For them, verse 28 is not a verdict but a warning about a habit. To "dispute before God" is, in this world, to argue about the terms of the case instead of obeying the summons — to ask how a human messenger can be trusted (v. 2), how bones can live (v. 3), whether the Book is "confounded" (v. 5). Every one of those questions is answered somewhere in the sūrah, and none of them is answered in the court. The verse's practical force is therefore a summons to stop negotiating with the message and start receiving it, while receiving is still possible.

And there is a gentleness in the severity, which the next verse will state outright: the reason the argument is closed is not that God is impatient but that He is trustworthy. What He sent ahead, He will not take back — and that, as verse 29 will say, is good news precisely because it is true of the pardon as well as of the threat.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:29

> **The Word is not changed with Me, and I do not wrong the servants."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Halves of One Verse**

This is one of the Qur'an's most compressed statements of how God governs, and it works by holding two claims that people habitually pull apart. *Mā ladayya bi-mubaddal* — "with Me there is no changing": the divine Word is not renegotiated, not revised, not softened under pressure, not swapped for an alternative. And: *wa-mā anā bi-ẓallāmin li-l-ʿabīd* — "and I am not one to wrong the servants": the unchanging Word is not an arbitrary one, because it never departs from justice.

Say the first without the second and sovereignty becomes caprice: a God whose "no" might become "yes" if enough is pleaded. Say the second without the first and justice becomes a standard standing over against God, to which He is answerable. The verse says both, in one breath, in the register of a courtroom ruling, and the pairing is the whole of classical Islamic theology's answer to the question "is the good good because God wills it, or does God will it because it is good?" The verse refuses the alternative: God's will and the good are not two things in competition, because He is the One who declares "I do not wrong."

**"The Word": Decree, Book, and Promise**

The source's note records that the commentators take the "Word" here in two directions, and, as it says, both are meant: the divine Decree — what God has determined about a people — and the Book, the Qur'an as God's speech. The doubling is supported by the Qur'an's own usage, where *kalima* and *qawl* cover promise and threat at once: "your Lord is the Forgiving, full of mercy: if He were to take them for what they have earned, He would hasten their punishment — but they have a time from which they will find no refuge" (18:58); and, of those beyond whom the Word has gone, "the Word of your Lord has come true against them: they will not believe, even if every sign came to them, until they see the painful punishment" (10:96-97). The point is not the lexical detail but the effect: in the sūrah's court, "the Word is not changed" means that the file and the sentence correspond, and that the correspondence was guaranteed before the file was written.

The nearest echo of the wording is decisive in its own right, and the source's note cites it here: "I will surely fill Hell with jinn and human beings all together" (32:13). The threat of verse 28 and the filling of verse 30 are, on this reading, one sentence spoken twice, once in the counsel of this world and once in the court of the next.

**"Not Changed with Me": Where, and With Whom**

The preposition is *laday* — "with Me, at My side, in My presence" — the same word that appears at verse 35 ("and with Us there is more") and in the verse quoted for verse 22's card of good deeds. It locates the unchangeability not in a document or a law but in a place of proximity: what is "with God" is settled in a way that what is with us is not. That is why the phrase can function as both threat and comfort within a single sūrah. To the obstinate it means: what was said about the Fire will be. To the penitent servant of verse 8 it means: what was said about the Garden will be, and no argument will get it altered.

The verse's comfort is documented in the sūrah's own idiom two verses later, where the Word that will not change is a Word about filling Hell — and, in the most moving of the Qur'an's self-restrictions, "your Lord has decreed mercy upon Himself" (6:12), a God who "changes His word" could promise nothing, including mercy. The sūrah also calls its own address "a clear light" (4:174) and says that its verses are "decided" — "a Book whose verses are made firm" (11:1) — so that the unchangeability of verse 29 is not an abstraction about omnipotence but a claim about this Book: what was said here stands. On the lips of anyone who has ever repented in this sūrah, the verse's negative form is therefore the most positive sentence in the passage: if the Word cannot be changed, the pardon He has announced cannot be withdrawn either.

**"I Do Not Wrong the Servants": The Self-Restraint That Is Not Restraint**

The second half of the verse is one of the Qur'an's first-person denials — "and I am not one to wrong the servants" — and it belongs to a family of such statements that run from the beginning of the Book to its end: "your Lord does not wrong anyone" (18:49); "and God does not wrong the servants" (3:174; 40:31); "your Lord is not one to destroy the towns unjustly while their people are heedless" (6:131); "that is because of what your hands have sent ahead, and because God is not a wrongdoer to the servants" (8:51; 22:10; 41:46).

To this the tradition adds a ḥadīth that the sūrah's hearers would recognise as its own moral register, and that the commentators quote in this connection:

> "God the Exalted says: O My servants, I have made injustice forbidden upon Myself, and I have made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another." (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Dharr)

That hadith is the best available commentary on the second half of the verse, because it draws the inference the sūrah draws: the divine self-restraint is not a limit imposed on God but an attribute of His perfection, and it therefore becomes an obligation for those made in His image as servants. The God who does not wrong the servants is the God who forbids injustice to Himself and then to them.

The word *ʿabīd* — the plural of *ʿabd*, servant, slave — is the sūrah's own choice, and it is the link to verse 11: the provision was "for the servants," and the One who provided is the One who will not wrong them. Between the two verses, in verses 16-27, stands the whole record of a human being's life; verse 29 says the record will be read by One whose verdict on it is just and whose sentence on it will not be renegotiated.

**The Two Errors the Verse Closes**

Commentators through the centuries have used this verse to close two doors at once.

- **The door of presumption** — "God is forgiving, He will let it pass." The verse's first half answers: the Word announced in this sūrah, in verses 12-14 and 24-26, does not change because the sinner would like it to. The Qur'an's own phrase for this presumption is the man who says "if I am returned to my Lord, I will have something better than this" (41:50; cf. 18:35 — "I do not think the Hour will ever come, and if I am returned to my Lord I will surely find better than this as a return").
- **The door of despair** — "my record is too black to be dealt with justly." The second half answers: justice means each soul is charged with its own, not more than its scope (v. 18; "God does not charge a soul except with what it can bear," 2:286), and that God has bound Himself not to wrong. Between these two denials sits the sūrah's whole ethic: take the threat with total seriousness, and take the mercy with total seriousness, because both are "with Me," and what is with Me does not change.

**A Note on the Sūrah's Manner of Closing Sentences**

Verses 28 and 29 are the last time in this sūrah that God speaks directly in the first person about the fate of the condemned; after verse 29 the voice returns to the narrative and the hortatory tones of verses 30-45. That placement gives the pair the shape of a formal pronouncement — the charge (24-26), the defence (27), the ruling on the proceedings (28), and on what follows it (29). It is a legal structure of extraordinary economy, and one that any student of law would recognise: the judge's short final sentence, in which the case is not re-argued but closed, and the reason given in a single clause: the notice was served, and the court does not wrong anyone.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:30

> **That Day We say to Hell, "Have you been filled?" And it will say, "Is there more?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Put to an Abyss**

*Yawma naqūlu li-jahannama hal imtalaʾt* — the address is not to the people in the Fire but to the Fire itself, and the whole scene turns on that. God interrogates a place. It answers. The verb of the question is perfect ("are you filled?"), the verb of the answer is an offer of more ("is there more?"), and the exchange is the last conversation in this sūrah before the tone changes to the Garden.

The Qur'an elsewhere gives speech and testimony to what human beings treat as inert: "and they will say to their skins: why have you testified against us? They will say: God who made all things to speak has made us to speak" (41:21); "until, when they come to it, their hearing, their sight and their skins testify against them to what they used to do" (41:20); "and when the earth has shaken with its shaking, and the earth has told its news" (99:4-5); "and the mountains and the birds, in a row, singing His praises" (38:19). Verse 30 belongs to this family, and its logic is the sūrah's: in a universe that came from a Word and is held by a Word, nothing is genuinely mute, and even the destination of the damned is a respondent in the trial.

**"Is There More?" — the Two Readings, and Why Both Are Terrifying**

The answer *hal min mazīd* is a bare interrogative, and the source's note records the two ways the commentators take it, both from al-Ṭabarī: some say the Fire means "I am full," so that the question is a rhetorical statement of satiation; others say it means "give me more," an appetite that has not been satisfied. The note then makes the subtle observation that decides the matter — the two readings depend on when the reply is heard, before or after what the ḥadīth describes as God's "Foot" is placed in Hell.

That is not pedantry. Each reading carries its own terror.

- If Hell means *I am full*, the horror is finality: a receptacle that was made for a purpose, and has now fulfilled it, with nothing left to do. The damned are the content that completed an otherwise idle creation.
- If Hell means *give me more*, the horror is scale: a mouth that can receive millions upon millions of generations and still be empty. The verse would then be describing an appetite matched only by God's self-restraint — and, as we saw at verse 29, the restraint is real: "I do not wrong the servants," so the Fire does not get more than it is owed.

Both readings sit comfortably in the sūrah's structure, in which the punishment is described with the same flat precision as the provision of verse 11: things are given their measure and no more.

**The Ḥadīth That Fills the Fire**

The note in the source cites a tradition transmitted by ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd and recorded in both Ṣaḥīḥs:

> "Hell continues to be thrown into, and it says: is there more? — until the All-Mighty places over it His Foot, and its two sides are folded together, and it says: enough, enough — by Your might. And not one of God's creatures will be wronged." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd)

The wording matches verse 30 so closely — the question, the demand for more, the ending, the reference to wronging — that the sūrah's own conclusion of the trial, "I do not wrong the servants" (v. 29), reads as the key to it: Hell is filled, then closed, and the closing is an act of justice, not of exhaustion. A second tradition, transmitted by ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr and quoted by the commentators on this verse, adds the shape of the scene: "the Fire keeps saying: I have been given too little, I have been given too little — until God puts into it what He wills." (In the source's note: "as for the Fire, it will not be filled until God puts His Foot upon it, saying to it, 'Enough, Enough.' Then it will be full, and it folds in upon itself. And God will not wrong anyone among His creation.")

**How the Commentators Read "Foot"**

The tradition's language raised, early and inevitably, the question of how to speak of a "Foot" of God, and the answers fall into the two disciplined attitudes that Sunni theology developed for all such texts.

The first is consignation: the words are affirmed as they came, their sense is entrusted to God, and no modality is asserted — because "foot" in the divine case cannot mean what it means in ours, and inventing a meaning is a bolder move than declining one. The second is interpretation: the phrase is read as the arrival of the divine command, the descent of a decree, the execution of an order, with the note that *qadam* also means the rank of an army and what goes before. Al-Ṭabarī and others preserve both kinds of reading, and the source's note here mentions that "the Foot" is often understood as a symbol of divine mercy, because it brings the Fire's insatiable appetite to an end — the reading that turns the tradition into a mercy-text on the lips of the exegetes.

What matters for the interpretation of this sūrah is the negative consensus: nobody who took these reports seriously read them as describing a body, and nobody read the Fire as an independent power that God must placate. The tradition's point is that the Fire is a servant, and its satiation is an act of divine authority. The final line of the ḥadīth in the source makes the same point in one phrase: "God will not wrong anyone among His creation."

**A Fire With a Measure, a Mercy Without One**

The verse's contribution to the sūrah's architecture is the notion of a limit fixed by speech. Hell is not an eternal appetite running on its own; it is asked a question, and the question implies an answer with a term. Verse 29 has just said why: "the Word is not changed with Me," and the Word includes the measure of the Fire's filling. In the next verse the sūrah will turn to the Garden, and the turn is prepared here, because the two destinations will be distinguished not by the intensity of their description but by whether they admit of a "more" at all.

That distinction answers everything from verse 6 onward. The sky was without rifts and the earth was stretched out; both were *near* evidence, and the verdict at verse 22 was that the hearers had been heedless of it, "so We removed from you your cover, so your sight today is piercing." Nearness, in this sūrah, is not only epistemic: the universe was arranged as a sign, and it will be arranged as a road.

**The Same Word at Both Ends: *Mazīd***

A small linguistic fact completes the scene. The Fire's question — *hal min mazīd*, "is there more?" — uses the only other occurrence of the root in this sūrah, and it is in the verse about the Garden: "for them is whatever they will in it, and *with Us there is more* (*mazīd*)" (v. 35). The single word thus spans the chapter's two destinations. There is a "more" for the Fire — an asking that ends, because the Word fixes the measure — and a "more" with God that does not end, because the Word there is a promise rather than a quota. The source's note for verse 35 makes exactly this pairing, in its own terms: the punishment of Hell is "set and delimited," while "the rewards of the Garden are infinite."

Verse 30's Hell and verse 35's Garden are therefore held in one frame by one word. It is the last structural flourish of the sūrah's judgement scene, and the next verses will spend the rest of the chapter unpacking the second half of the frame.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:31

> **And the Garden will be brought near for the reverent, not distant:**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Other Half of the Same Day**

Verses 30 and 31 are a diptych, and the hinge is the single word *yawma* — "that Day." On that Day God speaks to Hell, and on that Day the Garden is moved. The parallelism is exact and the difference is total: to the Fire, a question about whether it has reached its capacity; to the reverent, an act of conveyance, a bringing-hither. The damned are counted; the saved are escorted.

The verb of the second half, *zulifat* — "was brought near, was made adjacent" — carries the sūrah's whole vocabulary of proximity into the next world. The sky was "near" evidence (vv. 6-11); God was "nearer than the jugular vein" (v. 16); the return the deniers called "far" (v. 3) is now, in the sūrah's own two-word clause, "not distant." The chapter has been arguing about nearness from its first verse, and it resolves the argument by moving a Garden rather than by answering an objection.

The verse ends with a colon in the source's own punctuation, and the punctuation is right: what follows in verses 32-34 is what is said to the people of the Garden. The sūrah thus gives us, side by side, two addresses — the question put to Hell and the welcome put to the reverent — and the reader is meant to hear both.

**What "Brought Near" Means in the Qur'an's Own Language**

The root *z-l-f* supplies the Qur'an's word for an approach, a means of access, a thing by which one draws near; and the noun *zulfa* is used both of what human beings try to obtain and of what they are given. The verse that comments on our own most directly is this: "those whom they call upon besides Him seek the means of nearness to their Lord, whichever of them is nearest, and they hope for His mercy and fear His punishment" (17:57) — that is, *zulfa* is what every worshipper is after, and what every intermediary is supposed to provide. Our verse says that what the idolater tried to procure by proxies is given to the reverent by being brought to them. No journey, no intermediary, no ladder of access: the Garden is moved.

The same root gives the Qur'an its name for the prayers at the edges of the day: "and perform the prayer at the two ends of the day and in the approaches (*ṣu-lūfan*) of the night; the good deeds take away the evil deeds" (11:114) — a verse the commentators keep in view when they reach this sūrah's commands to praise "before the rising of the sun and before the setting" (v. 39), because the sūrah, having described the Garden's nearness, will prescribe the practice that rehearses it. Nearness, in this book, is not only a destination but a schedule.

**"For the Reverent": The Sūrah's Final Definition**

*Li-l-muttaqīn* — the saved party is named by a noun of their own activity, not by a privilege of birth, tribe or history. This is a point of some weight in a chapter addressed to a city that defined itself by custodianship of the Sanctuary and descent from Ibrāhīm and Ismāʿīl; the sūrah never once mentions their pedigree, and here it names them by a quality available to anyone: *tawā*.

The Qur'an's fullest description of the *muttaqūn* is worth quoting at this point because it matches our sūrah's own list of qualities with unnerving precision: "and hasten to forgiveness from your Lord, and to a Garden whose breadth is the heavens and the earth, prepared for the reverent — who spend in ease and in hardship, restrain their anger, and pardon people; God loves the doers of good — and who, when they do something shameful or wrong themselves, remember God and ask forgiveness for their sins" (3:133-135). Note the two things that passage and ours share: the Garden is *prepared* (not earned from nothing), and the reverent are defined by the handling of their own worst moments rather than by an absence of them.

**"Not Distant": The Answer to Verse 3**

The last clause of the verse is easy to read as filler — the Garden is brought near, and, well, not far away. Read as the sūrah's own echo, it is a rebuttal. The men of verse 3 said of the resurrection, *dhālika rajʿun baʿīd*, "that is a return far off." The word "far" was their verdict on the sheer unlikeliness of it. Here "far" is the word God takes back: the thing you called remote is not remote, and the nearness in question is not spatial. The sūrah has already said what makes it remote: the cover of verse 22, the heedlessness of "the reckoning has drawn near, yet they are in heedlessness" (21:1), and the rust the Qur'an names at the close of the chapter on those who defraud: "no — but what they used to earn has rusted upon their hearts" (83:14). The distance was never in the destination; it was in the heart.

That is also why the clause is consoling rather than merely descriptive. For the person who has been listening to this sūrah and felt the pull of the return, the sentence promises that the return has an end and a nearness. The chapter that began with a letter whose meaning the hearer cannot reach ends its description of the two worlds with the word "not distant."

**The Scene's Restraint**

One feature of the verse should be named, because it is the reason the section that follows is so striking. In a hundred places in the Qur'an the Garden is described in its furniture: rivers, couches, fruit, spouses, houses. Here the description is a single verb of motion. The sūrah that has spent five verses on the nearness of God to the jugular and on the sky without rifts does not need to inventory the Garden; it says only that it comes close, and leaves the rest to verses 32-35, which will speak of what is promised, of what is feared in secret, and of what "with Us there is more."

**The Passive Voice of Salvation**

Notice the grammar of the verse: the Garden "is brought near" — a divine passive, with no human subject anywhere — and the person is not said to arrive, to climb, to achieve, or even yet to enter. Entry comes only at verse 34, and it comes as a command issued from outside the self: "enter it in peace." The chapter's whole soteriology is packed into that sequence of voices. The human being acts in verses 32-33 (he returns, he guards, he fears, he comes with a penitent heart); God acts in verses 31 and 34 (the Garden is moved, the entry is decreed). Neither is reduced to the other, and the sūrah never has to explain how they fit, because it never puts them in the same sentence.

That is why the Qur'an's own commentary on this scene is a sentence of gratitude rather than of accomplishment: "and they will say: praise belongs to God, who guided us to this! We could never have been guided had God not guided us" (7:43) — a statement in which the saved take nothing away from their own striving and give away everything about its success. The passive of verse 31 says the same thing in a single verb, and the sūrah's hearers, who had been told that the reminder profits only "whoever has a heart" (v. 37), are left with the other half of the truth: the heart that has it, has it as well from the One who brings the Garden near.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:32

> **This is what you are promised, for every penitent servant, one who guards —**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Words the Whole Sūrah Has Been About**

*Hādhā mā tuʿadūna li-kulli ʿabbādin awwābin ḥafīẓ* — and the two nouns that define who receives the promise are the two the sūrah has been building toward since verse 8: "a reminder for every penitent servant (*kulli ʿabdin awwābin*)." The circle closes on the same words with which the section on creation opened, so that the whole of verses 8-32 is, formally, an elaboration of a single pair of qualities. Everything else — the sky, the water, the palms, the record, the court, the Fire — is scenery for the making of an *awwāb ḥāfiẓ*.

That is why the verse is worth reading as a definition rather than as a reward-schedule. The promise is not made to a person with certain achievements but to a person with a certain shape: one who keeps returning, and one who keeps guard.

**"Returning, Returning": What *Awwāb* Means**

The intensive form *faʿʿāl* denotes habit rather than event: an *awwāb* is not somebody who repented once but somebody whose default is return. The source's note captures both halves of the word: "someone who is penitent," and also "one who is constantly turned to God and thus remembers Him in all things." The first sense looks backward — repair of what was broken; the second looks forward — orientation that persists through the day.

The Qur'an gives the word its profile by putting it on the lips of its best characters and at the hinge of its most practical commands. Of Dāwūd: "recall Our servant Dāwūd, possessed of strength; he was one who turned often" (38:17), and the very next verse says what the turning looked like in his day: "We subjected the mountains to glorify with him, in the evening and at sunrise" (38:18) — the *awwāb* is a man whose return has hours. And the command that ties the word to forgiveness is a single sentence: "and perform the prayer at the two ends of the day and in the approaches of the night; the good deeds take away the evil deeds" (11:114).

To this the sūrah's own first note added the prophetic description of the turning as an act of the whole person: "everything that exists serves as a reminder of God's Might and Power and an indication of His Oneness, because 'in each thing there is a sign, indicating that He is the One,'" and the verse quoted there for the *awwāb* is exactly what our passage now promises. The *dhikr* of verse 8, the *dhikr* of verse 37, and the *awwāb* of verses 8 and 32 are one system: the world reminds, the heart returns, and the return is what the Garden is for.

**"A Guardian": What *Ḥafīẓ* Means**

The second noun is the sūrah's quietest and most severe. *Ḥafīẓ* — a keeper, a warden, one who guards what has been entrusted — and the Qur'an's own use of the word is the language of trust, as in Yāqūb's answer when his sons asked to take Yūsuf with them: "shall I entrust you with him as I entrusted you with his brother before? God is the best of guardians, and He is the most merciful of the merciful" (12:64). In the source's note here the word is glossed, with al-Ṭabarī, as "a person who keeps the commands of God and avoids falling into sin," and the verse's two nouns are then translated together as "everyone who is penitent and steadfast."

The word has already appeared in this sūrah in a way that no reader should be allowed to forget: at verse 18, where the human being's every word is attended by "a ready watcher" — *ʿatīd ḥafīẓ*. The pairing is the chapter's most exact teaching about these two words. The man called *ḥāfiẓ* at verse 32 is the one who has taken seriously the fact that he was already being kept at verse 18: guarded, watched, recorded, preserved in being. The ethics of the guardian is a response, not an initiative.

That reading also explains why *awwāb* and *ḥāfiẓ* belong together rather than being alternatives. Guarding without returning yields rigour — a person who keeps the form and never repairs his failures. Returning without guarding yields enthusiasm — a person who repents each night and guards nothing by day. The verse's saved man does both, and the sūrah's structure makes the combination rational: you can afford to guard because you will be forgiven, and you must keep returning because you will not be perfect.

**"This Is What You Are Promised": The Promise Underwrites Itself**

The demonstrative — "this" — points back to the Garden brought near, and the passive of the verb ("you are promised") leaves the promiser unnamed in the sentence because the whole sūrah has named Him. The promise is therefore measured by the one who made it, and two verses earlier God said: "the Word is not changed with Me" (v. 29). Verse 29's clause is often quoted only for the threat; here it does its real work, since the sentence the sūrah now calls a promise is the same Word that cannot be revised.

The Qur'an states the principle as a creed: "God does not fail in the promise" (3:9); "and the Word of your Lord has been accomplished in truth and justice: there is no changing of His Words, and He is the Hearing, the Knowing" (6:115) — the verse the source's note cites for verse 29's clause, and it is quoted here because the promise of this verse and the threat of that one rest on the same sentence; "and your Lord says: call upon Me, and I will answer you" (40:60); "and if you count God's favour, you cannot enumerate it" (14:34).

**A Word About the Colon, and About Who Speaks**

Verse 31's colon and verse 32's opening "this is what you are promised" mean that someone is speaking to the people of the Garden, and the sūrah does not say who. The commentators allow both the divine voice and the words of the angels of escort, and the ambiguity is a virtue: welcome in the Qur'an's accounts of Paradise arrives from many directions — "and the angels enter upon them from every gate: peace upon you, because you were steadfast" (13:23-24); "their greeting with Him on the Day they meet Him will be peace" (33:44); "a word of peace, from a Lord, merciful" (36:58); "they will hear therein no idle talk nor incitement to sin, but only the saying: peace, peace" (56:25-26).

Whatever the identity of the speaker, the sentence's content is that the promise is finally told to the person it was made to. The sūrah, which began by swearing that the warning was being delivered to a people who were marveling, ends the description of the saved with the one word they never expected to hear addressed to them: *hādhā*, "this is it."

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:33

> **whoever fears the All-Merciful, though unseen, and comes with a penitent heart.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Definition of the Secret Person**

The verse's one clause does two things at once: it names the reverent person's relation to what he does not see, and it names his direction of travel. *Man khashiya al-raḥmāna bi-l-ghaybi wa-jāʾa bi-qalbin munīb* — "whoever feared the All-Merciful with [Him] unseen, and came with a returning heart." The first half is about the private self; the second about the trajectory of the whole life. The sūrah has been describing a public trial — a court, a record, a companion, a verdict — and it says here that the person on the winning side of that trial is the one whose decisive acts were performed where nobody was looking.

This is the most concentrated statement of the *sūrah's* theory of sincerity anywhere in the chapter, and it should be read back into verses 16-18: God knows what the self whispers (v. 16), and every word is watched (v. 18). Verse 33 is the mirror-image promise: the person who has arranged his life as though there were an eye on him will be given the Garden that is being arranged for him.

**"The All-Merciful," Unseen**

The name chosen for the object of fear is *al-Raḥmān*, the All-Merciful — the name the Qur'an uses when it wants to insist on the reach of mercy before the reach of power, and it is the name with which the Qur'an opens the sūrah that is called by it: "the All-Merciful, taught the Qur'an" (55:1-2) — mercy and reminder joined, exactly as they are joined here. The pairing of "fear" with "the Merciful" is a deliberate dislocation, and it matches the sūrah's earlier reversal: the Threat and the Gift are from the same One, and the One who threatens is named by the attribute that outlasts the threat.

"Though unseen" — *bi-l-ghayb* — has two senses the source's note preserves, and the commentators accept both: fearing God while He is not seen, and worshipping God in a place where no one sees. The two are the same discipline. The Qur'an's own summary of the second is the definition of the believer: "who believe in the Unseen" (2:3); and the reward is stated in a verse the note cites here: "those who fear their Lord though they are unseen — for them is forgiveness and a great reward" (67:12). The structure of 67:12 is exactly the structure of our verse: unseen fear, then an answer no one could have calculated.

The hadith that the whole of this literature circles around is the Prophet's ﷺ one-line definition of excellence, in the narration of ʿUmar:

> "Excellence (*iḥsān*) is to worship God as though you see Him, for if you do not see Him, He sees you." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim)

Verse 33 is that ḥadīth in the sūrah's own vocabulary. The person who "fears the All-Merciful though unseen" is the person whose private worship has the quality of witnessed worship, and the phrase's second half — "He sees you" — is why the fear is not terror: the One who sees is the Merciful.

**"A Returning Heart": The Organ, Not the Effort**

*Qalb munīb* brings the sūrah back to its own centre of gravity. The word *munīb* is the same root as *ināba*, the "turning back" that the chapter has used for the servant of verse 8 and the *awwāb* of verse 32 — and here the grammar changes: it is not the person who is described as turning but his heart, as though the whole man were now named by the organ that made the movement. The source's note records the classical gloss exactly: "a heart that has turned away from what displeases God to what makes God content."

Two things follow from locating the change in the heart. The first is that the sūrah's moral psychology is not about willpower in the abstract; it is about reorientation, and reorientation is something that happens to a person when he attends to what he attends to. That is why this chapter has spent nine verses on the sky, the earth, the water and the palms before it said a word about the heart: the *dhikr* of verse 8 says that the created world is the instrument of reorientation, and "in each thing there is a sign, indicating that He is the One," the line the source quotes at verse 8, is the sūrah's compressed theory of how the heart turns.

The second is that a heart can be lost as well as found, and the Qur'an says so in the same register: "He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the breasts conceal" (40:19); "truly He knows what is in the breasts" (2:284; cf. 64:4); and "let them fear a Day in which hearts and sights are turned about" (24:37) — and the verse the sūrah has been driving at since verse 22: "so We removed from you your cover, so your sight today is piercing" (v. 22). The same eye that saw nothing of the sky in this world will see, on that Day, exactly what its heart was.

The Qur'an's other word for what is being asked here is a "sound" heart, and it appears in the most chilling of the sūrah's parallels:

> "The Day when wealth will not avail, nor children — but whoever comes to God with a sound heart (*qalb salīm*)." (Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:88-89)

Verse 33's "comes with a *munīb* heart" and that verse's "comes with a *ṣalīm* heart" are the same arrival described from two angles: *munīb* names the motion (turning back), *ṣalīm* names the condition (whole, free of the diseases the Qur'an calls *maraḍ al-qalb*). A heart can be simultaneously in motion and wounded; what the sūrah promises is the heart that has been healed by turning.

**Fearing in a World That Watches Nothing**

The verse's social edge deserves to be stated, because its audience was a society of reputations. The *ḥaram*, the tribes, the caravans, the assembly of Quraysh around the Kaʿba — everything in the hearers' world was regulated by being seen. Verse 33 abolishes the only standard that such a world cannot fake: it says that the decisive act is the one with no audience.

The practical consequence is the Qur'an's repeatedly stated preference for the concealed good: "if you disclose acts of charity, how excellent that is! And if you conceal them and give them to the poor, it is better for you, and He will remove from you some of your ills" (2:271); "and they give what they love to the poor, the orphan and the captive, saying: we feed you only for God's sake; we want from you neither thanks nor trouble" (76:8-9); and, of the other side, the man whose religion is an audience: "who are seen to be devout, and withhold the little kindnesses of daily use" (107:6-7). Verse 33 belongs to the first family of verses, and it is worth noting that its reward is a Garden brought near by God rather than a reputation sustained by people — the only transaction in the sūrah in which the return is greater than the outlay.

**The Verb of Coming**

The last word of the clause is *wa-jāʾa* — "and he came," a bare perfect verb with no object and no ornament, and it is the sūrah's final verb for the saved. Everything else has been done to them: the water was sent down, the land revived, the cover removed, the Garden brought near. Here, at the end, they come. The *ināba* of verse 8 and the return of verse 32 become, in verse 33, an arrival: the person whose defining habit was turning finds that the turning was, all along, a walking towards.

That is why the sūrah can leave the description there. A chapter whose first hearers asked "is this, when we are scattered dust, a return far off?" (v. 3) is answered, at last, with a single clause about the returner: he comes, with a heart that has been practising the arrival its whole life.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:34

> **Enter it in peace — this is the day of permanence."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Imperative**

The sūrah has issued two commands of this kind, and they are the chapter's moral poles. At verses 24 and 26 the voice said *iṭraḥā* and *fa-ṭriḥhu*: "throw, cast in" — the verb of handling refuse, of flinging something out of the way. Here it says *udkhulū bi-salāmin*: "enter, in peace." Two verbs, two directions of motion, and the distance between them is the whole chapter. The same One who commands the throwing also commands the welcoming, and the sūrah makes the hearer stand between the two sentences for exactly that reason: to feel that the difference between them is not a difference in the treatment but in the life.

The verse's opening imperative also completes the nearness announced at verse 31. The Garden was brought near; now the words are addressed to the ones for whom it was brought near. Nothing in the sentence describes what is inside it, because the sentence's whole work is to open the door.

**"Peace": The Word That Does Three Jobs**

*Salām* in this clause is simultaneously a greeting, a state, and a title, and the source's note registers the first two: "enter secure from every fear," or "enter with the greeting of peace." The third meaning is the sūrah's quiet addition, since *al-Salām* is itself a name of God in the Qur'an ("the King, the Holy, the Peace," 59:23), and the Garden is called "the Abode of Peace" — "God invites to the Abode of Peace and guides whom He wills to a straight path" (6:127); "for them is the Abode of Peace with their Lord, and He is their ally because of what they used to do" (10:25).

The greeting of the Garden is thus not a courtesy but a diagnosis: the word people have been waiting to hear is the word that says *nothing is wrong with you here, and nothing will be*. Every other sentence in this sūrah about the damned has been about a thing that went wrong and could not be undone; every sentence here is about the end of wrongness. That is why the Qur'an puts the word in the mouths of the angels at the moment of death as well: "those whom the angels take in while they are good, saying: peace upon you; enter the Garden for what you used to do" (16:32); and of the Day of the Heights, when even the boundary-dwellers greet: "peace be upon you" (7:46).

The formula appears elsewhere with the exact words of this verse attached, and the echo is worth hearing. In al-Ḥijr — the very valley whose people were named at verse 13 of this sūrah — the saved are told: "enter it in peace, secure" (15:46), and the next sentence tells them what the peace consists of: "and We will strip away whatever is in their breasts of rancour, brothers, facing one another on couches" (15:47). Verse 34's "peace" therefore does not mean quiet weather; in the Qur'an's own usage it means the removal of the last interior hostility, the curing of the breast, the end of keeping score.

**The Angels' Entry, and What It Adds**

The Qur'an describes the same welcome from the angels' side, and the description clarifies what this verse's brevity leaves out: "and the angels enter upon them from every gate: peace upon you, because you were steadfast. How excellent is the ultimate abode!" (13:23-24). Two details matter. The entry is "from every gate," so that no avenue of the person's life is left unaddressed; and the greeting is followed by a reason — "because you were steadfast" — which tells us that the peace is not a reprieve from what happened but a confirmation of what the person did with what happened. This sūrah has already made steadfastness its own ethic in a single verse: "and be patient over what they say" (v. 39). Verse 34 is what patience buys.

**"This Is the Day of Permanence"**

*Hādhā yawmu al-khulūd* — the verse names the day, not the garden, and the name is the theological claim. *Khulūd* is permanence, undisturbed continuation; the sūrah elsewhere attaches it to the same scene: "say: is that better, or the Garden of permanence which the reverent were promised, in which they will have whatever they will, abiding forever?" (25:15) — a verse whose wording ("in which they will have whatever they will") is the twin of our next verse, and whose comparative form ("is that better?") is the sūrah's own way of asking a hearer to weigh the two offers he is being made.

Permanence, in the Qur'an, is not simply duration. It is the abolition of the specific anxiety of this world, which is not shortness of time but the loss of things within it. The Qur'an gives the saved a summary of their relief in exactly those terms: "and they will say: praise belongs to God, who has removed sorrow from us; truly our Lord is Forgiving, Most Appreciative — who has settled us in an everlasting abode by His grace; no toil touches us therein, and no weariness touches us therein" (35:34-35). The two negatives at the end — no fatigue, no exhaustion — are the sūrah's own, and they will come back two verses later in a much stranger form: "no fatigue touched Us" (v. 38). The saved will be told that tiredness is not their problem, and the chapter will say of God, in the same words, that weariness is not His.

**A Day, Not a Place**

The verse's final decision is to call it a *day*. That is easy to miss and worth insisting on: the Qur'an's Paradise is habitually named by a day — "the Day when they will be gathered" (19:68), "the Day of Coming Forth" (50:42), "the Day of Reckoning" (the sūrah's own enumeration of the Day's names was surveyed at verse 20) — and never as a location the soul simply occupies. A day is an event with a beginning, and its character here is that its beginning coincides with the end of endings: verse 34's "day of permanence" is the first day of a series with no last term.

The consequence for the sūrah's argument is significant. The hearers of verse 3 objected to resurrection as an implausible *return* — a homecoming after dissolution, which they called "far." Verse 34 re-describes the return as an entry into permanence: what they mocked as a second time is in fact the abolition of time's depredations. The chapter that began by swearing by a "glorious Qur'an" ends its account of the saved with a sentence in which the word "day" has stopped meaning a period of twenty-four hours and has begun to mean a condition that will not lapse.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:35

> **Therein they shall have whatever they wish — and with Us there is more.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse with a Half in Reserve**

*Lahum mā yashāʾūna fīhā wa-ladayya mazīd* — the first clause is a complete sentence about a world; the second is an unfinished sentence about a relationship. The grammar itself performs the verse's argument: fulfilment inside the Garden, and then, standing outside the sentence's expectations, "and with Us there is more." The chapter's account of reward thus ends where human calculation ends, and it ends that way on purpose.

The source's note states what the commentators took the reserve clause to mean: "and with Us there is more indicates the vision of God, which is considered the greatest blessing, beyond all else, that one can desire in Paradise." The note adds the structural observation that makes this one of the most beautifully built passages in the sūrah: the "more" of the Garden is to be set against the "more" of Hell at verse 30 — "whereas the punishment of Hell is set and delimited, the rewards of the Garden are infinite."

**"Whatever They Wish": The Healed Will**

The phrase is the Qur'an's standard for the satisfaction of the saved, and its neighbours show how concretely it is imagined: "and fruit of what they select, and the meat of birds of what they desire" (56:20-21); "and they will be told: this is what you have inherited, for what you used to do — and they will have therein much fruit, of which they eat" (43:72-73). The verse in the sūrah of the Ha-Mīm-Sin-ʿAyn is especially close to ours in wording and in function, since it puts the sentence on the angels' lips at the moment the believer is dying: "they will have therein whatever their souls desire, and whatever they ask for — hospitality from a Most Forgiving, Most Merciful Lord" (41:31-32).

Read closely, however, "they have whatever they wish" does not describe the licence of an unchecked appetite. In the Qur'an the will of the saved is itself a gift, and the Book says so in a formula used both of this world and the next: "and you do not will except that God wills" (81:29; cf. 76:30). The Garden's fulfilment therefore presupposes a reshaped wisher. The classical reading of this verse — that the people of the Garden will not be tormented by unfulfilled desire, and will not be given things they no longer want — turns on exactly this point: satiation in the Qur'an is not the accumulation of objects but the healing of the appetite that needed them. That is why the sūrah can say "whatever they wish" without any sense of excess: nothing in the sentence flatters the wishes of the person who is healed.

**"And With Us": The Location of the Surplus**

*Mazīd* — "more, an increase" — has one other home in this sūrah: the Fire's own question, "is there more?" (v. 30). The single root thus frames the whole scene of judgement, and it divides the two destinations by a formal property rather than a quantitative one. Hell's "more" is a demand that will be answered and ended; the Garden's "more" is a gift that is simply announced, with no verb to tell us when it stops.

The phrase "with Us" (*ladayya*) is the other half of the verse's weight. The verse does not say "and there is more in it"; it says "with Us." The preposition, which appeared in verse 29 to speak of the Word that cannot be changed, here relocates the surplus from the Garden's inventory to God's own presence. The distinction is the whole of the classical commentary on this verse: within the Garden is what the soul wishes, and with God is what the soul was made for.

That is why the exegetes reached for the vision of God here, and why their reasoning is sound rather than ornamental. If the sūrah's first clause covers every object of desire, and the second clause adds something, the addition cannot be another object. The verse's own grammar forces the surplus to be of a different order, and the only candidate the sūrah has already supplied is nearness to the One who speaks — the same nearness, in the register of knowledge, that the chapter put at the centre of its anthropology: "and We are nearer to him than his jugular vein" (v. 16). The verse about being closer than the vein and the verse about what is "with Us" are, in this sūrah, the same claim at its two ends: already near in knowledge, nearer still in gift.

**The Qur'an's Other Word for the Surplus**

The nearest sibling of this clause is a single line in Sūrat Yūnus, and the commentators on our verse keep it in view because it supplies the same two-tier structure in one breath: "for the doers of good is the beautiful, and an addition (*ziyādah*); no darkness will cover their faces, nor humiliation — those are the companions of the Garden, abiding in it forever" (10:26). "The beautiful" is what they were promised; "the addition" is what they were not promised. And in the tradition the addition is glossed, again, as the vision: in the narration of Ṣuḥayb, when the people of the Garden have entered, God says to them: "do you wish Me to add anything for you?" — and nothing is given them more beloved than the lifting of the veil so that they look upon their Lord.

Whether one reads that tradition as exegesis or as theology, it is doing the work the verse invites: it refuses to let the last word about reward be a word about things. It is also the reason the clause can console a person who has lost real objects in this world — the sūrah promises that his want will be met, and that the meeting will not be the ceiling of what is coming.

**The Verse's Two Lessons for the Reader**

First, a lesson against ascetic contempt. The sūrah does not say the people of the Garden will stop wanting; it says their wanting will be satisfied. The chapter's ethic has been, from verse 8 onward, about the direction of desire rather than its abolition — the *awwāb* is a man who wants, and turns what he wants toward God. Verse 35 is the end of that sentence: desire, rightly trained, is what gets fulfilled.

Second, a lesson against the calculation of reward. The clause "and with Us there is more" makes arithmetic stop working at the last moment. A person who counts his good deeds in order to know what he is owed has, in the sūrah's language, misunderstood both the payment and the payer: the record of verses 17-18 is not a bill but a testimony, and the surplus of verse 35 is what no record can itemise. This is the point toward which the whole judgement scene has been moving — the cover is removed so that a person can see what he was, and then he is given more than what he was.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:36

> **How many a generation before them We destroyed, stronger than they in force! They probed the lands — is there any place of escape?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Turns Back to the Graveyard**

Verses 31-35 have brought the Garden near, opened its door, and promised a surplus no ledger can hold; and now, without transition, the chapter returns to the subject of verses 12-14: the destroyed nations. The juxtaposition is the sūrah's rhetoric at its most deliberate. The reader who has just been given an account of mercy is immediately given an account of the ones for whom mercy was available and was spent on other things.

The point of the return is not tonal but logical. Verses 12-14 established the historical precedent — denial has a track record and an outcome. Verse 36 supplies the missing term: outcome is not a function of capacity. The nations destroyed were *stronger* than the people being warned. If the stronger did not survive their denial, the argument that "we are the people of the Sanctuary, we are the tribe, we are the powerful" is finished before it starts.

**"Stronger Than They in Force"**

*Hum ashaddu minhum baʾṭan* — *baʾṭ* is the word for a hand's grip — taking hold of a thing and not letting go — and the Qur'an uses it of force exercised upon other people, so that the comparative here is not praise but indictment: they were better at hurting, and it did not save them. Elsewhere the Qur'an spells out the fate of that strength. ʿĀd "hew houses out of the mountains, feeling secure" (26:149; cf. 7:74), and were carried off by a wind; Pharaoh made light of his people and they obeyed him (43:54), and was drowned; Thamūd, settled in their stone homes, "became like the dry stubble of the pen-keeper" (54:29). The point of verse 36 is that this inventory of capacity is an inventory of the destroyed, and that the thing which decided their fate does not appear in the inventory at all. That is why the verse says "how many" (*kam*) rather than names them again: the number is left indefinite so that no hearer can comfort himself with being the exception.

**"They Probed the Lands": The Verb of the Search**

*Wa-nakhkhabū fī al-bilād* — the verb is from *naqb*, a hole, an aperture, and the derived verb means to probe, to search out, to examine something by making one's way through it; the same root gives *naqīb*, one who scrutinises. The image the verse creates is not of tourism but of a fugitive's reconnaissance: people going through the earth, tapping its surfaces, looking for a fissure, a route, a border that will take them out of what is coming to them.

Then the sūrah supplies the report of the search in a single question: *hal min maḥīṣ* — "is there any place of escape?" *Maḥīṣ* is a noun of fleeing and of the place one flees to, and the sūrah's word for the answer is not given. There is no reply, only the question, and that is the punishment. The Qur'an elsewhere states the same result in the past tense and without a question mark: "and the criminals will see the Fire and assume they are to fall into it, and they will find no way around it" (18:53); "and they will say: are there any intercessors for us, that they may speak to us?" (26:100); and, of the moment the search ends: "so God seized him with the punishment of the world and of the Hereafter. Indeed, in that is a lesson for whoever fears" (79:25-26).

Set verse 36's question against this sūrah's own earlier sentence about the man who hindered good and was cast in (vv. 24-26), and the two verses complete one another: the probe through the lands is the earthly life of the type described there — a life of searching for an exit that does not require turning.

**The Sūrah's Habit of the Rhetorical "How Many"**

This is not the only place the Qur'an uses *kam* to shatter a people's confidence in their own case. The source's note lists the family of formulas — "how many a generation We destroyed before them" — and among the closest in wording and purpose: "do they not see how many generations We destroyed before them, that they do not return to them?" (36:31); "do they not travel through the earth and see how was the end of those before them? They were stronger than they in power, and they cultivated the land and built it up more than these have built it" (30:9). The verse that our own is closest to, however, is in Ghāfir, and it repeats the two terms of this sūrah's sentence almost exactly — travel through the land, and greater strength: "do they not travel through the earth and see how was the end of those before them? They were stronger than they in might, and greater in traces in the earth; so God seized them for their sins, and there was for them none to shield them from God" (40:21). The "traces" in that verse — *āthār* — are what the Quraysh could touch on their caravan routes: the masonry of Thamūd, the columns of ʿĀd, the wells of al-Rass.

The verse's argument is therefore not "God destroys the strong" as a generalisation from history, but "you have the evidence in your own geography." The ruins are not decoration; they are the report of the search, filed by its failure.

**Why This Verse Comes Here**

A chapter that ends in a command to remind (v. 45) needs its hearers to understand what the reminder is against, and verse 36 states it: not weakness but impunity. The people addressed in this sūrah were not afraid of God because they were not, by their own account, afraid of anything — they had water, palms, sanctity, trade, and the graves of their fathers. Verse 36 says: that list is the inventory of the destroyed, and the destroyed were better supplied.

And after this verse the sūrah will do something no argument in the chapter has done yet: it will address the reader's equipment rather than his circumstances — "truly in that is a reminder for whoever has a heart" (v. 37). The sequence is exact. Once the lands have been probed and found to have no exit, the only remaining question is whether there is anything inside the person probing them that is capable of hearing the answer.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:37

> **Truly in that is a reminder for whoever has a heart, or lends his ear while he is present.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Names Its Own Audience**

*Inna fī dhālika li-dhikrā li-man kāna lahū qalbun aw alqā al-samʿa wa-huwa shahīd* — and the demonstrative "in that" gathers everything from verse 6 to verse 36: the sky without rifts, the water, the palms, the dead land, the destroyed nations, the record, the court, the Fire's question and the Garden's nearness. The verse says that all of it is a *dhikr*, a reminder — but only for a specified kind of person. That is the sūrah's most explicit statement about how its own address works. It has already sworn that the Book is a warning to a people who marvel (vv. 1-2), and it will end by commanding the Prophet ﷺ to remind with the Qur'an "whoever fears the Threat" (v. 45). In between, this verse installs the criterion: the reminder is not a magic of sound; it lands only where there is an organ to receive it.

The two conditions are stated as alternatives joined by "or" — a heart, or an attentive ear — and the grammarians note that the second is the weaker of the two, which is itself the point: even the person who has not yet got a heart's immediate vision, but who *listens* as though God were watching, is inside the address.

**"Whoever Has a Heart": An Idiom Used as a Theological Claim**

Arabic says of a person who grasps a thing that he "has a heart," and of one who acts like an animal that he has none; the source's note records the exegetes' gloss exactly: "Whoever has a heart that is aware, because if one's heart is not aware, it is as if he has no heart." Al-Ṭabarī's comment on the phrase — that a heart which is not awakened is as though it did not exist — makes the idiom into an ontology: the organ is defined by its waking, not by its presence.

This is the same verse-pair the Qur'an uses in its own argument from design: "do they not travel through the earth, so that they should have hearts to reason with, or ears to hear with? For it is not the eyes that are blinded, but the hearts which are in the breasts" (22:46). Read with our verse, 22:46 supplies both terms of the definition — hearts for *reasoning* and ears for *hearing* — and its punch line is the sūrah's whole case against verse 2's "wonder": the blindness in question is not sensory. The Quraysh saw the sky; what failed was the organ that sees *through* it.

The Qur'an also uses the negative of the same idiom as a diagnosis of the people it is addressing: "they have hearts with which they do not understand" (7:179, quoted above at verse 22); "God has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and over their sight is a veil" (2:7); and, most terribly, "so when they turned aside, God turned their hearts aside" (61:5). "Whoever has a heart" is therefore not a compliment to natural human sensitivity. In this book the heart is an organ that either wakes or is sealed, and the sūrah's nine verses of cosmology exist to wake it — which is precisely what verse 8 meant when it called the created world "a source of insight and a reminder for every penitent servant."

**"Lends His Ear": Presence as a Moral Faculty**

*Aw alqā al-samʿa* — literally "or casts the hearing," an idiom for attending with the whole mind; the source's note preserves the explanation from Zamakhsharī: "present with his intelligence, because whoever is not present in mind is as though he were absent." The sūrah is describing an act of attention, and it makes the act a condition of receiving revelation.

The contrast is drawn sharply elsewhere in the Qur'an, and always in the same terms: hearing without presence is not hearing at all. "Do not be like those who say: we hear, while they do not hear" (8:21); "among them are those who listen to you, until, when they go out from you, they say to those given the knowledge: what was that he just said? Those are they whose hearts God has sealed" (47:16); "and do not obey one whose heart We have made heedless of Our remembrance, who follows his own desire and whose affair is excess" (18:28). The sūrah's audience in Makkah was not, in the main, refusing to listen: they listened in the Ḥaram, they sent people to overhear the recitation, they debated it. Verse 37 identifies the missing term — *shahīd*, "while he is present" — and thereby converts listening from an acoustic event into a moral one.

There is an obvious reason this belongs at the end of a passage about records and witnesses. The chapter has just finished saying that everything is being attended to by watchers who miss nothing (vv. 17-18), that the cover will be lifted so that sight becomes piercing (v. 22), and that the Garden is brought near for the person who feared the All-Merciful unseen (v. 33). Verse 37 now describes the same quality in this world, in its secular form: to be a witness at one's own hearing. A person who cannot attend to a recitation will not attend to a judgement.

**"While He Is Present": The Third Reading and the Three Faculties**

The clause also has a second, stronger reading, which the source's note records: *wa-huwa shāhidun* — "and he is a witness," that is, a present, accountable observer rather than a passive receiver. On this reading, the verse assembles what the commentators describe as the three faculties by which understanding is achieved: the heart, the ear, and the eye of presence — precisely the equipment the Qur'an says God "produced for you": "and He brought you forth from the wombs of your mothers knowing nothing, and He made for you the hearing, the sight, and the hearts; seldom are you thankful" (16:78; cf. 32:9). Our verse is thus a mirror of the anthropology of verses 16-18: the faculties that are recorded when misused are the faculties by which the reminder is received.

The note also records the other two applications the commentators made of "and he is a witness": the People of the Book whose own scriptures bear witness to this prophecy, and — as a warning rather than a compliment — the hypocrite who hears the Qur'an and is a witness against himself by hearing it. The second of these is the more instructive, because it turns the verse's last word into a threat: presence means being present *as evidence*, and in this sūrah every witness is also witnessed.

**The Verse as a Verdict on Its Own Hearers**

It is worth stopping on the pastoral force of the sentence. A chapter that has described an enormous apparatus — cosmic, historical, judicial — and then says "in that is a reminder for whoever has a heart" has made a claim about the reader, not about the argument. The sūrah is not saying: here is proof, and the only obstacle is intelligence.
