It is saying: here is a reminder, and the only obstacle is a dead heart — and a dead heart is not an intellectual condition but a moral one, curable by the very attention the verse asks for. That is why the sūrah's next movement will not offer more evidence but a discipline: patience, praise at fixed hours, glorification in the dark (vv. 38-40).

The prophetic saying that sits behind this logic is one the commentators quote in exactly this context:

> "The likeness of the one who remembers his Lord and the one who does not remember Him is like the living and the dead." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī)

"To have a heart," in this sūrah, is to be alive; and the Book, as the Prophet ﷺ said of it, is "evidence for you or evidence against you" (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim) — a reminder, or an accusation, with nothing in between.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:38

> **And truly We created the heavens and the earth and what is between them in six days, and no weariness touched Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument Returns to Its Beginning, and Adds a Denial**

Verses 6-11 asked the hearers to look at what was made; verse 38 restates the same act as a single cosmic sentence, and then attaches to it a negative: *wa-mā massatanā min lughūb* — "and no weariness touched Us." The addition looks odd where an argument from design is being made. Why tell an audience that God was not tired?

The answer the classical exegetes give is in the source's note: the clause is "a response to the assertion that God rested on the seventh day after creating the earth and all that is in it." The sūrah is not only inviting inspection of the world; it is correcting a theology of the world it found in the bookish milieu of late antique Arabia — a Creator who completes a labour and is relieved by rest. Against that, the verse asserts the sheer untroubledness of the divine act: six days of making, and no fatigue.

The word *lughūb* is rare in the Qur'an and belongs to the vocabulary of exhaustion after effort — the same field as *ʿiyā*, and that is the sūrah's own earlier term. At verse 15 it asked the deniers of resurrection: "Did We tire with the first creation?" — the same denial in interrogative form. Verse 38 answers the question verse 15 only posed, and it does so in the first person plural, in the register of a royal declaration. The chapter's structure is tight here: what was raised as an objection to the *possibility* of the new creation is now closed by an assertion about the *ease* of the old one.

**The One Verse Some Counted Medinan**

Before the verse's content, its place in the chapter's history. The source's introduction records that all the commentators count Qāf Makkan, while "some maintain that v. 38 is from the Madinan period" — the only clause in the chapter for which such a view is reported, and it is a minority report without a documented occasion. Its plausible motive is visible in the verse itself: a correction of a claim about God's resting after creation would sit naturally in the argument with the People of the Book in Madinah. But the verse's function here is inseparable from its context — it is the premise of "so bear patiently what they say" in the next verse, addressed to a preacher worn out by a hostile Makkan audience — and the verse-counts transmitted by the readers do not treat it as an insertion. The chapter's structure is Makkan throughout, and the report is recorded here because a careful reader of the sūrah will meet it in the classical introductions.

**"Six Days": The Qur'an's Own Cosmogony**

The six-day formula is one of the most repeated claims in the Book: "your Lord is God, who created the heavens and the earth in six days, then settled upon the Throne" (7:54; cf. 10:3; 11:7; 25:59; 32:4; 57:4), and in one place the Qur'an adds the state of things before the making: "and His throne was upon the water" (11:7). The sūrah of the Prostration supplies the arithmetic that defuses a literalist reading of "day": "He arranges the matter from the heaven to the earth; then it ascends to Him in a Day whose measure is a thousand years of what you count" (32:5; cf. 22:47), and "the angels and the Spirit ascend to Him in a Day whose measure is fifty thousand years" (70:4).

The point the commentators draw from these is not that the six days are "really" long epochs — a modern apologetic move rather than a classical one — but that the units cannot be solar days at all, since the sun is part of what was made. The word *yawm* in the Qur'an names a period of appointed length, whether of human reckoning or of God's, and the sūrah that says "your Lord's Day is a thousand years" cannot be pressed into measuring God's week by our calendars. This is one of the few places where the classical exegesis is straightforwardly more careful than both literalism and its modern opposite: the number is asserted, its unit is not.

The tradition preserves a narration, transmitted by Muslim, that allocates the acts of creation to the days of our week — clay on Saturday, mountains on Sunday, trees on Monday, and Adam in the last hours of Friday. The hadith masters record it, the exegetes quote it, and its function is instructive rather than scientific: it puts the making of the world inside the rhythm of a week, which is the same rhythm the next verses will make the Prophet ﷺ keep with prayer.

**Why Weariness Is Denied and Not Merely Absent**

The clause's force lies in the verb: fatigue did not "touch" God. The Qur'an's phrasing is deliberately corporeal, and its denial is comprehensive — touching is the least a weariness could do to a body. Around this, the Book states its whole doctrine of divine self-sufficiency in a handful of verses: "God — there is no god but He, the Living, the Everlasting; neither slumber nor sleep seizes Him" (2:255); "do they not consider that God, who created the heavens and the earth and did not tire in creating them, is able to give life to the dead? Yes" (46:33); Our verse states the same claim in the first person, and with the added dignity of the plural of majesty: what was said there in the third person is here asserted by God about Himself.

Two things follow that matter for the sūrah's argument. First, resurrection is included in the sentence: "and what is between them" covers the human being, body and history, so that the verse's denial of weariness is the direct answer to verse 15's sneer about a "new creation." Second, the denial makes creation an act without cost: "Our command, when We will a thing, is only that We say to it 'Be,' and it is" (36:82); "His command is but a single blink of the eye" (54:50). If the making of the heavens cost nothing, the remaking of a corpse is not a second, harder labour — it is the same ease.

**"And What Is Between Them": The Hearer Included**

The middle term of the verse's triad — *wa-mā baynahumā* — is easy to skim, and it is where the sūrah's pressure is applied. The heavens and the earth are the two grand halves of the argument from verses 6-11; "what is between them" is everything in the between: the water and the palms, the generations destroyed and the generation addressed, the whisper in the chest and the angel on the right hand, the agony of death and the lifted cover. All of that is included in the act for which no weariness was, and all of it is included in the recreation the hearers find incredible.

The source's note at verse 15 quotes the embryological passage of al-Ḥajj against the deniers of the new creation, and it belongs here too: the One who made you "from dust, then from a drop, then from a clot" (22:5) was not tired then, and the verse says He is not tired now. Between the heavens and the earth there is also this: a person who has been made twice and objects to being made a third time.

**The Verse as the Ground of the Next Command**

The most consequential thing about verse 38 is its "so" — the consecutive particle that opens verse 39: "therefore be patient." The connection is not decorative. The logic is: if the making of the world cost Us nothing, then the salvation of your people does not depend on your exhaustion. The sūrah's prophet is a man being devoured by the failure of his mission ("perhaps you will waste yourself away over them, if they will not believe," 26:3; "do not consume yourself in sorrow over them" 35:8), and verse 38 is the theological answer: the God who did not tire in creating the heavens does not require you to tire in the impossible task of compelling them, and He has not entrusted their hearts to you.

That is the reason the chapter, having described a creation made without effort, immediately prescribes a worship performed at fixed, unhurried hours. Effortless making is answered by unanxious praise.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:39

> **So bear patiently with what they say, and glorify with the praise of your Lord before sunrise and before sunset.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"So": The Sūrah's Prescription**

The chapter has spent thirty-six verses making an argument, and its turn to the Prophet ﷺ is the shortest prescription in the Qur'an: *fa-ṣbir* — be patient; *wa-sabbih bi-ḥamdi rabbika* — and glorify, with praise. Nothing about the opponents is added, nothing about the punishment is repeated. What is commanded is endurance and doxology, and the pairing is a fixed idiom of this Book: "so be patient with what they say, and glorify the praise of your Lord before the rising of the sun and before its setting, and of the hours of the night glorify, and at the ends of the day, so that you may be satisfied" (20:130) — a verse so close to ours that the commentators treat the two as one passage, and its last words ("so that you may be satisfied") tell us the purpose of the discipline: not information, but contentment.

That is a strange and beautiful aim for a chapter that has been about a Threat. The sūrah's final movement will not be another warning but the settlement of the warner himself.

**Patience as the Refusal to Be Ruined by Words**

*Fa-ṣbir ʿalā mā yaqūlūn* — the object of patience is not persecution but *speech*: "what they say." The sūrah has quoted that speech seven times (vv. 2, 3, 7, 12-15, 24-26, 36), and it is a catalogue of insults and objections: sorcery, a liar, poetry, "what, when we are dust, is that a return far off?" The command is to be patient specifically over being talked about.

The Qur'an's other instructions on this point are unusually honest about the psychology of a prophet: "and be patient, and your patience is only with God; and do not grieve over them, and do not be constricted because of what they scheme" (16:127); "so be patient as were the messengers of resolve, and do not be in haste for them" (46:35); "and be patient for the judgement of your Lord, for you are before Our eyes" (52:48). From these the ethic of this verse can be stated in three parts: patience is possible only with God's help, it is not the same as approval, and it is undertaken by someone who is being watched over. "You are before Our eyes" is the verse's own support for the sūrah's command — the same guarantee as "We are nearer to him than his jugular vein" (v. 16), now turned from a statement about knowledge into a statement about care.

And the thing patience refuses, in this sūrah's terms, is the cover of verse 22: the impatient man is the man whose attention has been seized by what is said about him.

**Glorification as the Antidote to Dwelling on Words**

The source's note makes the point about the second command exactly: "the command to glorify the praise of your Lord indicates that one should not dwell upon the words of disbelievers, but instead turn to God." The verb *sabbaha* is not "pray" in the ordinary sense but "to declare God remote from what is unfitting," and the phrase *sabbih bi-ḥamdihī* joins two acts in one: the negation of the defect and the affirmation of the praise. The Prophetic wording of the same combination, in a ḥadīth of the two light phrases that weigh heavily, is:

> "Two words that are light on the tongue and heavy in the Balance, and beloved to the All-Merciful: glory be to God and praise be to Him; glory be to God, the Mighty." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra)

That tradition is the best available commentary on the mechanics of verse 39: the treatment for being wounded by speech is not a better argument but a louder praise, and the sūrah's prescription is thus the most practical of the chapter's verses. It says, in effect: when the objection is "is this a return far off?" (v. 3), do not argue; glorify.

**Before Sunrise and Before Sunset: The Two Appointments**

The times are named by their function, not by their ritual titles, and the source's note records the two readings of what is meant: for al-Ṭabarī, "before the rising of the sun" is the dawn prayer and "before the setting" the late afternoon prayer, while others take the second as covering both afternoon prayers. The command's form fits either: what the sūrah does is fence the day at both ends, and the effect of that fencing is a life whose first and last acts of attention are praise.

Two other verses of the Book supply the frame. "And perform the prayer at the two ends of the day and in the approaches of the night; the good deeds take away the evil deeds" (11:114); and "establish the prayer from the sun's decline until the darkness of the night, and the Qur'an at dawn — truly the Qur'an of the dawn is witnessed" (17:78). The witnessed dawn is the sūrah's own image for what it means to be *shāhid* (v. 37): the recitation at daybreak is one at which God's angels are present as witnesses, and the verse that speaks of "whoever lends his ear while he is present" belongs naturally with a prayer at which presence is guaranteed by others.

And the verse's most famous application comes from the ḥadīth the sūrah's commentators keep beside it, in which the Prophet ﷺ used this very verse as the warrant for not missing two prayers:

> "You will see your Lord as you see this moon, and you will not be crowded out of seeing Him; and if you are able not to be outdone by a prayer before the rising of the sun and a prayer before its setting, do so." Then he recited: "and glorify the praise of your Lord before the rising of the sun and before its setting." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī, from Jarīr ibn ʿAbdullāh al-Bajalī)

Read in this sūrah's sequence, that hadith is the key to the whole closing movement. Verse 35 promised "with Us there is more," and the note there recorded the commentators' reading: the vision of God. Verse 39, on this authority, is then the chapter's practical sentence: the discipline that trains a person for the sight of God is the discipline of showing up at the two edges of the day, before the world's noise has begun and before it has ended.

**"With the Praise of Your Lord"**

One last detail of the wording deserves notice: the praise belongs to the Lord, not to the worshipper. *Wa-sabbih bi-ḥamdi rabbika* — "glorify, with Your Lord's praise" — is read by the commentators either as an accompaniment (glorify while praising) or as an asking (glorify with the help and the enablement your Lord gives you). The second sense suits a chapter that has just said creation cost God no fatigue and that verse 39's patience "is only with God" (16:127): a person cannot even supply his own praise. The sūrah of Victory, which the note cites alongside this verse, states the same thing as a rite: "glorify the praise of your Lord, and ask His forgiveness" (110:3) — negation, praise, and the request for the strength to do both, in three clauses at the end of a prophet's life.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:40

> **And in some of the night glorify Him, and after the prostrations.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Schedule Completed**

Verse 39 fenced the day at its two edges; verse 40 adds the hours that are left — "and in some of the night," and then a clause whose meaning has occupied the jurists for fourteen centuries: "and after the prostrations." The two verses together give the sūrah's entire prescription for the human being: patience, praise at dawn, praise at dusk, glorification in the night, and glorification when the prayer is over. Nothing is said about victory, about the destruction of the opponents, or about the prophet's hurt. The chapter's answer to a hostile city is a timetable.

That is not an evasion. The sūrah has spent verses 6-11 arguing that the world is a reminder, and verses 16-36 describing a record, a court and two destinations. What remains to be said about a person who has understood all that is: keep to the hours. The knowledge the chapter offers is not information to be held but a rhythm to be kept, and the rhythm is the shape of a heart that has been reminded (v. 37).

**"And After the Prostrations": What the Clause Commanded**

The source's note records the range of readings, and the range is instructive. For some the clause means the obligatory night prayer; for others both sunset and night prayers; for al-Qurṭubī's sources it is the two cycles before dawn. The phrase "after the prostrations" is taken by some as a command to glorify God after each prescribed prayer — the practice of the tasbīḥ that every school of law eventually built into the conclusion of the rites — and Ibn ʿAbbās is reported in the note as reading the parallel clause of the sūrah of the Mount ("and at some of the night glorify Him, and when the stars recede," 52:49) as the two cycles before the dawn prayer, with "after the prostrations" as the two cycles after the sunset prayer.

Two facts stand behind the disagreement, and both are more important than the technical question of which prayer is meant. First, the sūrah's vocabulary is *tasbīḥ*, not *ṣalāh* — glorification rather than the rite by name — which is why the verse can be read either as a further prayer or as what is said at the end of one. Second, whatever the identification, the verse generated an observable practice: the Muslims of the first generations did in fact remain seated in their places of prayer after the salām, glorifying, and they did it on the authority of verses like this one.

The ḥadīth literature preserves the words they said, and their shape is exactly the sūrah's:

> "Glory be to God thirty-three times, praise be to God thirty-three times, God is greatest thirty-three times, and at the end of the hundred say: there is no god but God alone, with no partner; His is the dominion and His is the praise, and He has power over all things — it is forgiven him whose sins are like the sand of the sea." (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Kaʿb ibn ʿUjra)

And in the Prophet's ﷺ instruction to Muʿādh ibn Jabal, taken as a covenant to be kept after every prayer: "do not fail to say at the conclusion of every prayer: O God, help me to remember You, to thank You, and to worship You well" (Sunan Abī Dāwūd and Sunan al-Nasāʾī).

**The Night Hours, and Why They Count**

The sūrah's turn from the day's edges into the night is the Qur'an's most familiar movement, and the source's note lists the verses that make it a doctrine: "and in some of the night, be awake for it as an additional devotion for you; perhaps your Lord will raise you to a praiseworthy station" (17:79); "indeed, the rising by night is more forceful and truer in utterance" (73:6); "they used to sleep but little of the night, and in the hours before dawn they would ask forgiveness" (51:17-18); and, of the servants of the All-Merciful, "and those who pass the night for their Lord, prostrating and standing" (25:64).

Two of these deserve to be read against our verse. The "truer in utterance" of 73:6 names the reason the night is chosen: in the dark there is no audience, and so the speech is undivided — which is precisely the quality verse 33 required of the saved ("whoever fears the All-Merciful though unseen"). And 25:64 places *sujūd* in the same verse that ends with "peace": "and when the ignorant address them, they say: peace; and those who pass the night for their Lord prostrating and standing." Our sūrah has just commanded glorification "after the prostrations" (v. 40) and promised entry "in peace" (v. 34); the two qualities, night prostration and a peaceful tongue, belong together in the Qur'an's own account of the same people.

**"You Are Before Our Eyes": The Angels in Shifts**

The verse's mention of the night also returns the hearer to the sūrah's own anthropology. At verse 17 the recording angels were described as "receiving, seated on the right hand and seated on the left," and the sūrah's word for the watcher at verse 18 is accompanied in the chapter by the root *ʿ-q-b*, from which the classical lexicons derive the alternation of the angels in relays (*ʿuqab*) — one company replacing another. To that background the prophetic tradition adds the detail that explains why the dawn prayer in particular is called "witnessed":

> "The angels of the night and the angels of the day gather at the dawn prayer. Then the angels of the night ascend, and God asks them — and He knows best: 'How did you leave My servants?' They say: we left them while they were praying, and we came upon them while they were praying." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Hurayra)

That is the same verse 17-18 apparatus seen from the other side. The watchers who record every word in the day alternate in the night, and the prayer at the meeting of the two shifts is the one "witnessed" (17:78). Verse 40's command to glorify "in some of the night" therefore has, in the sūrah's own system, a consequence: the hours when nobody is watching are the hours when everybody is.

**The Universe That Praises, and the Person Who Joins In**

The last word to be said about this pair of verses is the theological one, and the Qur'an states it in a single line: "do you not see that to God prostrates whoever is in the heavens and whoever is on the earth, and the sun and the moon and the stars, and the mountains and the trees and the creatures of men?" (22:18); "and the stars and the trees both prostrate" (55:7); "there is not a thing but glorifies His praise, but you do not understand their glorification" (17:44).

Verse 38 said the heavens and the earth were made in six days without fatigue; verses 39-40 say: now put yourself into the same activity at the same hours. The praise of the dawn and the dusk is not a supplementary devotional exercise tacked onto a cosmological argument — it is the human share in a doxology the chapter has already attributed to the mountains (38:18) and to every existing thing. What the sūrah asks of the tired, insulted prophet is that he stop trying to be the author of the universe's praise and become one voice in it. That, and not a refutation, is what the chapter promises will make him "satisfied" (20:130).

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:41

> **And listen on the Day the Caller calls from a place close by.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Imperative of Attention, at Last**

*Wa-astamiʿ* — "and listen." After a verse and a half of praise, the sūrah issues a command about hearing, and the choice of verb is exact: *istimāʿ* is deliberate, willed listening, the act of inclining the ear with intention. The chapter has already made this the condition of receiving anything at all: "a reminder for whoever has a heart, or inclines the ear while he is a witness" (v. 37). Now the same faculty is put to its final use, and the imperative's point is that this time there is no option in the matter — the listening will happen.

The verse thus closes a thread that runs from the chapter's beginning. The disbelievers of Makkah were described as people who heard and did not attend: "and among them are those who listen to you, until, when they go out, they say: what was that he just said? Those are they whose hearts God has sealed" (47:16); "they are like livestock — nay, they are further astray in path" (25:44). Verse 41 answers with a Day on which the call reaches every ear without a veil, and it is not an argument against them but a description of the terminus of their present condition.

**"The Caller": The Voice With No Message**

The sūrah does not say who calls; it says only "the Caller" (*al-munādī*), and the source's note records the commentators' identifications: the blowing of the trumpet by the angel entrusted with it, and the view that while one angel blows, another does the calling. As at verse 20 of this sūrah, the naming of the angel rests on tradition rather than on revelation, and the important thing is the grammar: the caller has no speech attached to him here. No argument is offered on that Day, no invitation extended, no negotiation. The verse says "the Caller calls from a place close by," and the content of the call is simply the summons itself.

The Qur'an's other portraits of that moment keep the same spareness: "the Day they hear the Cry with truth — that is the Day of Coming Forth" (50:42, the next verse); "they follow the Caller, with no crookedness in it, and voices are hushed before the All-Merciful, so you hear nothing but a whisper" (20:108); "then what will make you know what the Day of Judgement is — a Day when no soul will possess anything for another soul, and the command on that Day belongs to God" (82:18-19); "the Day they come forth from the graves swiftly, as though hastening to a target, their eyes humiliated, abasement covering them, and that is the Day they were promised" (70:43-44). The last of these is the closest to our verse's image: a people running toward a voice, not because they have been persuaded but because there is nowhere else.

**"From a Place Close By": The Final Refutation of Verse 3**

The sūrah's nearness motif, which we have watched since verse 6, arrives here at its destination. *Min makānin qarīb* — and the source's note preserves two ways the commentators took the phrase, both of them about access rather than decibels. Either the call comes from a place near the heaven — and Jerusalem is named, the place of the gathering, the site to which the earth will be changed: "the Day when the earth is changed to another earth, and the heavens, and they come forth before God, the One, the Subduer" (14:48); or the call comes from near the ones called — "from beneath their feet," as the note records the second reading, so that the summons rises from the very ground of the graves.

Either way the verse is answering the sūrah's first scoff. Verse 3 quoted the men of Makkah: "this is a wondrous thing! When we are dead, we shall become scattered? That is a return far off." What the whole chapter has argued is that the distance they posited is in their own hearts (v. 37), not in the facts (vv. 6-11). Now the return is given a location, and it is the location of the voice: "from a place close by." The sūrah's answer to "far" is not a rebuttal but a distance measurement: nearer than the jugular vein (v. 16), not distant at all (v. 31), and here, from a near place.

There is also a horror in the nearness. What is close cannot be escaped, cannot be out-waited, cannot be negotiated with at a distance; and the note's reading "from beneath their feet" puts the summons where the corpse is. The sūrah's God does not call across a void.

**"Listen": The Verb Addressed to the Prophet ﷺ**

One feature of the sentence is easy to lose: the imperative is addressed to the one who conveys the message, not to its opponents. The structure of these two verses is thus: *you* listen (v. 41a), *they* will hear (v. 42). The Prophet ﷺ is commanded to attend with his heart now, to the recitation, while the hearing of that Day is left to be endured by the people who did not attend then. This is the sūrah's last word about the difference between the two modes of hearing, and it is the practical conclusion of the whole chapter: revelation is the near call heard at a distance, and resurrection is the far call heard close.

That is why the pairing of verses 39-41 reads as it does — patience, praise, then "listen." The discipline of the hours is what trains an ear for a call that will otherwise be unbearable.

**"Listen": The Two Grammars of the Command**

The verse's first word can be attached in two ways, and the source's note preserves both. Read with the preposition of time — "listen on the Day" — the sentence is a warning about a future event. But the note records also the reading "listen to My Words on the Day," and Zamakhsharī's gloss takes the verb as governing the whole account: the Day, its caller, its nearness and its outcome are a description to which one attends *now*, because that is the only way anyone will be able to attend on the Day itself. The second reading is the more severe, since it turns an eschatological picture into an instruction about this hour's recitation: the hearing of the last Day is rehearsed in the hearing of this sūrah.

That is also why the imperative is directed at the messenger rather than the crowd. Elsewhere the Qur'an tells the Prophet ﷺ that his part is the delivery: "upon you is only the delivery, and upon Us is the reckoning" (13:40); "and if you are able to seek a hole in the earth or a ladder to the sky and bring them a sign — had God willed, He would have gathered them all to guidance" (6:35). Verse 41 adds what the delivery is for: an ear, opened in advance. The verse's brevity — a command, a caller, a place — is the point of the whole scene: no detail is needed, because the only question the Day will ask of anyone is whether he was listening when the words were near.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:42

> **The Day they hear the Cry with truth — that is the Day of Coming Forth.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Day Named by Its First Event**

*Dhālika yawmu al-khurūj* — "that is the Day of Going Forth." The sūrah gives the Hour one of its catalogue of names, and the name is a verb of motion: not the Day of Judgement, not of Recompense, not of Distress, but of *coming out*. What is emphasized is the first act, not the last — the exit from the graves, the leaving of the position, the end of the fixed place each body had been given in the earth. The whole chapter has been describing a world in which nothing moves except by God's command, and it ends its account of the last things with the single motion every person will make without being able to decline it.

That naming also completes the sūrah's structure of distance. Verse 3 called the return "far"; verse 31 brought the Garden "not distant"; verse 41 put the caller "in a near place"; verse 42 has the people come out. The chapter's argument about space is finished: everything was near all along, and the only distance was the one a person put between himself and what he had been shown.

**"With Truth": The Sūrah's Last Thread**

The Cry is heard *bi-l-ḥaqq* — "with truth," and the same phrase appeared at verse 19 of this sūrah: "and the agony of death has come with truth" — *wa-jāʾat sakratu al-mawti bi-l-ḥaqq*. That is the sūrah's own commentary on our clause: the death each person has already met, in a form he could not talk his way out of, and the summons he will meet, are the same kind of event — an arrival "with truth," carrying its own reality and no persuasion.

The word *ḥaqq* is the thread on which the chapter has been hung from the start. The Book is "the clear" (v. 2 — "nay, they wonder that a warner has come to them," and the sūrah's opening swore that the revelation is not confounded but *majīd*, glorious, v. 1); creation is made "in truth" (6:73; 45:22 — "God created the heavens and the earth in truth, and every soul will be repaid for what it earned, and they will not be wronged"); the driver and witness come "with truth" (v. 21); the cover is removed so that sight is "piercing" (v. 22); and here, at the end, the Cry is heard "with truth." Against all of that stands the single adjective the sūrah chose for its opponents in verse 5: *marīj* — confounded, mixed up, "completely mixed so that its true nature cannot be discerned," as the source's note explains the word. The chapter is, in one of its deep structures, the story of two states: the world as *ḥaqq*, and the human affair as *marīj*. Verse 42 is the moment the second is dissolved into the first, and that is why "truth" is the adverb of the Cry rather than a description of its content: it will not be *about* what is true; it will *be* true.

**"The Cry": The Sound That Ends Argument**

The word *al-ḥaqqata* here — "the Cry," the "assault" of sound, in the cognate form used by the sūrah named after it: "the Striking Reality — and what will make you know what the Striking Reality is? Thamūd and ʿĀd denied the Stunning Blow" (69:1-4). The root gives the Hour a noun that means the thing which is real in the sense of being inescapable: what the Qur'an elsewhere calls *al-wāqiʿa*, "that which comes to pass, and there is no liar to its lying" (56:1-2), and *al-ṭāmma al-kubrā*, "the Great Overwhelming" (79:34).

Set against verse 20 of this sūrah, the two verses complete one another. There the trumpet was blown and the Day called "the Day of the Threat" (*yawmu al-waʿīd*); here the Cry is heard and the Day called "the Day of Coming Forth." The threat and the exit are one event seen from its two sides: as a word already sent (verse 28: "I have sent the Threat to you"), and as a step now taken. That is the sūrah's final statement of what it means for a warning to be true — not that it is confirmed after the fact, but that the thing warned of is the thing that arrives.

**Hearing, Again: The Chapter's Last Lesson About the Ear**

The verse's first words are "the Day they hear," and the emphasis falls on the verb rather than on what is heard. This is the answer to the sūrah's own verse 37, which made "inclining the ear" the one condition under which the reminder profits anybody. What the chapter now says is that hearing will not remain a condition; it will become a state. The ear that was refused in the mosque by the Ḥaram's listeners, and that "casts the hearing" only from the sincere, is given its object without its freedom.

The tradition's most concentrated statement of that reversal is the parable the Prophet ﷺ gave of the Qur'an's audience in his own city, and the sūrah has been quoting it all along without naming it: "the likeness of what I have been sent with, of guidance and knowledge, is like rain which falls upon the earth; and a portion of it is fertile, which receives the water and produces herbage in abundance, and a portion of it is firm, which holds the water, by which God benefits people, who drink from it, irrigate with it and sow; and a portion of it is barren, which holds no water and yields no herbage. That is the likeness of the one who understands God's religion and benefits from what I was sent with, and the likeness of the one who does not raise his head for it and does not accept God's guidance which I was brought with." (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī and Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, from Abū Mūsā)

Verse 42 is the rain falling on the third ground.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Hinge**

Two verses remain before the chapter closes, and they are among its gentlest: "We give life and We give death, and to Us is the return" (v. 43), and the earth splitting to let them out, "a gathering easy for Us" (v. 44). Verses 41-42 are the hinge that makes those possible: having named the Call and the Coming Forth, the sūrah can at last say the sentence it has been building toward since verse 11 — "even so is the coming forth" (*kadhālika al-khurūj*). That verse, on the revival of dead land, promised the resurrection as a corollary of agriculture; verse 42 now gives the same word its Day-name, and the chapter can therefore end not with a threat but with a commission to remind.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:43

> **It is We who give life and who cause death, and to Us is the return.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Shortest Summary of the Chapter's Whole Case**

*Innā naḥnu nuḥyī wa-numītu wa-ilaynā al-maṣīr* — two verbs and one prepositional phrase, and they state everything the sūrah has spent forty-two verses assembling. The hearers had objected to resurrection; the chapter answered with sky, water, palms, revived soil, the destroyed nations, the record, the court, the Fire's question and the Garden's nearness, the caller and the coming forth. Then it compresses the argument into a sentence a child can memorise: We are the ones who give life and cause death, and the journey ends with Us.

The compression is itself the proof, in the sūrah's manner. A claim that requires forty-two verses of evidence in one mouth requires only two verbs in the mouth of the One who is speaking here, and the shift of voice from the third person of argument to the first person of authority is the chapter's last and most decisive rhetorical act.

**Present Tense: Life and Death as an Ongoing Act**

The verbs are imperfect — *nuḥyī wa-numītu* — "We give life, and We keep causing death" — and the tense carries the verse's theology. Arabic's present form denotes what is repeated and what continues, so the sentence is not a report about two past acts (creating, then killing) but a description of what God is doing: the giving and taking of life is an unbroken present. The Qur'an says so elsewhere in a single line: "He brings the living out of the dead and brings the dead out of the living" (6:95); and, in the sūrah's own interrogative style: "who gives life to the earth after its death?" (30:50; cf. 43:11, "and We sent down water in measure, and We settled it in the earth, and We are able to take it away"), and "whoever We give life to, We make him return to the least of creation" (36:68).

This is the verse's quiet answer to verse 15's question — "Did We tire with the first creation? Nay, but they are in confusion about a new creation" — because on this description there is no "first" and "new" in God's grammar. There is only giving life and taking it, continuously, and the resurrection is one more item in the same open series. The confusion of the deniers, the sūrah says, is that they treated creation as a completed job.

**"To Us Is the Return": A Destination That Is a Person**

The last phrase is the one the Qur'an repeats most in its own summaries — "and to God the matters are returned" (2:210); "to your Lord is the limit" (96:8); "and He it is who originates creation and repeats it, and it is easiest for Him" (30:27) — but the source's note makes an exact observation about our verse: of the many verses that speak of the return to God, this is the only one where the destination is put in the first person plural, "unto Us." The detail matters. The sūrah's "We" has been the voice of sending down water, of stretching the earth, of creating in six days without fatigue; the same "We" now owns the destination. The return is not to a place, not to a state, not to an abstract account: it is "to Us," and the chapter has spent its whole length describing what an "Us" is — the One who is nearer than the jugular vein, and who does not wrong the servants.

To that the verse's companions add the two things the sūrah has insisted on all along. That the return is to *Him* means it is to a presence, not an oblivion: "He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the breasts conceal" (40:19). And that it is to *Us* means it is to a judge who has already declared His terms: "the Word is not changed with Me, and I do not wrong the servants" (v. 29).

**The Death That Is Not an Interruption**

Read as a sentence about the world's actual texture, the verse says that dying is inside God's activity, not outside it — a thing He does, not a thing that happens to Him or to us in spite of Him. The Qur'an's other formulations of this are severe and consoling at once: "every soul will taste death; then to Us you will be returned" (3:185); "and no soul dies except by God's permission, in a record appointed" (3:145); "God takes the souls at the hour of their death" (39:42); and of the moment itself in the sūrah of Ascent: "no — when it reaches the throat, and it is said: who can treat? and he knows it is the parting, and the leg is wound about with leg — to your Lord, on that Day, is the destination" (75:26-30).

Our sūrah's verse 19 was that passage's twin: "and the agony of death comes with truth." Verse 43 now names what is on both sides of the agony: the Giver, and the destination. Nothing in the sentence softens death; everything in it locates it.

**The Inner Reading: Hearts Raised, Souls Buried**

Because the verbs are present and continuous, the verse has also always been read as a statement about spiritual life and death, and the source's note preserves the finest of those readings, from Ismāʿīl Ḥaqqī Burusawī's commentary:

> "We give life to hearts that are dead and cause carnal souls that are alive to die. And unto Us is the journey's end for one whose carnal soul has died and whose heart is alive."

The reading is not an escape from the verse's plain sense but a second layer of the same claim, and the sūrah itself has prepared it: the man without a heart is dead (v. 37), the heedless man is described by the Qur'an as one who cannot come forth from darkness (6:122: "is one who was dead and We gave him life and made for him a light by which he walks among the people, like the one whose likeness in the darknesses is that he cannot come forth from them?"), and the Garden's entry is for the one whose heart came alive and turned (v. 33). Ismāʿīl Ḥaqqī's "the carnal soul died and the heart lived" is the sūrah's own definition of the *awwāb ḥāfiẓ* of verse 32, described in the vocabulary of death and life instead of the vocabulary of return and guard.

What the two readings share is the denial that death is finally a matter of bodies. The chapter's opponents were arguing about bones and dust (v. 3); the verse tells them that the God they are mocking is the one who is giving and taking life right now, in the very organs with which they are mocking Him.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:44

> **The Day the earth splits them open, and they hasten forth. That is a gathering easy for Us.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Earth as the Last Witness**

*Yawma tashaqqaqu al-arḍu ʿanhum siʿāran* — the verse's image is not of a resurrection from nowhere but of an ejection from somewhere: the ground, in which the bodies have been deposited, bursts open away from them and lets them out, and the word for their exit is the ordinary word for running (siʿāran), so that the earth and the people on it are described in a single motion, the ground opening and the dead streaming. The Qur'an's cognate images are equally physical: "and the earth stretches out and casts off what is within it and becomes empty" (84:3-4); "they come forth from the graves swiftly, as though hastening to a target" (20:104; 70:43); "and when the trumpet is blown, at once they come from their graves toward their Lord, sliding" (36:51).

The sūrah has already said who will be inside that ground and what will be said there: "We know what the earth consumes of them, and with Us is a preserving Book" (v. 4). Verse 44 is the moment the earth is asked to hand over what it has been eating, and the whole argument of verses 3-4 resolves into a single image: the decomposition the hearers pointed to as proof of impossibility is described as a deposit with a receipt.

Why, then, does only the earth get a verb here, when the Qur'an's other scenes of the end have the sky split, the mountains levelled and the seas set on fire (22:1-2; 52:1-6; 69:13-15; 81:1-6; 82:1-3)? The source's note answers: because the verse is about the bodies, and the bodies are in the ground. The chapter's eschatology stays physical and local to the last — the same realism with which it began by swearing over a book that could be heard and a warning that could be refused.

**"A Gathering Easy for Us": The Sūrah's Own Verdict on Its Difficulty**

*Dhālika ḥashrun ʿalayna yasīr* — "that is a gathering which is easy for Us," and the sentence is the answer to every objection the chapter has quoted. Verse 3 said "that is a return far off"; verse 15 asked whether creation was a fatigue; verse 44 concludes that the assembly of all the dead, in one instant, on one plain, is *yasīr* — light, easy, without resistance.

The Qur'an uses the same word for the same claim in the same context: "say: is there of your partners any who originates creation and then repeats it? Say: God originates creation and then repeats it" (10:34); "and He it is who originates creation, then He repeats it, and that is easiest for Him" (30:27); "do they not see that God, who created the heavens and the earth and did not tire in creating them, is able to give life to the dead?" (46:33); "and they swear their most solemn oaths by God: God will not raise up anyone who dies — nay, it is a promise binding upon Him, but most people do not know" (16:38); and "they say: when we are lost in the earth, are we then in a new creation? Nay, they disbelieve in the meeting with their Lord. Say: the angel of death, who is put in charge of you, will take you, then to your Lord you will be returned" (32:10-11). The word *ʿalaynā* — "upon Us," "incumbent upon Us" — is stronger than the English "for Us" suggests, and the commentators press it: the gathering is not only easy for God, it is a duty He has taken upon Himself, the same idiom as "your Lord has decreed mercy upon Himself" (6:12), and the claim of 16:9 that the direct way is "upon God.". The verse's claim is thus double: no difficulty, and no option. What is easy for God is certain.

**Ease and Haste, Side by Side**

There is a contrast inside the verse that its translators sometimes flatten: the people come out *siʿān* — in headlong haste — while the gathering is *yasīr* — effortless. The two adverbs belong to different parties, and the scene is built on the mismatch. For the resurrected, the moment is panic, disorientation, the ground giving way, the direction of movement decided by someone else; for God, it is the least of what He does. The Qur'an's other description of the same asymmetry is the scene in al-Kahf, where the presenting is theirs and the ease is His: "and they will be set before your Lord in rows — you have come to Us as We created you the first time; nay, you claimed that We would never appoint a time for you" (18:48).

This is also the sūrah's last word about the word "day." Since verse 20 the chapter has named the Day — of Threat, of Coming Forth, of the Gathering — and here the naming is followed by an adjective about its cost, and the cost is zero. The hearers had been treating resurrection as the hardest thing in the world to believe; the chapter's final eschatological sentence tells them it is the easiest thing in the world to do.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

It is worth noting what the sūrah declines to add at this point, since its reticence is part of its teaching. There is no description of the balance, no intercession, no pleading, no enumeration of the ranks arriving. The chapter ends its account of the event with a geological image and a verdict of ease. Everything after verse 44 is addressed to the living.

That proportion is deliberate. The Qur'an's scenes of judgement, in other sūrahs, supply all the details withheld here; this sūrah's purpose was never to give a full map of the next world but to make the next world *credible* for a particular city that found it absurd. Once credibility is established — the earth splits, they stream out, it is easy — the map is unnecessary, and the chapter moves immediately to the only thing still to be settled: what the messenger is for.

**Easy, Made Easy, and the Question That Follows**

The verse's last two words — *yasīr* — put it in the company of the Qur'an's other great claim about divine ease. "And We made the Qur'an easy to remember: is there anyone who will remember?" (*wa-la-qad yassarnā al-Qur'āna li-dh-dhikri fa-hal min muddakir*) is the refrain with which Sūrah al-Qamar closes every one of its four warnings (54:17, 22, 32, 40), and that sūrah, as we saw in the introduction, is the chapter the Prophet ﷺ paired with this one when he recited them together at the two feasts. The pairing is more than a liturgical detail: both sūrahs are built on questions beginning with *hal min* — "is there any refuge?" (50:36), "is there more?" (50:30), "is there anyone who will remember?" (54:40) — and both end by insisting that what God does is easy.

The correspondence gives the sūrah its final word about the resurrection's difficulty. What the Makkans called "far" is called here *yasīr*; what they thought impossible God has, in the paired sūrah, already made *easy to remember*, and put within the reach of anyone who will say the words. The gathering of the dead and the learning of a chapter are, in this Book's strange arithmetic, the same category of act — both are things God has made easy, and both are waiting only on whether a person chooses to attend.

---
## Sūrah Qāf 50:45

> **We know best what they say, and you are not upon them a tyrant. So remind, with the Qur'an, whoever fears My Threat.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Clauses and a Change of Job**

*Naḥnu aʿlamu bimā yaqūlūna wa-mā anta ʿalayhim bi-jabbār fa-dhakkir bi-l-qurʾāni man yakhāfu waʿīd* — and the last verse of the chapter reassigns the prophet's work in three moves. First, an assertion about knowledge, in the first person plural that has closed verses 15, 38 and 43: "We know best what they say." Second, a denial of authority over them: "you are not a *jabbār* over them." Third, a command with an instrument and an object: "remind, with the Qur'an, whoever fears My Threat."

The order is not rhetorical decoration. The verse first takes back from the messenger the burden of being informed about his opponents' hearts — a burden that had been crushing him ("perhaps you will waste yourself away over them, if they will not believe," 26:3; "do not consume yourself in sorrow over them," 35:8) — then it removes the power he does not have, and only then does it give him the task that remains.

**"We Know Best What They Say": The End of Judging**

The clause is a comfort and a limitation at once, and the source's note brings out both. It is a comfort because nothing the opponents say will cost the prophet anything he has to carry: the report has already been filed, and it is not the messenger's business to keep a copy. The source's note points, for this opening clause, to Ṭā Hā's parallel "We know best what they say" (20:104), set in the same scene of resurrection; and in our sūrah the same first-person knowledge has already closed verse 4 and verse 16, so the clause here is not a threat but a relief. It is a limitation because the same knowledge excludes the messenger's verdicts: God knows what is said, and He also knows what will yet be said by the same mouths. The note makes precisely this point, and gives the chapter's own example: some who appear outwardly to reject the truth carry faith in their hearts already, and the sūrah's audience would have understood, since several of the Prophet's ﷺ most vocal opponents became among his closest companions — most famously ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who heard this very sūrah recited and went out to kill the reciter and came back a Muslim.

Whether one presses the details of that account or not, its logic is the verse's: a chapter that ends by saying "We know best what they say" forbids despair about any particular hearer. The knowledge that condemns is the same knowledge that could, at any moment, change a person — and it is not the messenger's to administer.

**"You Are Not a Tyrant Over Them": The Word *Jabbār***

The denial is the strongest word Arabic has for coercion. *Jabbār* is from the root meaning to force, to compel, and also — in the ordinary idiom of the language — to set a bone by force, hence "the one who puts things right by compelling them." Applied to God the word has a positive sense, "the Compeller"; applied to a human being it is a reproach, and it is used that way in the Qur'an, most memorably by the scouts who were told to enter the Holy City: "O Moses, there is within it a people of violence (*jabbārūn*), and we will not enter it until they leave" (5:22). So the verse says: you are not to be that, over them — not with the sword, not with social force, not by any of the means by which a community is bullied into belief.

The Qur'an states the same limit in a dozen forms, and it is not a modern insertion but the Book's own description of the prophet's office: "you are not a controller over them" (88:22); "would you compel people to become believers?" (10:99); "there is no coercion in religion" (2:256); "and if your Lord had willed, all on earth would have believed — would you compel the people until they are believers?" (10:99); "and say: the truth is from your Lord, so whoever wills, let him believe, and whoever wills, let him disbelieve" (18:29); "upon you is only the delivery, and upon Us is the reckoning" (13:40; cf. 88:25-26). At the other end of the verse's moral arc stands 16:125, which supplies the method: "call to the way of your Lord with wisdom and beautiful exhortation, and argue with them in the way that is best."

This is the last thing the sūrah says about the relation between revelation and society, and it is worth noting how much the chapter had already said about the society's fate. The two do not conflict: the punishment belongs to God, the reminder to the messenger, and the coercion belongs to no one. The Makkan audience had political power over the small community of believers; the verse tells the believers that even if the situation reversed, the method would not.

**"So Remind With the Qur'an": The Chapter Closes on Its Own Opening**

*Fa-dhakkir bi-l-qurʾān* — and the verb and the noun are both the sūrah's own. It called itself, at verse 1, a "glorious Qur'an," and at verse 8 it said the whole apparatus of creation was "a source of insight and a reminder (*dhikrā*) for every penitent servant," and at verse 37 it defined the one kind of hearer for whom "in that is a *dhikr*." Now the chapter's final imperative is to do what the chapter has been doing — remind — with the instrument with which it has been reminding. The command, in other words, is to continue.

Two consequences follow for the reader. The first is that the sūrah, which began with an oath over a Book, ends with an assignment to a Book: nothing in the chapter's forty-four preceding verses was designed to stand in place of the recitation but to prepare a person to receive it. The second is the specification of the means: "with the Qur'an," not with argument, not with wonder-works, not with force. That is the same refusal the chapter made at its beginning, when it called the demand for a visible miracle a marvel (v. 2) and answered it with a sky and an olive tree.

**"Whoever Fears My Threat": The Audience of the Reminder**

The object clause is the sūrah's final definition of the person it addresses, and the source's note records the range: some read it as the believers, while others take it more broadly, as all but the most obstinate disbelievers, because every human being carries the primordial covenant — "am I not your Lord? They said: yes, we testify" (7:172) — and the natural disposition (30:30) that God placed in them.

The grammar decides in favour of the breadth: *man yakhāfu waʿīdī* is a relative clause describing a quality, not a group, so that whoever has fear in him, at whatever level, is inside the address — including the hearer who fears only that he ought to fear. That is why the verse's use of "My Threat" instead of "My punishment" is so apt: a threat is what turns a person before the event, and the sūrah has spent its whole length arguing that the threatening event is easy, near, and coming. The note's parallel is the sūrah of the Highest, whose own summary of reminding matches ours exactly: "so remind — the reminder benefits whoever fears, and the most wretched will avoid it" (87:9-10).

Fear, in this chapter, is not the opposite of hope but its condition: the man who fears the Threat is the man who takes the cover off (v. 22), who hastens to the prayer at the edges of the day (vv. 39-40), and who, having read the record, does not wait for his companion to read it aloud (v. 23). The sūrah ends where verse 8 said it began: with a reminder, and with the person who is able to hear it.

**The Last Word of Sūrah Qāf**

The chapter closes on the possessive "My Threat" (*waʿīdī*), a first-person singular where the sūrah's closing clauses have been using "We." The movement is the whole of its theology in miniature: the "We" of creation, of sending down water, of the six days and the effortless gathering, narrows at the last moment to a "My" — the personal address of a God who has been speaking to a named city about a named Book. Its hearers are left with the memory of a threat they now believe in, and a command that forbids them from enforcing it, and a permission to hope about anyone they have given up on, since the knowledge of what people say has never been theirs.

---

## Conclusion: The Shape of the Whole

Sūrah Qāf is built in concentric movements, and having walked through it verse by verse it is possible to name the design in a few lines.

- **Verses 1-5** state the case and the objection: a glorious Book, a people who marvel that a warner came from among them, and the specific scandal of a return they call "far off."
- **Verses 6-11** answer with the visible world: sky, earth, water, palms, grain, the dead land revived, and the sentence "even so is the coming forth" — the resurrection placed inside ordinary agriculture.
- **Verses 12-14** add the historical argument: six destroyed peoples, denied *the messengers* rather than one of them, and the sentence "so My Threat came due."
- **Verses 15-22** turn inward: creation was no fatigue; the self is known to the last whisper; the angels record; death comes "with truth"; the cover is lifted and the sight becomes piercing.
- **Verses 23-30** are the court: the file produced, the casting in, the list of three social crimes capped by the setting-up of another god, the companion's disclaimer, the cut-off plea, the unchanging Word and the un-wronging Lord, and Hell's question about whether it is full.
- **Verses 31-37** describe the other destination: the Garden brought near, the two qualities of its people, the fear of the All-Merciful unseen, entry in peace, what the souls wish — and "with Us there is more" — then the reminder's criterion: "whoever has a heart."
- **Verses 38-45** close the chapter: creation in six days without weariness; patience and praise at the edges of the day and in the night; the Caller from a near place; the Day of Coming Forth; the return to "Us"; the easy gathering; and the commission to remind, with the Qur'an, whoever fears the Threat — with a prohibition on coercion, and no other job given.

Its dominant word is nearness, and the reason is that its argument is never about whether God exists but about whether He is reachable: nearer than the jugular, closer than the vein, not distant, from a near place, and with a Garden moved toward the ones who feared Him while unseen. Its dominant vice, correspondingly, is not doubt but inattention — *ghafla*, the cover, the hardened "stiff neck," the confounded affair, the ear that hears and is not present. And its dominant mercy is the last verse of all: the prophet is not made a taskmaster over anyone, the burden of other people's hearts is lifted from him, and the entire apparatus of sky and judgement and Fire is placed in the service of a sentence that may be refused.

The chapter that opens by swearing that the Book is not a thing to be marvelled at ends by telling its bearer that he is not there to compel, only to remind. Between the oath and the commission, every verse has done the same work the sūrah names in verse 8: it is a reminder, for whoever has a heart.

May God make us among those who incline the ear and are present, and let the last thing we hear from this sūrah be the thing it asks its hearers to become: a penitent servant, a guardian, a heart that came back.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah Qāf]**
